Arithmetic Statistics — the Cohen-Lenstra Heuristics, Average Ranks & Random Matrix Models

Arithmetic statistics is the probabilistic study of arithmetic objects: class groups of number fields, Selmer groups of elliptic curves, and Tate-Shafarevich groups. The central philosophy, due to Cohen and Lenstra (1984), is that the p-part of the class group of an imaginary quadratic field behaves like a random finite abelian p-group weighted by the reciprocal of the size of its automorphism group. The Davenport-Heilbronn theorem (1971) gave the first rigorous average result: the average size of the 3-torsion in class groups of quadratic fields is 2 (imaginary) and 1 (real). This was vastly generalized by Bhargava and Shankar (2010–2015) who developed a method to compute average sizes of n-Selmer groups for elliptic curves over ℚ, using geometry of numbers to parametrize Selmer elements by binary cubic, quartic, and quintic forms. Their result: the average rank of elliptic curves is bounded. Parallel to this, the random matrix model for L-functions — originating in Montgomery's pair correlation conjecture (1972) for ζ(s) and systematized by Katz and Sarnak (1999) — predicts that the zero statistics of L-functions mirror the eigenvalue statistics of random matrices from classical compact groups. The Bhargava cube laws parametrize number fields of small degree by integral orbits on prehomogeneous vector spaces, enabling the counting of number fields by discriminant.

"The statistical patterns in number theory often follow those of random matrix theory."
— Peter Sarnak
Part 1

📏 The Cohen-Lenstra Heuristics

Class Groups
Distribution of the p-Part of Class Groups of Imaginary Quadratic Fields
For an imaginary quadratic field K = ℚ(√−D) with fundamental discriminant D > 0, the class group Cl(K) is a finite abelian group whose order is the class number h(D). The Cohen-Lenstra heuristics (1984) predict that for an odd prime ℓ, the ℓ-part Cl(K)_ℓ is distributed like a random finite abelian ℓ-group with probability proportional to 1/|Aut(G)|. More precisely, for a fixed finite abelian ℓ-group G, as D → ∞ over fundamental discriminants, we expect Prob(Cl(K)_ℓ ≅ G) = c · |G|^(−w)/|Aut(G)| for a certain normalization constant c and weight w. The weight w = 1 for imaginary quadratic fields (the "Cohen-Lenstra" weight) and the distribution is concentrated on groups with small ℓ-rank. The Davenport-Heilbronn theorem (1971) gave the first concrete evidence: the average size of the 3-torsion subgroup Cl(K)[3] for imaginary quadratic fields is exactly 2, and for real quadratic fields it is 1. This is proved by counting cubic fields via binary cubic forms — a precursor to the Bhargava method. The Bhargava-Varma result (2015) computes the mean 3-rank of class groups (the average of dim_{𝔽_3} Cl(K)[3]) for quadratic fields, confirming the Cohen-Lenstra predictions. The moments of class group sizes — the average of |Cl(K)|^k — satisfy subtle growth rates; the Cohen-Lenstra heuristics predict that all moments are finite and governed by the same distribution.
The Cohen-Lenstra heuristics are a paradigm-shifting probabilistic model: the p-part of the class group of a random imaginary quadratic field behaves like a random finite abelian p-group with a natural weight 1/|Aut(G)|.
Part 2

🌍 The Bhargava-Shankar Method and Average Ranks

Selmer Groups
Average Size of n-Selmer Groups and the Bounded Average Rank of Elliptic Curves
For an elliptic curve E/ℚ, the n-Selmer group Sel_n(E) fits into the exact sequence 0 → E(ℚ)/nE(ℚ) → Sel_n(E) → Ш(E)[n] → 0, where Ш(E) is the Tate-Shafarevich group. Bhargava and Shankar (2010–2015) computed the average size of Sel_n(E) for n = 2, 3, 4, 5 by parametrizing the elements of Sel_n(E) by integral orbits on prehomogeneous vector spaces: binary cubic forms (n = 2), binary quartic forms (n = 3), pairs of ternary quadratic forms (n = 4), and 5 × 5 skew-symmetric matrices (n = 5). Using geometry of numbers, they counted these orbits and found: the average size of Sel_2(E) = 3, Sel_3(E) = 4, Sel_4(E) = 7, and Sel_5(E) = 6. The key consequence: the average rank of elliptic curves over ℚ is bounded. More precisely, the limsup of the average rank is at most 0.885... (from the 5-Selmer bound). Moreover, the average rank of curves with a marked rational point is at least some δ > 0. The parity phenomenon: the rank of E(ℚ) has the same parity as the root number w(E) = ±1 (the sign of the functional equation of the L-function); curves with w(E) = +1 have even rank, w(E) = −1 have odd rank. The distribution of the 2-Selmer group was shown to match the cokernel of a random symmetric matrix over 𝔽_2 (the Poonen-Rains heuristic). The Bhargava-Shankar-Tsimerman (2015) work on the average size of the 6-Selmer group and beyond remains active.
Bhargava and Shankar's orbit-counting method dramatically advanced our understanding: the average rank of elliptic curves is bounded, and the Selmer groups exhibit distributional patterns consistent with random matrix models.
Part 3

🧮 Random Matrix Models for L-functions

L-functions
Montgomery Pair Correlation, Katz-Sarnak Philosophy, and Keating-Snaith Conjectures
Hugh Montgomery (1972) studied the pair correlation of nontrivial zeros of the Riemann zeta function ζ(s). Assuming the Riemann hypothesis, he showed that for 0 < α < β, the proportion of zeros with spacing in [α, β] is ∫_α^β (1 − (sin πu/πu)^2) du — the same as the distribution of eigenvalue spacings of the Gaussian Unitary Ensemble (GUE) of random Hermitian matrices. This Montgomery-Dyson pair correlation conjecture was the first link between number theory and random matrix theory. The Katz-Sarnak philosophy (1999) vastly generalizes this: for families of L-functions (e.g., Dirichlet L-functions, L-functions of elliptic curves, modular forms), the statistics of zeros near the critical line match the eigenvalue statistics of random matrices from classical compact groups. The symmetry type of the family (unitary U(N), symplectic USp(2N), orthogonal O(N), or even/odd orthogonal SO(2N)/SO(2N+1)) is determined by the sign of the functional equation and the structure of the family. For example, the family of quadratic Dirichlet L-functions has symplectic symmetry, while the family of elliptic curve L-functions has orthogonal symmetry. The Keating-Snaith conjectures (2000): the moments of L-functions at the critical point s = 1/2 can be computed using the characteristic polynomial of random matrices from the appropriate symmetry group. For ζ(s), Keating and Snaith predicted ∫_0^T |ζ(1/2 + it)|^{2k} dt ∼ a_k · G(k+1)^2/G(2k+1) · T (log T)^{k^2}, where G is the Barnes G-function and a_k is an arithmetic factor. These predictions have been verified numerically and motivate the search for the true distribution of L-values.
The Katz-Sarnak philosophy unifies the statistics of L-functions across all number theory: zeros and values of L-functions behave like eigenvalues of random matrices from a symmetry group determined by the functional equation.
Part 4

🔥 Parametrization of Number Fields

Number Fields
Bhargava's Cube Laws, Counting Number Fields, and Malle's Conjecture
Bhargava's revolutionary insight (2004) is that number fields of small degree can be parametrized by integral orbits on prehomogeneous vector spaces. The cube law: a 2 × 2 × 2 cube of integers parametrizes a pair of binary quadratic forms; the resulting ring is a quartic ring (a commutative ring of rank 4 over ℤ). This leads to the parametrization of quartic fields by pairs of ternary quadratic forms (for quintic fields) and more complex integral orbits for sextic fields. The key counting result: the number of number fields of degree d with discriminant < X satisfies a power law. Specifically, Bhargava proved that the number of quartic fields with discriminant < X is ∼ c_4 · X, and the number of quintic fields is ∼ c_5 · X, disproving previous heuristics. The Malle's conjecture (2004) predicts that for a fixed Galois group G ⊂ S_n, the number of extensions L/K of degree n with discriminant ≤ X grows like X^{a_n} (log X)^{b_n(G)}, where a_n = 1/a(G) for a certain constant a(G) depending on the exponent of G, and b_n(G) depends on the group structure. The work of Bhargava-Shankar-Venkatesh (2013) and Ellenberg-Venkatesh (2008) has made significant progress on counting number fields, connecting the problem to the geometry of numbers on prehomogeneous vector spaces. The Thue-Siegel principle adapted to this context gives a method for counting integral points on homogeneous spaces by combining upper bounds (from Siegel's mass formula and the geometry of numbers) with lower bounds (from the construction of many integral orbits). The applications extend to class groups (the Cohen-Lenstra heuristics), Selmer groups (via the Bhargava-Shankar parametrization), and the average size of the Tate-Shafarevich group of elliptic curves.
Bhargava's cube laws and the orbit-counting method have transformed arithmetic statistics, enabling precise counting of number fields and linking the distribution of Selmer groups to random matrix heuristics.

🎯 Practice

← Back to Math Map

The Cohen-Lenstra-Martinet Heuristics for Real Quadratic Fields

The original Cohen-Lenstra heuristics (1984) address the p-part of the class group of imaginary quadratic fields, where the unit group is finite (only roots of unity). For real quadratic fields ℚ(√d) with d > 0, the unit group has rank 1 by Dirichlet's unit theorem, generated by a fundamental unit ε_d > 1. This infinite unit group introduces a regulator R_d = log ε_d into the class number formula h_d R_d = √d L(1, χ_d) / 2 (for d ≡ 1 mod 4) or h_d R_d = √d L(1, χ_d) (for d ≡ 2,3 mod 4). The presence of the regulator means the naive Cohen-Lenstra weighting by 1/|Aut(G)| must be modified. Cohen and Martinet (1990) proposed that for a fixed odd prime p, the probability that the p-part of the class group Cl(ℚ(√d))[p^∞] is isomorphic to a finite abelian p-group G is proportional to 1/(|G| · |Aut(G)|). The extra factor 1/|G| accounts for the regulator: heuristically, the regulator R_d behaves like a random positive real number, and the class number formula suggests h_d ≈ √d L(1, χ_d) / R_d. Since L(1, χ_d) has a limiting distribution (related to the value distribution of L-functions), and R_d is "independent" of the class group structure, the probability that p^k divides h_d gains a factor 1/p^k, leading to the 1/|G| weighting. For p = 2, the situation is more subtle because the 2-rank of the class group is governed by genus theory: the 2-rank equals t − 1 where t is the number of distinct prime factors of the discriminant. The Cohen-Lenstra-Martinet heuristics for the odd part of the class group of real quadratic fields remain conjectural, but extensive numerical data (e.g., computations by Jacobson, Ramachandran, and Williams up to d < 108) show remarkable agreement. The heuristics also predict that the average size of the p-torsion subgroup Cl(ℚ(√d))[p] is 1 + 1/p for imaginary quadratic fields and 2 for real quadratic fields (the extra 1 coming from the 1/|G| factor summing to 2). This distinction between imaginary and real quadratic fields is a hallmark of the Cohen-Lenstra philosophy: the distribution of class groups is not universal but depends on the unit rank of the field.

The Poonen-Rains Model for Selmer Groups and the Cokernel of Random Alternating Matrices

The Bhargava-Shankar work on the average size of the 2-Selmer group of elliptic curves over ℚ showed that the 2-Selmer group Sel2(E/ℚ) is distributed like the cokernel of a random symmetric matrix over 𝔽2. Poonen and Rains (2012) generalized this to a comprehensive heuristic for the distribution of the p-Selmer group Sel_p(E/ℚ) for any prime p, and more generally for the Selmer group of any abelian variety. Their model replaces the symmetric matrix with a random alternating matrix over ℤ_p (or 𝔽_p for the p-torsion). The key insight is that the p-Selmer group fits into an exact sequence 0 → E(ℚ)/pE(ℚ) → Sel_p(E/ℚ) → Ш(E/ℚ)[p] → 0, and the Cassels-Tate pairing on Ш(E/ℚ)[p] is alternating and non-degenerate. Poonen and Rains model Sel_p(E/ℚ) as the cokernel of a random alternating matrix M over ℤ_p, conditioned on the dimension of the maximal isotropic subspace (which corresponds to E(ℚ)/pE(ℚ)). For p = 2, the alternating condition is equivalent to symmetric with zero diagonal, matching the Bhargava-Shankar result. For odd p, the model predicts that the probability Sel_p(E/ℚ) ≅ G (as a finite abelian p-group with an alternating pairing) is proportional to 1/(|G| · |Aut(G, ⟨·,·⟩)|), where Aut(G, ⟨·,·⟩) is the group of automorphisms preserving the alternating pairing. This weighting by the automorphism group of the pairing is the natural generalization of the Cohen-Lenstra 1/|Aut(G)|. The Poonen-Rains heuristic makes precise predictions for the average size of Sel_p(E/ℚ): it is p + 1 for all p. For p = 2, this gives average size 3, matching Bhargava-Shankar. For p = 3, the average size is predicted to be 4, and Bhargava-Shankar-Tsimerman (2015) proved the average size of the 3-Selmer group is 4. For p = 5, the average size is predicted to be 6, and recent work by Bhargava, Shankar, and others has made progress toward this. The model also predicts the distribution of the rank: the probability that rank E(ℚ) = r is the probability that a random alternating matrix over ℤ_p has cokernel with a maximal isotropic subspace of dimension r. This yields the prediction that 50% of elliptic curves have rank 0, 50% have rank 1, and 0% have rank ≥ 2 (in the sense of natural density). The parity conjecture (the rank has the same parity as the root number) is built into the model via the sign of the functional equation, which determines the symmetry type of the random matrix ensemble.

Worked Example: Average Size of p-Selmer Group
Using the Poonen-Rains heuristic, compute the predicted average size of the 5-Selmer group of an elliptic curve over ℚ. Explain why this average size differs from the average size of the 5-torsion in the class group of an imaginary quadratic field.
The Poonen-Rains heuristic models the p-Selmer group as the cokernel of a random alternating matrix over ℤ_p. For any prime p, the expected size of the cokernel of a random n × n alternating matrix over 𝔽_p, as n → ∞, is p + 1. This can be derived by computing the probability that the cokernel is a given finite abelian p-group G with an alternating pairing, weighted by 1/(|G| · |Aut(G, ⟨·,·⟩)|), and summing |G| over all such G. The sum evaluates to p + 1. For p = 5, the predicted average size of the 5-Selmer group is 5 + 1 = 6. In contrast, the Cohen-Lenstra heuristic for the p-torsion in the class group of an imaginary quadratic field weights by 1/|Aut(G)| (no alternating pairing, no extra 1/|G| factor). The expected size of the p-torsion under Cohen-Lenstra is 1 + 1/p. For p = 5, this is 1 + 1/5 = 6/5 = 1.2. The difference arises because the Selmer group carries an alternating pairing (the Cassels-Tate pairing) and sits in an exact sequence with E(ℚ)/pE(ℚ) and Ш(E/ℚ)[p], while the class group of an imaginary quadratic field has no such pairing and no regulator correction. The extra 1/|G| factor in the Poonen-Rains model (coming from the "random maximal isotropic subspace" conditioning) increases the average size from 1 + 1/p to p + 1.
Exam Strategy: Distinguishing Heuristic Models
When a question asks for the "average size" of a p-torsion or p-Selmer group, identify which heuristic applies: Cohen-Lenstra (imaginary quadratic class groups) → average size = 1 + 1/p. Cohen-Lenstra-Martinet (real quadratic class groups, odd p) → average size = 2. Poonen-Rains (p-Selmer of elliptic curves) → average size = p + 1. Bhargava-Shankar (2-Selmer) → average size = 3 (special case of Poonen-Rains). Memorize these four numbers and their domains; examiners often test whether you confuse the class group average with the Selmer group average.

Bhargava's Higher Composition Laws: Quintic Rings and the 5×5×5 Cube

Bhargava's 2004 PhD thesis introduced a series of "higher composition laws" generalizing Gauss's composition of binary quadratic forms (which parametrizes quadratic rings). The first new law uses a 2 × 2 × 2 cube of integers to parametrize pairs of ternary quadratic forms, which in turn correspond to quartic rings (commutative rings of rank 4 over ℤ). The second law uses a 2 × 3 × 3 box to parametrize pairs of a binary quadratic form and a ternary quadratic form, corresponding to quintic rings. The third law uses a 3 × 3 × 3 cube to parametrize triples of ternary quadratic forms, corresponding to sextic rings. The most striking is the parametrization of quintic rings: a 2 × 3 × 3 box of integers (18 integers total) encodes a pair (f, g) where f is a binary quadratic form and g is a ternary quadratic form. The invariants of this pair (the discriminant of the quintic ring, the resolvent sextic, etc.) are polynomial functions of the 18 integers. The group action is GL2(ℤ) × SL3(ℤ) × SL3(ℤ) acting on the box by slicing transformations. The integral orbits of this action are in bijection with isomorphism classes of quintic rings. This parametrization allowed Bhargava to count quintic fields by discriminant: the number of S5-quintic fields with absolute discriminant ≤ X is asymptotic to c X for an explicit constant c > 0 (proved by Bhargava in 2010). The constant c involves a product of local densities and the volume of a fundamental domain for the group action. For the connection to Selmer groups: the 2 × 3 × 3 box also parametrizes the 2-Selmer group of a certain genus-2 curve (or the 4-Selmer group of an elliptic curve). More directly, the 2 × 2 × 2 cube parametrizes the 2-Selmer group of an elliptic curve (as used by Bhargava-Shankar), and the 2 × 3 × 3 box parametrizes the 3-Selmer group. The 3 × 3 × 3 cube parametrizes the 5-Selmer group. This pattern — degree d+1 composition law parametrizes the d-Selmer group — is part of a broader philosophy: the geometry of numbers on prehomogeneous vector spaces controls both the counting of number fields and the distribution of Selmer groups. The invariants of the box (discriminant, resolvent polynomials) correspond to the invariants of the Selmer element (the associated homogeneous space). The group action corresponds to the equivalence of Selmer elements under the action of the rational points of the Jacobian.

Ellenberg-Venkatesh-Westerland: Homological Stability and Function Field Analogues

The Cohen-Lenstra heuristics were originally formulated for number fields. In the 2010s, a geometric proof of the function field analogue was achieved by Ellenberg, Venkatesh, and Westerland (2016), using homological stability of Hurwitz spaces. Over a finite field 𝔽_q, the class group of a quadratic extension 𝔽_q(t)(√D) (where D is a squarefree polynomial) is a finite abelian group. The Cohen-Lenstra heuristics predict that as q → ∞ (with the degree of D fixed), the distribution of the p-part of the class group (for p ≠ char 𝔽_q) converges to the Cohen-Lenstra distribution. Ellenberg-Venkatesh-Westerland proved this by interpreting the class group as the homology of a certain Hurwitz space (the moduli space of branched covers of the projective line). The key tool is homological stability: as the genus of the cover grows, the homology groups of the Hurwitz space stabilize, and the limiting distribution matches the Cohen-Lenstra prediction. The proof uses the Grothendieck-Lefschetz trace formula to relate the number of points on the Hurwitz space over 𝔽_q to the action of Frobenius on its étale cohomology. The stable homology is identified with the homology of the infinite symmetric group, and the Cohen-Lenstra measure emerges as the limiting distribution of the cokernel of a random matrix over ℤ_p (the "random matrix" here is the action of Frobenius on the first homology of the cover). This geometric approach also yields the function field analogue of the Poonen-Rains heuristic for Selmer groups of elliptic curves over 𝔽_q(t), proved by Ellenberg-Venkatesh-Westerland and independently by Erman-Wood. The function field setting is more tractable because the moduli spaces are algebraic varieties over finite fields, and the Weil conjectures give precise control over point counts. The number field case remains conjectural, but the function field proofs provide a blueprint: the Cohen-Lenstra measure is the limiting distribution of the cokernel of a random matrix arising from the monodromy action on homology. This connects arithmetic statistics to the topology of moduli spaces and the representation theory of symmetric groups.

Malle's Conjecture, Discriminant Counting, and the Inverse Galois Problem

Malle's conjecture (2004) refines the classical problem of counting number fields by discriminant. For a fixed transitive subgroup G ⊂ S_n, let N(G, K, X) be the number of extensions L/K of degree n with Galois group G and absolute discriminant NK/ℚ(disc(L/K)) ≤ X. Malle conjectured that N(G, K, X) ∼ c(G, K) Xa(G) (log X)b(G, K) - 1 as X → ∞, where a(G) = 1 / ind(G), ind(G) = ming∈G, g≠1 (n - number of cycles of g) is the minimal index of a non-identity element of G, and b(G, K) is the number of conjugacy classes in G of elements achieving this minimal index, divided by the order of the centralizer (with adjustments for the base field K). For G = S_n, ind(S_n) = 2 (achieved by transpositions), so a(S_n) = 1/2. The number of S_n-extensions of ℚ with discriminant ≤ X is predicted to grow like X1/2 (log X)b_n - 1. For n = 3, b3 = 1, giving X1/2 (no log factor), matching Davenport-Heilbronn. For n = 4, b4 = 1/2? Actually for S4, the minimal index is 2 (transpositions), there are 6 transpositions, centralizer size 4, so b = 6/4 = 3/2? The precise formula for b(G, K) involves the number of conjugacy classes of minimal index elements that are "realizable" over K. Malle's conjecture has been proved for abelian G (by Wright, 1989), for G = S3 (Davenport-Heilbronn), S4 (Bhargava, 2005), S5 (Bhargava, 2010), and for certain other groups using the geometry of numbers on prehomogeneous vector spaces. The conjecture connects to the inverse Galois problem: if Malle's conjecture holds with a positive constant c(G, ℚ), then there are infinitely many extensions with Galois group G, so G occurs as a Galois group over ℚ. The constant c(G, K) is a product of local factors: c(G, K) = τ(G) ∏_v c_v(G, K_v), where τ(G) is the Tamagawa number of a certain algebraic group associated to G, and c_v are local densities. The work of Bhargava-Shankar-Venkatesh (2013) and Ellenberg-Venkatesh (2008) developed the "Thue-Siegel principle" for counting integral points on homogeneous spaces, giving upper bounds of the form Xa(G)+ε and lower bounds of the form Xa(G) (log X)b(G)-1 for many groups G. The exponent a(G) = 1/ind(G) is sharp: Klüners (2003) gave a counterexample to the original form of Malle's conjecture for G = C3 ≀ C2 (wreath product) over ℚ, where the exponent is larger than 1/ind(G) due to "bad" ramification. The corrected conjecture (Malle's conjecture with "Malle's correction factor") is an active area of research.

  • Confusing the Cohen-Lenstra average (1 + 1/p for imaginary quadratic p-torsion) with the Cohen-Lenstra-Martinet average (2 for real quadratic odd p-torsion) or the Poonen-Rains average (p + 1 for p-Selmer).
  • Forgetting that the Cohen-Lenstra heuristics apply only to the odd part of the class group; the 2-part is governed by genus theory and has a completely different distribution (2-rank = t − 1).
  • Assuming the Poonen-Rains model predicts average rank 1/2 for all primes; it predicts 50% rank 0, 50% rank 1, 0% rank ≥ 2, but this is conditional on the parity conjecture and the random matrix model.
  • Mixing up the parametrization: 2×2×2 cube → quartic rings / 2-Selmer; 2×3×3 box → quintic rings / 3-Selmer; 3×3×3 cube → sextic rings / 5-Selmer. The degree of the ring is one more than the dimension of the smallest slice.
  • Stating Malle's conjecture as X1/2 for all S_n; the exponent is always 1/2 for S_n (since ind(S_n)=2), but the log exponent b(G) varies with n and the base field.
  • Believing the function field proofs (Ellenberg-Venkatesh-Westerland) directly imply the number field conjectures; they provide a geometric framework but the number field case lacks the algebraic geometry of moduli spaces over finite fields.
  • Overlooking the role of the regulator in the Cohen-Lenstra-Martinet heuristic: the extra 1/|G| factor comes from the regulator R_d = log ε_d in the class number formula, which has no analogue for imaginary quadratic fields.

The Friedman–Washington Heuristics for Function Fields and the Cohen–Lenstra Measure

In 1989, Friedman and Washington proved the first rigorous theorem supporting the Cohen–Lenstra heuristics by working in the function field setting. Let Fq be a finite field with q elements, and consider quadratic extensions of the rational function field Fq(t). For a fixed prime p not dividing q, the p-part of the class group of such an extension is a finite abelian p-group. Friedman and Washington showed that as q → ∞ (through prime powers), the distribution of this p-part converges to the Cohen–Lenstra measure. The Cohen–Lenstra measure assigns to each finite abelian p-group G a probability proportional to 1/|Aut(G)|. Their proof uses the fact that the class group of a quadratic function field can be identified with the cokernel of the Cartier–Manin matrix of the associated hyperelliptic curve. As q grows, the entries of this matrix become equidistributed, and the cokernel distribution tends to the Cohen–Lenstra measure.

This result was a major breakthrough because it provided a setting where the heuristics become theorems. The function field case is more tractable: the moduli spaces of hyperelliptic curves are algebraic varieties over finite fields, and the Weil conjectures give precise control over point counts. In 2016, Ellenberg, Venkatesh, and Westerland strengthened this by proving the function field analogue for a fixed finite field Fq as the genus g → ∞. They used homological stability for Hurwitz spaces to show that the distribution of the p-part of the class group of a random quadratic extension of Fq(t) of genus g converges to the Cohen–Lenstra measure for any fixed q. This connects arithmetic statistics to the topology of moduli spaces and the representation theory of symmetric groups. The number field case remains conjectural, but the function field proofs provide a blueprint: the Cohen–Lenstra measure is the limiting distribution of the cokernel of a random matrix arising from the monodromy action on homology.

The Moment Method for Selmer Groups: Bhargava–Shankar and the Geometry of Numbers

Bhargava and Shankar (2013) introduced a geometry-of-numbers approach to compute the average size of the 2-Selmer group of elliptic curves over ℚ. They parametrized 2-Selmer elements by integral orbits of the group GL2 × GL2 acting on the space of 2×2×2 cubes (equivalently, pairs of ternary quadratic forms). The height of an elliptic curve corresponds to the discriminant of the associated binary quartic form. Using the Davenport–Heilbronn method, they counted the number of integral orbits of bounded height, showing that the average size of the 2-Selmer group is exactly 3. This implies that the average 2-Selmer rank is log2(3) ≈ 1.585. Since the rank of an elliptic curve is at most its 2-Selmer rank, the average rank is at most 1.585. Later refinements by Bhargava, Skinner, and Zhang (2014) improved the bound to 0.885 under the parity conjecture. The moment method extends to higher Selmer groups: Bhargava and Shankar (2015) used the space of 2×3×3 boxes to study the average size of the 3-Selmer group, proving it is 4. The geometry of numbers on prehomogeneous vector spaces provides a uniform framework for counting Selmer elements and bounding average ranks.

Worked Example: Average 2-Selmer Size
Explain the key steps by which Bhargava and Shankar proved that the average size of the 2-Selmer group of elliptic curves over ℚ is 3.
  1. Parametrization: They established a bijection between locally soluble 2-coverings of an elliptic curve E and GL2(ℤ) × GL2(ℤ)-orbits on the space of 2×2×2 integer cubes with non-zero discriminant. The discriminant of the cube equals the discriminant of the associated binary quartic form, which is the height of E.
  2. Counting orbits: They counted the number of integral orbits with height ≤ X using the geometry of numbers. The space of cubes is a prehomogeneous vector space; a fundamental domain for the group action is constructed, and the number of lattice points in the domain is estimated by its volume plus an error term.
  3. Davenport–Heilbronn method: The main term comes from the volume of the region defined by |disc| ≤ X. The error term is controlled by a careful analysis of the cusps of the fundamental domain, yielding an asymptotic formula with a power-saving error.
  4. Average size: Summing over all elliptic curves ordered by height, the total number of 2-Selmer elements up to height X is asymptotic to 3 times the number of elliptic curves up to height X. Hence the average size of the 2-Selmer group is 3.
Exam-Day Strategy
Memorise the key numerical outputs: average 2-Selmer size = 3, average 3-Selmer size = 4, average 2-Selmer rank = log₂3 ≈ 1.585, and the improved average rank bound 0.885 (conditional on parity). Know the parametrisation dictionary: 2×2×2 cube 2-Selmer / quartic rings; 2×3×3 box 3-Selmer / quintic rings; 3×3×3 cube 5-Selmer / sextic rings. The degree of the ring is one more than the smallest slice dimension.

Random Matrix Theory and the Keating–Snaith Conjectures for Moments of L-functions

The Montgomery–Dyson pair correlation (1973) showed that the zeros of the Riemann zeta function have the same local statistics as eigenvalues of random Hermitian matrices from the Gaussian Unitary Ensemble (GUE). Katz and Sarnak (1999) formulated a general philosophy: the statistics of zeros of L-functions in a family should match the eigenvalues of random matrices from a classical compact group (unitary, symplectic, or orthogonal) determined by the symmetry type of the family. Keating and Snaith (2000) used this to conjecture precise formulas for the moments of the Riemann zeta function on the critical line. They modeled ζ(1/2 + it) by the characteristic polynomial of a random unitary matrix, and computed the moments of |ζ(1/2 + it)|2k as integrals over the unitary group U(N) in the limit N → ∞. Their conjecture predicts that the 2k-th moment is asymptotically ak (log T)k2 where ak is a product of a random matrix integral and an arithmetic factor. This has been verified for k = 1, 2 (Hardy–Littlewood, Ingham) and for all k assuming the Riemann Hypothesis and certain conjectures. The ratios conjecture of Conrey, Farmer, and Zirnbauer (2008) extends this to ratios of zeta values. Random matrix models also predict the distribution of values of L-functions at the central point, the distribution of low-lying zeros, and the one-level density. These predictions have been tested numerically and proved in function field settings.

The Delaunay Heuristics for Tate–Shafarevich Groups of Elliptic Curves

In 2001, Delaunay proposed a probabilistic model for the distribution of the Tate–Shafarevich group Sha(E/ℚ) of an elliptic curve over ℚ. The Cassels–Tate pairing gives Sha a non-degenerate alternating bilinear form, making it a finite abelian group with a symplectic structure. Delaunay modeled Sha as the cokernel of a random alternating matrix over the p-adic integers ℤp, with the probability of a given finite abelian p-group G equipped with an alternating form proportional to 1/|Aut(G, form)|. This yields a distribution on isomorphism classes of finite abelian p-groups with alternating forms. For each prime p, the predicted average size of the p-part of Sha is p + 1, and the probability that Sha is trivial is ∏i≥1 (1 − p−2i−1). The Delaunay heuristics are consistent with the Poonen–Rains model for Selmer groups, since the Selmer group sits in an exact sequence with Sha and the Mordell–Weil group. Numerical data for elliptic curves of conductor up to 108 show good agreement with the Delaunay predictions for the distribution of |Sha|. The heuristics also predict that the average rank of elliptic curves is 1/2, in line with the Bhargava–Shankar results.

  • Confusing the Friedman–Washington limit (q → ∞) with the Ellenberg–Venkatesh–Westerland limit (genus → ∞ for fixed q); they are distinct regimes.
  • Assuming the moment method gives the exact average rank; it only gives an upper bound (1.585) unless combined with the parity conjecture.
  • Mixing up the symmetry types: unitary for the Riemann zeta family, symplectic for quadratic twists, orthogonal for families with root number +1.
  • Stating the Keating–Snaith conjecture as proved for all moments; it is only proved for k = 1, 2 unconditionally, and for all k under RH and additional conjectures.
  • Overlooking the alternating form in the Delaunay model; the distribution is on groups with a symplectic structure, not on plain abelian groups.
  • Believing the Delaunay heuristics predict average rank 0; they predict 1/2, matching the Bhargava–Shankar conditional result.
  • Confusing the parametrisation: 2×2×2 cube for 2-Selmer, 2×3×3 box for 3-Selmer, 3×3×3 cube for 5-Selmer; the degree of the ring is one more than the smallest slice dimension.

These advanced topics illustrate how arithmetic statistics, random matrix theory, and the geometry of numbers intertwine to give precise conjectures and theorems about the distribution of fundamental arithmetic invariants.

The Bhargava–Gross–Wang Heuristics for Higher Rank Selmer Groups

In 2017 Bhargava, Gross, and Wang extended the Cohen–Lenstra philosophy to Selmer groups of elliptic curves over ℚ of arbitrary rank. Their model treats the p‑Selmer group as the cokernel of a random p‑adic matrix whose distribution is dictated by the local conditions at each prime. For a fixed prime p, they conjecture that the probability a given finite abelian p‑group G occurs as the p‑Selmer group is proportional to 1/|Aut(G)| multiplied by a product of local densities that depend only on the reduction type of the curve at p. The heuristics predict that the average size of the p‑Selmer group is p+1, matching the Delaunay prediction for the Tate–Shafarevich group when the Mordell–Weil rank is zero. When the rank is positive, the average size grows by a factor of p^r where r is the expected rank. The paper also gives a precise conjectural distribution for the p‑part of the Tate–Shafarevich group conditional on the rank, and shows compatibility with the Poonen–Rains model for Selmer groups of abelian varieties. Numerical data for p=2,3,5 up to conductors 107 agree with the predicted moments to within a few percent.

The Wood–Zureick‑Brown Non‑Abelian Cohen‑Lenstra Heuristics

Melanie Wood and David Zureick‑Brown (2015) formulated a non‑abelian analogue of the Cohen–Lenstra heuristics for class groups of number fields whose Galois closure has a prescribed non‑abelian Galois group G. Instead of weighting a finite abelian group by 1/|Aut|, they weight a finite G‑module M by 1/|Aut_G(M)|, where Aut_G denotes automorphisms commuting with the G‑action. The resulting probability measure on isomorphism classes of finite G‑modules is shown to be the unique measure satisfying a natural “independence of primes” property. For G=S3 they recover the distribution of 3‑torsion in class groups of quadratic fields predicted by Davenport–Heilbronn. For G=D4 the heuristics predict the distribution of 2‑torsion in class groups of quartic D4‑extensions, a case later verified by Bhargava’s parametrisation of quartic rings. The framework also yields conjectures for the distribution of Galois module structures of class groups in towers of extensions, linking arithmetic statistics to the representation theory of finite groups.

Worked Example: Computing a Non‑Abelian Weight
Let G = S3 act on a finite G‑module M ≅ (ℤ/3ℤ)2 with the standard permutation representation. Compute the weight 1/|Aut_G(M)| assigned by the Wood–Zureick‑Brown heuristics.
The G‑module M decomposes as the direct sum of the trivial representation 1 and the 2‑dimensional irreducible representation ρ of S3 over 𝔽3. An automorphism commuting with the G‑action must preserve each isotypic component. On the trivial summand Aut ≅ GL1(𝔽3) of order 2. On the ρ‑summand Aut ≅ GL1(𝔽3) again of order 2 because ρ is absolutely irreducible over 𝔽3. Hence |Aut_G(M)| = 2·2 = 4 and the weight is 1/4.
Exam‑Day Strategy
Memorise the three pillars of the non‑abelian heuristics: (1) replace abelian groups by G‑modules, (2) weight by inverse G‑automorphism group size, (3) enforce prime‑independence. For any concrete G (S3, D4, A4) be ready to write down the isotypic decomposition of the standard permutation module over 𝔽_p and compute |Aut_G|. This yields the predicted average size of the p‑torsion in the class group of a G‑extension.

The Smith Conjecture and Distribution of Class Groups in Cyclic Extensions

In 1990, H. Smith conjectured a precise distribution for the p‑class groups of cyclic extensions of degree p over ℚ. For a fixed odd prime p, let K run over all cyclic extensions of degree p ordered by discriminant. Smith predicted that the p‑part of the class group Cl(K)[p^∞] behaves like the cokernel of a random p‑adic matrix drawn from the Haar measure on the group of p‑adic matrices satisfying the relation (σ−1)^p=0, where σ generates Gal(K/ℚ). Equivalently, the probability that Cl(K)[p^∞] ≅ G is proportional to 1/|Autℤ_p[σ]/((σ−1)^p)(G)|. This measure coincides with the Cohen–Lenstra measure when p=2 (quadratic fields) and with the Davenport–Heilbronn measure for p=3 after accounting for the extra relation. Recent work of Ellenberg, Venkatesh, and Westerland (2016) proved the function‑field analogue of Smith’s conjecture for ℓ‑adic coefficients, and Bhargava–Varma (2020) established the conjecture for p=3 over ℚ using the geometry of numbers on the space of binary cubic forms. The conjecture remains open for p≥5, but numerical data for p=5 up to discriminant 1012 show excellent agreement with the predicted moments.

The Arithmetic Statistics of Torsion in Jacobians of Hyperelliptic Curves

For a hyperelliptic curve C of genus g over ℚ given by y2 = f(x) with f square‑free of degree 2g+1 or 2g+2, the Jacobian J(C) is a principally polarised abelian variety of dimension g. The distribution of the p‑torsion subgroup J(C)[p] as f varies has been studied by Poonen–Rains (2012), Bhargava–Gross–Wang (2017), and Xiong (2020). The heuristics model J(C)[p] as the cokernel of a random alternating matrix over ℤ_p of size 2g, with probability proportional to 1/|Sp2g(ℤ_p)·M| where M is the matrix. This yields an average size of p^g+1 for the p‑torsion, and predicts that the probability J(C)[p] is trivial equals ∏i=1^g (1−p−2i). For g=1 (elliptic curves) this recovers the Delaunay heuristics. For g=2, Bhargava–Gross–Wang proved that the average size of the 2‑torsion in Jacobians of genus‑2 curves is 3, matching the random matrix prediction. Xiong extended the result to all odd p, showing the average size of J(C)[p] is p+1. The distribution of the full p‑primary part remains conjectural, but the moments computed from the random alternating matrix model agree with data for conductors up to 106.

The Geometry of Numbers Approach to Counting Number Fields of Bounded Discriminant

Schmidt’s geometry‑of‑numbers method (1995) counts extensions of ℚ of fixed degree n by embedding the space of rings of integers into a Euclidean space and applying Davenport’s lemma on lattice points in expanding domains. Bhargava (2004‑2008) refined this by parametrising rings of rank n via integral orbits of a reductive group acting on a coregular representation: 2×2×2 cubes for quartic rings, 2×3×3 boxes for quintic rings, and 3×3×3 cubes for sextic rings. The discriminant corresponds to a homogeneous polynomial invariant, and the number of fields with |Disc| ≤ X is asymptotic to c_n X where c_n is an explicit product of local densities. Taniguchi and Thorne (2013) extended the method to count D4‑quartic fields, obtaining c4 = ζ(2)ζ(3)ζ(4)/ζ(5) times a rational factor. For degree 5, Bhargava’s parametrisation of quintic rings via 4×4×4×4 hypercubes (later simplified to 2×3×3 boxes) gave the first asymptotic for quintic fields with Galois group S5. The current best error term for cubic fields is O(X5/6+ε) due to Belabas–Bhargava–Pomerance (2010), while for quartic fields the error is O(X2/3+ε). These results illustrate how the geometry of numbers translates arithmetic counting problems into lattice‑point problems amenable to analytic techniques.

Common Traps

  • Confusing the Wood–Zureick‑Brown weight 1/|Aut_G(M)| with the abelian weight 1/|Aut(M)|; the G‑action can drastically reduce the automorphism group.
  • Assuming the Smith conjecture holds for all primes p; it is proved only for p=2,3 over ℚ and for all p in the function‑field setting.
  • Treating the random alternating matrix model for Jacobians as a model for the full p‑primary torsion; it only governs the p‑torsion subgroup.
  • Mixing the geometry‑of‑numbers parametrisations: 2×2×2 cubes give quartic rings, 2×3×3 boxes give quintic rings, 3×3×3 cubes give sextic rings; the degree is one more than the smallest slice dimension.
  • Believing the average size of p‑Selmer groups is always p+1 regardless of rank; the Bhargava–Gross–Wang heuristics predict a factor p^r when the expected rank is r.
  • Overlooking the local density factors in the non‑abelian heuristics; they depend on the splitting behaviour of primes in the G‑extension and must be included for correct numerical predictions.
  • Stating that the error term for counting quartic fields is O(X1/2); the best known unconditional exponent is 2/3+ε.

The Structure of Class Groups Beyond Cohen-Lenstra

The Cohen-Lenstra Heuristics (CLH) primarily predict the distribution of the p-part of class groups of number fields, often focusing on the probability that the p-part is isomorphic to a given finite abelian p-group G. While remarkably successful, especially for imaginary quadratic fields, the full structure of class groups is more complex, and the heuristics themselves have seen significant refinements and extensions.

For instance, the original CLH for imaginary quadratic fields K = ℚ(√-d) predicts that the probability that the p-part of the class group Cl(K) is isomorphic to G is proportional to 1/|Aut(G)|, weighted by 1/|G| for p=2. This model implicitly assumes that the class group behaves like a random finite abelian p-group. However, the presence of specific arithmetic structures can lead to deviations. For example, for p=2, the 2-rank of the class group of an imaginary quadratic field is directly related to the number of distinct prime factors of the discriminant, by Gauss's genus theory. This means the 2-part is never trivial if the discriminant has more than one prime factor, contradicting a naive application of the CLH for p=2 which would predict a non-zero probability for the trivial group. The CLH for p=2 is thus usually stated for the odd part of the class group, or with specific modifications to account for genus theory.

For real quadratic fields, the Cohen-Lenstra-Martinet (CLM) heuristics are more intricate due to the presence of a non-trivial unit group. The class group Cl(K) of a real quadratic field K is related to the ideal class group, but the regulator (derived from the fundamental unit) plays a crucial role. The CLM heuristics predict that the probability that the p-part of Cl(K) is isomorphic to G is proportional to 1/|Aut(G)| × 1/|G|, but with additional considerations for the unit group. Specifically, for real quadratic fields, the probability of the p-part being trivial is predicted to be higher than for imaginary quadratic fields, reflecting the "swallowing" effect of the fundamental unit on certain ideal classes. For example, the probability that the 3-part of the class group of a real quadratic field is trivial is predicted to be 1 - 1/3 - 1/9 ≈ 0.555, significantly higher than the 1 - 1/3 ≈ 0.667 for imaginary quadratic fields (where the 3-part is trivial with probability 2/3).

Furthermore, the CLH are primarily concerned with the abelian structure of class groups. In more general number fields, especially those with non-abelian Galois groups, the class group structure can be influenced by the Galois module structure. The Wood-Zureick-Brown non-abelian Cohen-Lenstra heuristics extend these ideas by considering the class group as a module over the group ring of the Galois group, leading to predictions for the distribution of these modules. This involves replacing the simple 1/|Aut(G)| factor with 1/|AutG(M)|, where M is a G-module.

Another important aspect is the behavior of exceptional primes. For certain families of number fields, specific primes might behave differently than predicted by the general heuristics. For instance, in cyclotomic fields, the class number is known to be divisible by certain primes related to Bernoulli numbers, leading to deviations from random behavior for those specific primes. These deviations highlight the limits of purely probabilistic models and underscore the deep arithmetic connections that sometimes override statistical randomness. The interplay between Iwasawa theory, which studies the growth of class groups in towers of number fields, and arithmetic statistics provides a deeper understanding of these phenomena, often revealing how the p-adic L-functions govern the structure of p-class groups in such towers.

Explicit Parametrization of Cubic Fields via Binary Cubic Forms

Bhargava's work on the parametrization of number fields provides a concrete method for counting fields of a given degree and Galois group, building upon the geometry of numbers. A foundational example is the parametrization of cubic fields and orders using integral binary cubic forms. This method not only counts fields but also illuminates the structure of their rings of integers.

An integral binary cubic form is a polynomial of the form F(x, y) = ax³ + bx²y + cxy² + dy³ where a, b, c, d are integers. The discriminant of such a form is given by Disc(F) = b²c² - 4ac³ - 4b³d - 27a²d² + 18abcd. This discriminant is a fundamental invariant. The key insight, building on earlier work by Delone and Faddeev, is that there is a canonical bijection between the set of GL₂(ℤ)-equivalence classes of integral binary cubic forms with non-zero discriminant and the set of isomorphism classes of cubic rings (rings that are free ℤ-modules of rank 3) up to isomorphism. Furthermore, irreducible forms correspond to maximal cubic orders, which are the rings of integers of cubic fields.

The action of GL₂(ℤ) on binary cubic forms (by linear change of variables) preserves the discriminant and corresponds to isomorphism of the associated cubic rings. For example, if F(x, y) corresponds to a cubic ring R, then a form G(x, y) = F(px + qy, rx + sy) for a matrix [[p, q], [r, s]] in GL₂(ℤ) corresponds to a ring isomorphic to R.

To count cubic fields of bounded discriminant, one counts the number of GL₂(ℤ)-equivalence classes of irreducible binary cubic forms F(x, y) such that |Disc(F)| ≤ X. This is where the geometry of numbers comes into play. The coefficients (a, b, c, d) of the binary cubic form can be viewed as integer lattice points in a 4-dimensional Euclidean space ℤ⁴. The discriminant is a homogeneous polynomial of degree 4 in these coefficients. The challenge is to count lattice points in a region defined by |Disc(F)| ≤ X, modulo the action of GL₂(ℤ).

Bhargava's method involves defining a fundamental domain for the action of GL₂(ℤ) on the space of binary cubic forms. This fundamental domain is a region in ℝ⁴. Counting integer points in this expanding region as X grows gives an asymptotic formula. The number of cubic fields K with |Disc(K)| ≤ X is asymptotic to c₃X, where c₃ is a constant derived from the volume of the fundamental domain and local densities. Specifically, c₃ = (1/3) × (∏p (1 + p⁻¹ - p⁻² - p⁻³)) × ζ(2) × ζ(3). The factor of 1/3 accounts for the fact that each cubic field corresponds to three distinct binary cubic forms (related to the three embeddings into ℂ).

This method has been generalized by Bhargava to count number fields of higher degrees (quartic, quintic, sextic) by identifying them with orbits of specific coregular representations of reductive groups. For instance, quartic fields are parametrized by 2×2×2 cubes of integers, and quintic fields by 2×3×3 boxes of integers. The discriminant of the field corresponds to a specific invariant polynomial of these objects. The geometry of numbers then allows for the asymptotic counting of these objects within a bounded discriminant range.

Application of Discriminant Calculation
Consider the binary cubic form F(x, y) = x³ + xy² + y³. Determine its discriminant and discuss what this implies about the associated cubic ring.
For F(x, y) = ax³ + bx²y + cxy² + dy³, we have a=1, b=0, c=1, d=1. The discriminant formula is Disc(F) = b²c² - 4ac³ - 4b³d - 27a²d² + 18abcd. Substitute the values: Disc(F) = (0)²(1)² - 4(1)(1)³ - 4(0)³(1) - 27(1)²(1)² + 18(1)(0)(1)(1) Disc(F) = 0 - 4 - 0 - 27 + 0 Disc(F) = -31. Since the discriminant is non-zero, this form corresponds to a cubic ring. Moreover, since -31 is square-free, the form is irreducible over ℚ, and the associated cubic ring is a maximal order, which is the ring of integers of a cubic field. The field is ℚ(θ) where θ is a root of x³ + x + 1 = 0 (obtained by setting y=1 in F(x,y) and solving for x). The discriminant of this cubic field is -31. This is an example of a totally complex cubic field, as its discriminant is negative.
Mastering Parametrization Details
When studying Bhargava's parametrizations, don't just memorize the "2x2x2 cube for quartic rings" type of statement. Understand the *correspondence*: what mathematical objects are being mapped to what, what invariants are preserved (like the discriminant), and how the group action relates to isomorphism. This deeper understanding helps in solving problems that require applying these concepts, rather than just recalling them. Focus on the cubic case as a detailed exemplar.

Moments of L-functions and Random Matrix Ensembles

The connection between the statistics of zeros of L-functions and eigenvalues of random matrices, famously articulated by Katz and Sarnak, extends to the moments of L-functions. The Keating-Snaith conjectures provide a precise "recipe" for predicting the asymptotic behavior of moments of families of L-functions, drawing directly from Random Matrix Theory (RMT).

A moment of an L-function L(s, f) is typically an average value of L(1/2 + it, f) raised to some power, often averaged over a family of L-functions. For example, the k-th moment of the Riemann zeta function ζ(s) is the asymptotic average of |ζ(1/2 + it)|²ᵏ as t → ∞. For families of L-functions, the average is taken over the family members, ordered by conductor.

The Keating-Snaith conjectures (2000) propose that the moments of L-functions can be modeled by the characteristic polynomials of random matrices from specific classical compact groups. The "recipe" involves:

  1. Identifying the Random Matrix Ensemble: The first step is to determine which classical compact group (Unitary U(N), Symplectic Sp(2N), or Orthogonal O(N)) corresponds to the arithmetic family of L-functions under consideration. This choice is guided by the symmetry type of the family, often related to the functional equation of the L-functions. For example, families of L-functions associated with elliptic curves often correspond to the Symplectic ensemble, while Dirichlet L-functions might correspond to Unitary or Orthogonal ensembles depending on the specific family.
  2. Averaging Characteristic Polynomials: The k-th moment of the L-function is then predicted by the average of the k-th power of the characteristic polynomial of a random matrix from the identified ensemble, evaluated at a specific point (usually related to the central point s=1/2).
  3. Introducing Arithmetic Factors: Crucially, the RMT predictions must be multiplied by "arithmetic factors" or "Euler products." These factors account for the local behavior of the L-functions at finite primes and infinity, which are not captured by the generic RMT model. These factors are typically products over primes p of local densities, reflecting the specific arithmetic of the family.

For instance, for the 2k-th moment of the Riemann zeta function on the critical line, the RMT prediction comes from the Unitary ensemble. The asymptotic formula for ∫₀ᵀ |ζ(1/2 + it)|²ᵏ dt is conjectured to be proportional to T (log T)ᵏ² times an arithmetic factor. The (log T)ᵏ² term arises directly from the average of characteristic polynomials in the Unitary ensemble.

The specific ensembles are:

  • Unitary Ensemble (U(N)): Corresponds to families of L-functions with no specific symmetry beyond the functional equation, or those where the conductor grows. Examples include the family of Dirichlet L-functions L(s, χq) for fixed q and varying characters χ, or the family of L-functions of modular forms.
  • Symplectic Ensemble (Sp(2N)): Corresponds to families of L-functions whose functional equation exhibits a specific symmetry, often related to elliptic curves or Siegel modular forms. For example, the family of L-functions of elliptic curves ordered by conductor.
  • Orthogonal Ensemble (O(N)): Corresponds to families of L-functions with another type of symmetry, often related to real quadratic fields or specific types of number field extensions.

These conjectures have been numerically verified in many cases and have led to significant theoretical progress. For example, the work of Conrey, Farmer, Keating, Rubinstein, and Snaith (2005) provided a detailed conjecture for the moments of L-functions in various families, including the Unitary, Symplectic, and Orthogonal cases, with explicit arithmetic factors. While proving these conjectures remains a major challenge in analytic number theory, they provide a powerful framework for understanding the statistical behavior of L-functions and connecting them to deep results in random matrix theory.

The Selmer-Shafarevich Exact Sequence and Rank Heuristics

The study of Selmer groups is central to understanding the arithmetic of elliptic curves and abelian varieties, particularly their Mordell-Weil group and the elusive Tate-Shafarevich group. The connection is encapsulated in a fundamental exact sequence, which links these objects and provides a framework for heuristics about their sizes and distributions.

For an elliptic curve E defined over ℚ, the Mordell-Weil theorem states that the group of rational points E(ℚ) is a finitely generated abelian group, E(ℚ) ≅ ℤʳ ⊕ E(ℚ)tors, where r is the rank and E(ℚ)tors is the finite torsion subgroup. The rank r is one of the most mysterious invariants of an elliptic curve. The Tate-Shafarevich group Ш(E/ℚ) (pronounced "Sha") measures the failure of the Hasse principle for principal homogeneous spaces for E. It is conjectured to be finite.

For a prime p, the p-Selmer group Selp(E/ℚ) is defined as a subgroup of H¹(ℚ, E[p]), where E[p] is the p-torsion subgroup of E(ℂ). It is constructed using local conditions at all primes (including infinity) and captures information about the global points. The Selmer group is finite and computable.

The crucial link between these groups is the following short exact sequence (or a variant thereof, depending on the context and specific definitions):
0 → E(ℚ)/pE(ℚ) → Selp(E/ℚ) → Ш(E/ℚ)[p] → 0

Here, E(ℚ)/pE(ℚ) is isomorphic to (ℤ/pℤ)ʳ ⊕ E(ℚ)tors[p]. The dimension of E(ℚ)/pE(ℚ) as a ℤ/pℤ-vector space is r + dimℤ/pℤ(E(ℚ)tors[p]).
This sequence implies that:
dimℤ/pℤ(Selp(E/ℚ)) = r + dimℤ/pℤ(E(ℚ)tors[p]) + dimℤ/pℤ(Ш(E/ℚ)[p])

The Bhargava-Shankar method, as discussed previously, provides heuristics for the average size of Selp(E/ℚ) (specifically, Sel₂(E/ℚ) and Sel₃(E/ℚ)). Their results predict that the average size of Sel₂(E/ℚ) for elliptic curves ordered by conductor is 3, and for Sel₃(E/ℚ) it is 4.

From the exact sequence, if we assume Ш(E/ℚ)[p] is trivial on average (or bounded), then the average rank r can be deduced from the average size of Selp(E/ℚ). For example, for curves with trivial 2-torsion (E(ℚ)tors[2] = 0), the average dimension of Sel₂(E/ℚ) is 1. This would imply an average rank r of 1, if Ш(E/ℚ)[2] is trivial. However, the Bhargava-Shankar results show that the average rank is bounded by 1/2 for Sel₂(E/ℚ) and 1/3 for Sel₃(E/ℚ). This discrepancy implies that Ш(E/ℚ)[p] is not trivial on average, but rather contributes to the size of the Selmer group.

Specifically, the average size of Sel₂(E/ℚ) is 3, and for curves with trivial 2-torsion, this implies that on average, r + dim(Ш(E/ℚ)[2]) = 1. This leads to the prediction that the average rank of elliptic curves is 1/2, and the average size of Ш(E/ℚ)[2] is 1/2. This is a profound result, as it suggests that the average rank is not an integer, but rather that curves with rank 0 and rank 1 occur with roughly equal probability (and higher ranks are rarer). The existence of Ш(E/ℚ) is thus crucial for these rank predictions.

The Delaunay heuristics for Tate-Shafarevich groups, mentioned in the existing lesson, provide further predictions for the distribution of Ш(E/ℚ)[p]. These heuristics, based on random matrices, suggest that Ш(E/ℚ)[p] is often non-trivial and contributes significantly to the Selmer group, thus influencing the observed distribution of ranks. The full understanding of the Selmer-Shafarevich exact sequence, combined with these statistical heuristics, offers the most comprehensive picture of the arithmetic of elliptic curves.

Refinements of Malle's Conjecture and the Inverse Galois Problem

Malle's Conjecture is a central hypothesis in arithmetic statistics, providing an asymptotic formula for the number of number fields with a given Galois group G and bounded discriminant. It is deeply connected to the Inverse Galois Problem, which asks whether every finite group G can be realized as the Galois group of some field extension K/ℚ. Malle's conjecture provides a quantitative answer to how many such fields exist.

The original Malle's Conjecture (2002) states that for a transitive finite group G, the number of number fields K/ℚ with Galois group Gal(K/ℚ) ≅ G and |Disc(K)| ≤ X is asymptotic to cG X(aG) (log X)(bG-1) for some positive constant cG, and integers aG and bG. The exponents aG and bG are defined purely in terms of the group G.
Specifically, aG = 1 / minH≤G, H≠{1} (index(G:H) - 1) where the minimum is taken over all non-trivial subgroups H of G.
And bG is the number of conjugacy classes of maximal subgroups of G that attain this minimum.

However, Malle's original conjecture was found to be incorrect for certain groups. For example, Klüners (2004) found a counterexample for G = C₃ × C₃, the direct product of two cyclic groups of order 3. For this group, the conjecture predicted aG = 1/4, but numerical data and later theoretical work suggested a different exponent. This led to refined versions of Malle's Conjecture.

The refined Malle's Conjecture (sometimes called the Malle-Klüners Conjecture or the "corrected" Malle's Conjecture) introduces an additional condition related to the existence of certain "exceptional" prime factors in the discriminant. The refined conjecture modifies the exponent aG or introduces additional logarithmic factors for specific groups. The key insight is that the discriminant of a number field is not just a product of local factors but also depends on the ramification behavior of primes. For certain groups, the minimal index subgroup condition might not fully capture the density, especially when there are specific local obstructions to realizing certain ramification types.

For example, for abelian groups, the refined Malle's Conjecture is often formulated in terms of the conductor-discriminant formula and the structure of abelian extensions. For G = C₃ × C₃, the corrected exponent aG is 1/2, not 1/4. This correction arises from considering the structure of the relevant field extensions and their ramification.

The significance of Malle's Conjecture lies in its ability to provide a statistical framework for the Inverse Galois Problem. While the Inverse Galois Problem asks for existence, Malle's Conjecture quantifies the abundance of such extensions. Proving Malle's Conjecture, even in its refined form, is extremely challenging. It has been proven for several classes of groups, including abelian groups, S₃ (symmetric group on 3 elements), and S₄. For many other groups, it remains open, and numerical evidence often guides the formulation of the precise exponents and constants.

The methods used to approach Malle's Conjecture often involve a combination of:

  1. Geometry of Numbers: As seen in Bhargava's work, parametrizing number fields by orbits of group actions on representations.
  2. Analytic Number Theory: Counting lattice points in fundamental domains and applying sieve methods.
  3. Galois Cohomology: Understanding the structure of field extensions and their ramification.

The study of Malle's Conjecture continues to be an active area of research, pushing the boundaries of what is known about the distribution of number fields and their Galois groups. It provides a bridge between abstract group theory and concrete counting problems in number theory.

Common Traps

  • Confusing the original Malle's Conjecture with its refined versions; the original is known to be incorrect for certain groups like C₃ × C₃. Always refer to the refined or corrected versions when discussing its general applicability.
  • Assuming the average rank of elliptic curves is an integer based on Selmer group heuristics; the Bhargava-Shankar results imply an average rank of 1/2 or 1/3, indicating the non-trivial average contribution of the Tate-Shafarevich group.
  • Overlooking the role of arithmetic factors in Keating-Snaith conjectures for moments of L-functions; Random Matrix Theory provides the leading power of log X, but the precise constant requires these arithmetic factors.
  • Applying Cohen-Lenstra Heuristics for p=2 to imaginary quadratic fields without accounting for Gauss's genus theory; the 2-rank is constrained by the number of prime factors of the discriminant.
  • Misinterpreting the Selmer-Shafarevich exact sequence as directly equating the dimension of the Selmer group to the rank; it also includes the contribution from the p-torsion of the Mordell-Weil group and the p-torsion of the Tate-Shafarevich group.
  • Believing that Bhargava's parametrization of cubic fields via binary cubic forms is the only way to count them; it is a powerful method, but other approaches exist, though often less effective for asymptotic counting.
  • Ignoring the specific random matrix ensemble (Unitary, Orthogonal, Symplectic) when discussing moments of L-functions; the choice of ensemble is crucial and depends on the symmetry of the L-function family.

The Distribution of 2‑Selmer Groups in Quadratic Twist Families

The quadratic twist family of an elliptic curve E over Q provides a natural testing ground for the Cohen–Lenstra philosophy applied to Selmer groups. For a fixed elliptic curve E and a fundamental discriminant d, the 2‑Selmer group Sel2(E(d)) fits into the exact sequence

0 → E(d)(Q)/2E(d)(Q) → Sel2(E(d)) → Ш(E(d))[2] → 0

where Ш denotes the Tate–Shafarevich group. Heath‑Brown (2004) proved that the average size of the 2‑Selmer group in the family of quadratic twists of the congruent number curve y2 = x3 − x equals 3. Kane (2013) extended this to all elliptic curves with full rational 2‑torsion, showing the same average. Smith (2017) removed the 2‑torsion hypothesis and established that for any elliptic curve E/Q the average size of Sel2(E(d)) over fundamental discriminants d with |d| ≤ X tends to 3 as X → ∞.

The limiting distribution of the 2‑Selmer groups is conjectured to follow the Poonen–Rains model for the cokernel of a random alternating matrix over F2. In this model the probability that the 2‑Selmer group has dimension r is proportional to 1/|Aut(G)| where G runs over finite abelian 2‑groups equipped with a non‑degenerate alternating pairing. The predicted frequencies are:

  • dim = 0 (trivial group) with probability 1/2,
  • dim = 1 (group ≅ Z/2Z) with probability 1/4,
  • dim = 2 (group ≅ (Z/2Z)2) with probability 1/8,
  • and in general dim = r with probability 2−(r+1).

These probabilities sum to 1 and give an average size of 3, matching the proven average. The work of Bhargava–Klagsbrun–Lemke Oliver–Shankar (2020) on the distribution of 2‑Selmer groups in quadratic twist families of higher rank curves provides further numerical confirmation of the Poonen–Rains predictions.

The Bhargava–Shankar–Wang Theorem on Average 5‑Selmer Ranks

Building on the geometry‑of‑numbers method that yielded the average size of 2‑ and 3‑Selmer groups, Bhargava, Shankar, and Wang (2019) determined the average size of the 5‑Selmer group of elliptic curves over Q when ordered by height. They proved that the average size of Sel5(E) is exactly 6. The proof parametrizes 5‑Selmer elements by orbits of the group G = GL2 × GL4 acting on a 40‑dimensional representation arising from the invariant theory of the 5×5×5 cube. The main term comes from counting integral orbits of bounded height in a fundamental domain, while the error term is controlled by a sieve that removes orbits corresponding to non‑locally‑soluble 5‑coverings.

The result has immediate consequences for the average rank. Since the 5‑Selmer group sits in the exact sequence

0 → E(Q)/5E(Q) → Sel5(E) → Ш(E)[5] → 0,

the average 5‑Selmer rank (the F5-dimension of the Selmer group) is log5 6 ≈ 1.113. Combined with the known average 2‑Selmer rank of 1/2 and the average 3‑Selmer rank of 2/3, this forces the average Mordell–Weil rank to be at most 1/2. In fact, Bhargava–Shankar (2015) had already shown the average rank is ≤ 0.885; the 5‑Selmer bound sharpens this to ≤ 0.5, and together with the parity conjecture it suggests the average rank is exactly 1/2.

The theorem also yields a distribution conjecture for the 5‑Selmer group modeled on the cokernel of a random 5×5 alternating matrix over F5. The predicted probabilities for the 5‑Selmer group to be isomorphic to a given finite abelian 5‑group G with alternating pairing are proportional to 1/|Aut(G)|. Numerical data from the LMFDB up to height 1010 agree with these predictions to within the expected error margins.

Worked Example: Average 5‑Selmer Size
An exam question asks: "Using the Bhargava–Shankar–Wang theorem, compute the average 5‑Selmer rank of elliptic curves over Q ordered by height, and explain why this implies an upper bound for the average Mordell–Weil rank."
The theorem states the average size of the 5‑Selmer group is 6. The 5‑Selmer rank is the dimension of this group as an F5-vector space, so the average rank equals log5(6) ≈ 1.113. The exact sequence 0 → E(Q)/5E(Q) → Sel5(E) → Ш(E)[5] → 0 shows that the 5‑Selmer rank equals the Mordell–Weil rank plus the 5‑rank of Ш(E). Since the 5‑rank of Ш(E) is non‑negative, the Mordell–Weil rank is ≤ the 5‑Selmer rank for each curve. Averaging preserves the inequality, giving average rank ≤ 1.113. Combined with the 2‑Selmer average rank of 1/2 and the 3‑Selmer average rank of 2/3, the strongest bound is 1/2. Hence the average Mordell–Weil rank is at most 1/2.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a question asks for an average Selmer rank, first recall the exact average size theorem (2‑Selmer = 3, 3‑Selmer = 4, 5‑Selmer = 6). Convert size to rank by taking logp. Then use the Selmer exact sequence to bound the Mordell–Weil rank. Remember that the average of a minimum is not the minimum of averages; the inequality rank ≤ Selmer rank holds curve‑by‑curve, so it survives averaging.

The Geometry of Numbers for Counting Quartic Fields via Pairs of Ternary Quadratic Forms

Bhargava’s 2004 parametrization of quartic rings identifies isomorphism classes of quartic rings R with GL2(Z) × GL3(Z)-orbits on pairs of ternary quadratic forms (Q1, Q2) in 12 variables. The discriminant of the quartic ring equals the discriminant of the pair of forms, a homogeneous polynomial of degree 12. To count quartic fields of bounded discriminant, one counts integral orbits with |Disc| ≤ X in a fundamental domain for the group action. The main term is obtained by integrating the characteristic function of the region over the fundamental domain, yielding an asymptotic formula

N4(X) = c4 X + O(X1−δ)

where c4 = (π2/720) ∏p (1 − p−2) ≈ 0.037. The constant factorizes as a product of an archimedean volume and local densities at each prime, reflecting the Cohen–Lenstra heuristic that the probability a random quartic ring is maximal at p equals the local density. Bhargava (2005) proved this asymptotic with a power‑saving error term using a geometry‑of‑numbers argument that combines a Davenport‑type lemma for the GL2 action with a sieve for the GL3 action.

The same method extends to counting quartic fields with prescribed Galois group. For S4 extensions the constant is c4; for D4 extensions the constant involves an extra factor of 1/2 coming from the index of the stabilizer. The parametrization also yields the distribution of the 2‑torsion in the class group of quartic fields: the 2‑rank of the narrow class group corresponds to the number of independent pairs of forms that become equivalent over Q2. This connection was exploited by Bhargava–Varma (2015) to prove that the average size of the 2‑torsion subgroup in the class group of S4 quartic fields is 3, matching the Cohen–Lenstra prediction for the 2‑part of class groups of degree‑4 fields.

The Random Matrix Model for Families of L‑functions with Orthogonal Symmetry

Katz and Sarnak (1999) formulated a precise conjecture linking the statistics of low‑lying zeros of families of L‑functions to eigenvalue distributions of classical compact groups. For families with orthogonal symmetry the relevant ensembles are SO(even) and SO(odd). The symmetry type is determined by the root number of the functional equation: a family where the root number is +1 for all members corresponds to SO(even), while a family with root number equidistributed between +1 and −1 corresponds to SO(odd).

Typical examples of SO(even) families include quadratic Dirichlet L‑functions L(s, χd) with fundamental discriminant d > 0, and symmetric square L‑functions of holomorphic cusp forms. For SO(odd) families one has quadratic Dirichlet L‑functions with d < 0, and L‑functions of elliptic curves over Q twisted by quadratic characters. The n‑level density of zeros near the central point s = 1/2 is conjectured to match the n‑level density of eigenangles of random matrices from the corresponding orthogonal group. For test functions whose Fourier transform has support in (−2, 2) the n‑level density has been proved unconditionally for many families (Rubinstein 2001, Gao 2005, Miller 2006).

The one‑level density for SO(even) is

W1(SO(even))(f) = ∫ f(x) dx − (1/2) f(0),

while for SO(odd) it is

W1(SO(odd))(f) = ∫ f(x) dx + (1/2) f(0).

The difference of a half‑unit mass at the origin reflects the forced zero at the central point for SO(odd) families (the functional equation forces L(1/2) = 0 when the root number is −1). Higher moments of L‑functions in these families are predicted by the Keating–Snaith conjecture: the 2k‑th moment of L(1/2, χd) over fundamental discriminants |d| ≤ X grows like ak (log X)k(k+1)/2 where ak is a product of an arithmetic factor and a random matrix integral over the orthogonal group. The arithmetic factor involves the Euler product of local coefficients, while the random matrix integral is a Selberg‑type integral over SO(2N) or SO(2N+1) in the limit N → ∞.

  • Confusing the original Malle conjecture with its refined versions; the original is known to be incorrect for certain groups like C₃ × C₃.
  • Assuming the average rank of elliptic curves is an integer based on Selmer group heuristics; the Bhargava–Shankar results imply an average rank of 1/2 or 1/3, indicating the non‑trivial average contribution of the Tate–Shafarevich group.
  • Overlooking the role of arithmetic factors in Keating–Snaith conjectures for moments of L‑functions; Random Matrix Theory provides the leading power of log X, but the precise constant requires these arithmetic factors.
  • Applying Cohen–Lenstra heuristics for p = 2 to imaginary quadratic fields without accounting for Gauss’s genus theory; the 2‑rank is constrained by the number of prime factors of the discriminant.
  • Misinterpreting the Selmer–Shafarevich exact sequence as directly equating the dimension of the Selmer group to the rank; it also includes the contribution from the p‑torsion of the Mordell–Weil group and the p‑torsion of the Tate–Shafarevich group.
  • Believing that Bhargava’s parametrization of cubic fields via binary cubic forms is the only way to count them; it is a powerful method, but other approaches exist, though often less effective for asymptotic counting.
  • Ignoring the specific random matrix ensemble (Unitary, Orthogonal, Symplectic) when discussing moments of L‑functions; the choice of ensemble is crucial and depends on the symmetry of the L‑function family.

The Cohen‑Lenstra Heuristics for Higher p‑Parts and the Role of the p‑Adic Regulator

The original Cohen‑Lenstra heuristics predict the distribution of the p‑part of the class group of an imaginary quadratic field by weighting each finite abelian p‑group G proportionally to 1/|Aut(G)|. When one moves to higher p‑parts, the same weighting appears but the underlying probability space changes: the p‑adic regulator of the field enters the heuristic through the volume of the unit lattice in the p‑adic completion. For a fixed odd prime p, the conjectural probability that the p‑rank equals r is given by a product over k≥1 of (1−p−k) multiplied by a factor involving the p‑adic regulator. This refinement was first made precise by Cohen and Martinet (1990) and later extended by Garton (2015) to arbitrary number fields. The p‑adic regulator measures the failure of the global units to be p‑adically independent; its appearance explains why the heuristics for real quadratic fields differ by a factor of 2 in the 2‑part.

In practice, computing the p‑adic regulator for a given field requires a basis of the unit group modulo torsion and the p‑adic logarithm map. The regulator enters the heuristic as a normalising constant that makes the total mass of the probability distribution equal to 1. For imaginary quadratic fields the unit group is finite, so the regulator is 1 and the original weighting is recovered. For fields with positive unit rank, the regulator grows roughly like (log ε)^r where ε is a fundamental unit and r is the unit rank. This growth suppresses the probability of large p‑rank, matching the empirical observation that class groups of real quadratic fields tend to have smaller p‑parts than those of imaginary quadratic fields of comparable discriminant.

The heuristic also predicts correlations between the p‑part of the class group and the p‑adic valuation of the regulator. Numerical experiments by Malle (2008) and by Bhargava–Varma (2015) confirm that the joint distribution of (Cl_K[p^∞], v_p(Reg_K)) follows the product of the Cohen‑Lenstra weight and a simple explicit density on the regulator valuation. This joint model has been used to predict the frequency of fields with prescribed p‑rank and regulator valuation, a problem that remains open for general number fields but is now accessible for quadratic families via the geometry‑of‑numbers methods of Bhargava–Shankar–Wang.

The Bhargava–Shankar–Tsimerman Method for Average 3‑Selmer Ranks of Elliptic Curves

Building on the geometry‑of‑numbers framework that counts 2‑Selmer groups via binary quartic forms, Bhargava, Shankar and Tsimerman (2015) developed a parametrisation of 3‑Selmer elements of elliptic curves over Q by integral orbits of the representation of GL2 × SL3 on the space of pairs of ternary quadratic forms. The invariant theory of this representation yields a discriminant polynomial of degree 12 whose square‑root is the conductor of the associated elliptic curve. By applying a sieve to the lattice of integral orbits, they proved that the average size of the 3‑Selmer group in the family of all elliptic curves ordered by height is exactly 4. This implies an average 3‑Selmer rank of 1/2, and together with the known bound on the average 2‑Selmer rank (3/2) gives an unconditional upper bound of 7/6 for the average Mordell–Weil rank.

The proof proceeds in three stages. First, a fundamental domain for the action of GL2(Z) × SL3(Z) on the real vector space of pairs of ternary quadratic forms is constructed using a combination of Minkowski reduction for the GL2 factor and a Siegel set for the SL3 factor. Second, a Davenport‑type lemma shows that the number of integral orbits with height ≤ X is asymptotic to a constant times X5/6, where the constant is an explicit product of local densities. Third, a square‑free sieve removes orbits corresponding to non‑maximal 3‑Selmer groups; the sieve succeeds because the discriminant polynomial is square‑free on a set of density 1. The local densities are computed by evaluating the p‑adic orbital integrals, which factor as a product of a Cohen‑Lenstra‑type factor for the 3‑part of the class group of the associated cubic resolvent field and a factor coming from the geometry of the representation.

A striking corollary is that the distribution of the 3‑Selmer group, when weighted by 1/|Aut|, matches the distribution of the cokernel of a random 3 × 3 alternating matrix over Z3. This matches the Poonen‑Rains model for p‑Selmer groups and provides the first case where the full distribution (not just the average size) is proved for a p‑Selmer group with p > 2. The method also yields a power‑saving error term, which is essential for applications to the distribution of ranks in quadratic twist families.

Worked Example: Computing the Average 3‑Selmer Size
Using the Bhargava–Shankar–Tsimerman theorem, determine the average size of the 3‑Selmer group of elliptic curves over Q ordered by naive height, and explain why this implies an average 3‑Selmer rank of 1/2.
The theorem states that the average size of the 3‑Selmer group is exactly 4. For a finite abelian 3‑group G, the 3‑rank is dimF3(G/3G). The expected 3‑rank under the distribution weighted by 1/|Aut(G)| can be computed from the known probabilities: P(G≅0)=1/2, P(G≅Z/3)=1/3, P(G≅(Z/3)2)=1/8, etc. The weighted average of the 3‑rank equals Σ r·P(rank=r) = 1/2. Hence the average 3‑Selmer rank is 1/2.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a question asks for the average size of a p‑Selmer group, recall the Bhargava–Shankar results: average 2‑Selmer size = 3, average 3‑Selmer size = 4, average 5‑Selmer size = 6. The pattern is average size = p+1 for p=2,3,5. Memorise these three numbers; they appear frequently in multiple‑choice questions on arithmetic statistics.

The Katz–Sarnak Density Conjecture for Low‑Lying Zeros in Families with Symplectic Symmetry

Katz and Sarnak (1999) conjectured that the statistics of low‑lying zeros of a family of L‑functions are governed by the eigenvalue distribution of a classical compact group attached to the family. For families with symplectic symmetry the relevant group is USp(2N) in the limit N→∞.
