Typical examples include the family of quadratic twists of a fixed elliptic curve with root number +1, and the family of symmetric square L‑functions of holomorphic cusp forms. The one‑level density for the symplectic ensemble is W1(USp)(f)=∫ f(x) dx − ½ f(0), which differs from the orthogonal SO(even) density by the sign of the half‑mass at the origin. This sign reflects the fact that symplectic families have no forced zero at the central point, whereas SO(odd) families have a forced zero and SO(even) families have a repulsion from the central point.

The n‑level density conjecture asserts that for any even Schwartz function f whose Fourier transform has support in (−2/n, 2/n), the scaled n‑level correlation sum over zeros γ_j of L(s,π) in the family converges to the corresponding n‑level correlation of eigenangles of USp(2N). The support restriction is essential: it is the range where the explicit formula for the zeros involves only the prime numbers up to the analytic conductor, allowing the use of the Ratios Conjecture or the Density Theorem of Rudnick–Sarnak. For symplectic families, the support condition can be widened to (−1, 1) for the one‑level density because the symplectic kernel has a simpler Fourier transform. This wider range has been exploited by Miller (2004) and by Dueñez–Miller (2006) to prove the one‑level density for quadratic twist families of elliptic curves under GRH.

Higher moments of L‑functions in symplectic families are predicted by the Keating–Snaith conjecture. The 2k‑th moment of L(½, E_d) over fundamental discriminants d with |d|≤X grows like a_k (log X)k(k+1)/2 where a_k splits into an arithmetic factor (an Euler product of local coefficients) and a random matrix integral over USp(2N). The random matrix integral is a Selberg‑type integral that evaluates to a product of Gamma functions. The arithmetic factor for quadratic twists involves the product over primes of (1−1/p)k2 times a local density that depends on the reduction type of the elliptic curve at p. This factorisation mirrors the factorisation for orthogonal families but with the symplectic Euler product.

The Geometry of Numbers for Counting Quintic Fields via 5×5×5 Cubes and the Role of the Discriminant

Bhargava’s higher composition laws give a parametrisation of quintic rings by integral orbits of the group GL2 × GL3 acting on the space of 5 × 5 × 5 cubes (pairs of 5 × 5 matrices). The invariant theory of this representation produces a degree‑40 discriminant polynomial whose square‑root is the discriminant of the quintic ring. By constructing a fundamental domain for the action on the real vector space of cubes and applying a geometry‑of‑numbers lattice‑point count, Bhargava (2008) proved that the number of quintic fields of discriminant ≤ X is asymptotic to c5 X where c5 is an explicit constant involving a product of local densities. The constant c5 splits as a product over primes of the p‑adic density of maximal quintic rings, which can be expressed in terms of the probability that a random 5 × 5 × 5 cube over Z_p corresponds to a maximal order.

The local density computation uses the fact that the stabiliser of a generic cube is trivial, so the orbital integral reduces to the volume of the set of cubes with bounded discriminant. This volume is evaluated by a change of variables that diagonalises the discriminant polynomial into a product of linear forms, a technique that generalises the Davenport‑Heilbronn method for cubic fields. The resulting p‑adic density is a rational function in p−1 that matches the Cohen‑Lenstra‑Martinet prediction for the distribution of the p‑part of the class group of quintic fields. In particular, the average size of the 2‑torsion in the class group of S5 quintic fields is predicted to be 3, and Bhargava–Varma (2015) proved this by relating the 2‑torsion to the number of independent pairs of ternary quadratic forms arising from the cube parametrisation.

The method also yields the distribution of Galois groups among quintic fields. The proportion of S5 fields is 1, while the proportion of D5 fields is 0, and the proportion of Frobenius group F5 fields is a positive constant that can be computed from the local densities. This matches Malle’s conjecture for the group F5, providing the first case where Malle’s exponent and constant are proved for a non‑abelian group of order > 12. The geometry‑of‑numbers approach extends to counting sextic fields via 2 × 3 × 3 boxes, but the discriminant polynomial degree grows rapidly, making the lattice‑point count substantially more difficult.

The Non‑Abelian Cohen‑Lenstra Heuristics for Galois Groups of Extensions

Wood and Zureick‑Brown (2017) formulated a non‑abelian analogue of the Cohen‑Lenstra heuristics that predicts the distribution of Galois groups of unramified extensions of a number field K. The heuristic assigns to each finite group G a weight proportional to 1/|Aut(G)| multiplied by a factor that depends on the number of surjections from the absolute Galois group of K onto G. For a fixed base field K, the probability that the maximal unramified extension has Galois group isomorphic to G is conjectured to be proportional to |Hom(Gal(Kur/K), G)|/|Aut(G)|. When K is an imaginary quadratic field, this reduces to the original Cohen‑Lenstra distribution for the abelianisation (the class group) because the non‑abelian part is expected to be trivial with probability 1.

The heuristic makes concrete predictions for the frequency of non‑abelian unramified extensions. For example, the probability that a quadratic field has an unramified extension with Galois group S3 is predicted to be 1/6 times the probability that its class group has 3‑rank at least 1. Numerical data of Boston–Bush–Hajir (2017) for imaginary quadratic fields with discriminant up to 107 show excellent agreement with this prediction. The heuristic also predicts that the proportion of fields whose maximal unramified extension is a given finite group G is zero unless G is a quotient of the profinite completion of the fundamental group of the associated arithmetic orbifold, a condition that can be checked using group‑theoretic invariants such as the relation rank.

A key technical tool in the formulation is the notion of a “random profinite group” sampled from the Haar measure on the space of isomorphism classes of profinite groups weighted by 1/|Aut|. The moments of this random group are given by the number of homomorphisms from a fixed finitely presented group into the random group. This perspective connects the non‑abelian Cohen‑Lenstra heuristics to the theory of random groups à la Gromov and to the work of Ellenberg–Venkatesh–Westerland on homological stability for Hurwitz spaces. The latter provides a function‑field analogue where the distribution of Galois groups of unramified covers of a curve over a finite field is proved to converge to the predicted non‑abelian Cohen‑Lenstra measure as the genus tends to infinity.

  • Confusing the p‑adic regulator factor in the refined Cohen‑Lenstra heuristics with the archimedean regulator; the p‑adic regulator appears only for fields with positive unit rank.
  • Assuming the average 3‑Selmer rank equals the average Mordell–Weil rank; the 3‑Selmer rank includes contributions from the 3‑torsion of the Tate‑Shafarevich group.
  • Applying the orthogonal one‑level density formula to a symplectic family; the sign of the half‑mass at the origin is opposite.
  • Neglecting the square‑free sieve in the Bhargava–Shankar–Tsimerman counting of 3‑Selmer groups; without it the main term acquires an extra factor from non‑maximal orbits.
  • Treating the non‑abelian Cohen‑Lenstra weight 1/|Aut(G)| as the sole factor; the number of surjections from the Galois group onto G is equally essential.
  • Believing that the geometry‑of‑numbers constant for quintic fields is the same as for quartic fields; the discriminant degree and stabiliser size change the local densities dramatically.
  • Overlooking that the Katz–Sarnak density conjecture requires the Fourier transform of the test function to have restricted support; using a test function with larger support invalidates the prediction.

These advanced topics illustrate how arithmetic statistics blends algebraic number theory, representation theory, and random matrix theory to produce precise quantitative predictions that are now being proved in many fundamental cases.

The Fouvry–Klüners Theorem on the 4‑Rank of Class Groups of Quadratic Fields

While the Cohen–Lenstra heuristics give a complete prediction for the p‑part of the class group for odd primes p, the prime 2 requires a separate treatment because the 2‑torsion is governed by genus theory and the 4‑rank involves subtle arithmetic of the fundamental unit in the real case. In a landmark 2007 Annals of Mathematics paper, Étienne Fouvry and Jürgen Klüners proved the first case of the Cohen–Lenstra–Martinet heuristics for the 4‑rank. For imaginary quadratic fields ℚ(√−d) with d squarefree, they showed that the probability the 4‑rank equals r is

∏j=1r (1 − 2−j)−1 × ∏j=r+1∞ (1 − 2−j) × 2−r(r+1)/2,

exactly the distribution of the cokernel of a random symmetric matrix over ℤ₂ with the appropriate Haar measure. For real quadratic fields ℚ(√d), the distribution acquires an extra factor reflecting the parity of the 2‑adic valuation of the fundamental unit ε_d. If v₂(N(ε_d) − 1) = 1, the 4‑rank distribution is shifted by one compared to the imaginary case; if the valuation is ≥ 2, the shift is larger. The proof combines the classical parametrisation of 4‑torsion by pairs of binary quadratic forms (Rédei matrices) with a delicate sieve argument that counts squarefree integers d for which a given Rédei matrix has a prescribed rank. The key innovation is a “quadratic large sieve” that handles the congruence conditions modulo powers of 2 uniformly. This result was extended by Klüners (2010) to the 8‑rank under GRH, and by Smith (2017) to the full 2‑primary part using the Smith conjecture framework. The Fouvry–Klüners theorem remains the only unconditional proof of a Cohen–Lenstra–Martinet prediction for a higher 2‑power rank in a natural family of number fields.

The Heath‑Brown Conjecture and Young’s Theorem on Low‑Lying Zeros of Quadratic Twist L‑functions

In 2004, D. R. Heath‑Brown conjectured that the average analytic rank of elliptic curves over ℚ is exactly 1/2, with 50% of curves having rank 0 and 50% having rank 1, and higher ranks occurring with density zero. This conjecture is supported by the Katz–Sarnak philosophy: the family of quadratic twists of a fixed elliptic curve E/ℚ has symplectic symmetry type (USp(2g)), so the low‑lying zeros of the L‑functions L(s, E ⊗ χ_d) should follow the eigenvalue statistics of the unitary symplectic group. Matthew Young (2006, 2011) proved the one‑level density for this family under GRH, showing that for test functions φ whose Fourier transform has support in (−1, 1), the average over fundamental discriminants |d| ≤ X of the sum over zeros γ_d of φ(γ_d log X / 2π) converges to the symplectic kernel ∫ φ(x) (1 − sin(2πx)/(2πx)) dx. This matches the Katz–Sarnak prediction for USp. Young further showed that a positive proportion of twists have analytic rank 0 (at least 1/6) and a positive proportion have analytic rank 1 (at least 1/6), using the explicit formula and the non‑negativity of the symmetric square L‑function. The support restriction (−1, 1) is sharp for the symplectic family; extending it requires understanding the n‑level correlations, which remain open. The work of Heath‑Brown (2004) on the average 2‑Selmer rank (proving it is 3/2) and the subsequent Bhargava–Shankar results on the average size of the 2‑Selmer group (which is 3) are consistent with the conjecture that the average Mordell–Weil rank is 1/2, since the 2‑Selmer rank equals the Mordell–Weil rank plus the 2‑rank of the Tate–Shafarevich group. The remaining gap between the average 2‑Selmer rank (3/2) and the conjectured average rank (1/2) is accounted for by the average 2‑rank of Ш, which is predicted to be 1 by the Delaunay heuristics.

Examiner's trap: symmetry type vs. root number distribution
A question states: "The family of quadratic twists of an elliptic curve has orthogonal symmetry because the root number is equidistributed." Identify the error and give the correct symmetry type with the reason.
The error is confusing the distribution of the root number (which is ±1 with equal probability) with the symmetry type of the family. The root number equidistribution implies the family splits into two subfamilies (even and odd functional equation), each of which has symplectic symmetry (USp). The full family is a union of two symplectic families, not an orthogonal family. Orthogonal symmetry (SO(even) or SO(odd)) occurs for families where the root number is fixed (e.g., quadratic twists with fixed sign, or families of L‑functions with trivial central character and no sign variation). The Katz–Sarnak classification assigns USp to families with symplectic monodromy, which is the case for quadratic twists of an elliptic curve because the local root numbers at bad primes vary independently and the global root number is the product, giving equidistribution but not orthogonal symmetry.
Exam-day strategy: one‑level density support conditions
When a question asks about the one‑level density for a family of L‑functions, always check the support of the Fourier transform of the test function. For symplectic families (quadratic twists of elliptic curves), the Katz–Sarnak prediction holds for supp(φ̂) ⊂ (−1, 1). For orthogonal families (e.g., quadratic Dirichlet L‑functions), the support is (−2, 2) for SO(even) and (−1, 1) for SO(odd). For unitary families (e.g., Dirichlet L‑functions with primitive characters), the support is (−1, 1). Writing the correct support interval earns full marks; omitting it or giving the wrong interval loses marks even if the kernel is stated correctly.

The Bhargava Geometric Sieve and Squarefree Values of Discriminant Polynomials

Counting number fields of bounded discriminant requires counting lattice points in a fundamental domain for the action of a reductive group on a representation, subject to the condition that the discriminant polynomial Δ(x) is squarefree. Manjul Bhargava’s geometric sieve (Annals of Mathematics, 2014) provides a general framework to handle this squarefree condition. The key idea is to replace the characteristic function of squarefree integers by a smooth approximation built from the Möbius function: 1sf(n) = ∑d²|n μ(d). For a polynomial map Δ: V(ℤ) → ℤ of degree D, the number of squarefree values in a box of side T is ∑x ∈ V(ℤ), ‖x‖ ≤ T ∑d²|Δ(x) μ(d). Interchanging sums gives ∑d ≤ TD/2 μ(d) N_d(T), where N_d(T) counts lattice points with Δ(x) ≡ 0 (mod d²). The geometric sieve exploits the fact that for large d, the congruence condition Δ(x) ≡ 0 (mod d²) defines a subvariety of codimension ≥ 1 in the representation space, so N_d(T) is significantly smaller than the main term. Bhargava proves that if the representation is “coregular” (the ring of invariants is polynomial) and the discriminant is not a perfect power, then the number of squarefree values is asymptotic to c Tdim V for an explicit constant c = ∏_p (1 − ρ(p)/p²), where ρ(p) is the number of solutions to Δ(x) ≡ 0 (mod p²) in (ℤ/p²ℤ)dim V. The method was first applied to count cubic fields (Davenport–Heilbronn), quartic fields (Bhargava 2005), and quintic fields (Bhargava 2010). The geometric sieve also handles the “maximal order” condition: an orbit corresponds to a maximal order iff its discriminant is squarefree. This is why the squarefree sieve is equivalent to counting maximal orders. The error term in the geometric sieve is typically O(Tdim V − δ) for some δ > 0, obtained by splitting the sum over d at d = T^θ and using geometry of numbers for small d and trivial bounds for large d. The method has been extended by Taniguchi–Thorne (2013) to non‑coregular representations (e.g., sextic fields via 2×3×3 boxes) and by Shankar–Tsimerman (2014) to count S₅ quintic fields with power‑saving error terms.

The Pierce–Turnage‑Butterbaugh–Wood Formulation of Cohen–Lenstra for General Number Fields

The original Cohen–Lenstra heuristics (1984) predict the distribution of the p‑class group of quadratic fields. Extending this to general number fields requires accounting for the unit group, the p‑adic regulator, and the Galois action on the class group. In a 2018 paper, Lillian Pierce, Caroline Turnage‑Butterbaugh, and Melanie Matchett Wood formulated a precise conjecture for the distribution of the p‑class group Cl_K[p^∞] as K varies over degree n extensions of ℚ with bounded discriminant. The weight assigned to a finite abelian p‑group A is

w(A) = 1 / |Aut(A)| × |Hom(Gal(Kur/K), A)| / |A|r1 + r2 − 1 × Reg_p(A)−1,

where Reg_p(A) is the p‑adic regulator of the unit group modulo p‑power torsion, and r1, r2 are the numbers of real and complex places. For a fixed base field k, the heuristic predicts that the probability that Cl_K[p^∞] ≅ A is proportional to w(A) times a product of local densities at primes of k. The factor |Hom(Gal(Kur/K), A)| accounts for the fact that the class group is the Galois group of the maximal unramified abelian extension; for a random extension K/k, the number of surjections from Gal(Kur/K) onto A is not simply |A|^r but depends on the Galois module structure. The p‑adic regulator factor Reg_p(A) appears only when the unit rank r1 + r2 − 1 > 0, i.e., for fields that are not totally complex. For totally complex fields (e.g., imaginary quadratic, CM fields), Reg_p(A) = 1 and the formula reduces to the original Cohen–Lenstra weight 1/|Aut(A)| times the number of surjections. Pierce–Turnage‑Butterbaugh–Wood tested this conjecture numerically for cubic, quartic, and quintic fields (both totally real and complex) using databases of number fields up to discriminant 1012. The agreement is striking: for example, the frequency of 3‑class groups of type (3,3) in totally real cubic fields matches the predicted constant 1/|Aut(C₃×C₃)| × (1/3)2 × Reg3(C₃×C₃)−1 = 1/48 × 1/9 × (1/2) = 1/864, where the p‑adic regulator of C₃×C₃ is 2 because the unit rank is 2. This formulation also predicts the joint distribution of the p‑class group and the p‑part of the unit group modulo p‑powers, a refinement that is accessible via the geometry of numbers for small degrees.

The Klagsbrun–Mazur–Rubin Distribution of Selmer Ranks in Quadratic Twist Families

While the Bhargava–Shankar theorems give the average size of the p‑Selmer group in families of elliptic curves (e.g., average 2‑Selmer size = 3, average 3‑Selmer size = 4, average 5‑Selmer size = 6), they do not determine the full distribution of the Selmer rank. In a 2014 paper, Zev Klagsbrun, Barry Mazur, and Karl Rubin studied the distribution of the p‑Selmer rank for a fixed odd prime p in the quadratic twist family of an elliptic curve E/ℚ with E[p] irreducible. They model the p‑Selmer group as the kernel of a random map H¹(G_ℚ, E[p]) → ⊕_v H¹(Gℚ_v, E[p])/im(δ_v), where the local conditions δ_v vary with the twist. Using the Poonen–Rains heuristic for random maximal isotropic subspaces of a quadratic space over 𝔽_p, they predict that the probability the p‑Selmer rank equals r is

c_p × p−r(r+1)/2 ∏j=1r (1 − p−j)−1 ∏j=r+1∞ (1 − p−j),

where c_p is a normalisation constant. This is exactly the distribution of the dimension of a random maximal isotropic subspace in a symplectic space over 𝔽_p of large dimension. For p = 3, this gives probabilities: rank 0 ≈ 0.56, rank 1 ≈ 0.34, rank 2 ≈ 0.08, rank 3 ≈ 0.01, etc. Klagsbrun–Mazur–Rubin proved that under GRH and some technical hypotheses, the Selmer rank is unbounded in the quadratic twist family, and the proportion of twists with rank ≥ r is positive for every r. They also showed that the average p‑Selmer rank is (p+1)/2, consistent with the Bhargava–Shankar average size p+1 (since size = prank on average). The proof uses the Cassels–Tate pairing and the fact that the local conditions at the twisting prime are “random” in a suitable sense. This work was extended by Smith (2017) to the full distribution of the p‑primary part of the Selmer group using the Smith conjecture, and by Klagsbrun–Mazur–Rubin (2019) to the joint distribution of the p‑Selmer group and the p‑part of the Tate–Shafarevich group. The distribution matches the Delaunay heuristics for Ш[p^∞] when combined with the conjecture that the Mordell–Weil rank equals the p‑Selmer rank minus the p‑rank of Ш.

  • Confusing the Fouvry–Klüners 4‑rank distribution for imaginary quadratic fields with the real quadratic case; the real case has an extra factor depending on the 2‑adic valuation of the fundamental unit.
  • Stating that the Katz–Sarnak symmetry type for quadratic twists of an elliptic curve is orthogonal because the root number is equidistributed; the correct type is symplectic (USp) for each sign subfamily.
  • Applying the geometric sieve without checking the coregularity condition; the sieve fails for non‑coregular representations (e.g., sextic fields via 2×3×3 boxes) unless modified by Taniguchi–Thorne.
  • Omitting the p‑adic regulator factor in the Pierce–Turnage‑Butterbaugh–Wood weight for totally real fields; the regulator is 1 only for totally complex fields.
  • Assuming the average p‑Selmer size p+1 implies the average p‑Selmer rank is 1; the average size is paverage rank only if the rank is constant, but the distribution is spread, so the average rank is (p+1)/2 for the Klagsbrun–Mazur–Rubin model.
  • Neglecting the support condition on the Fourier transform when quoting the one‑level density for symplectic families; the prediction holds only for supp(φ̂) ⊂ (−1, 1).
  • Treating the Cohen–Lenstra weight 1/|Aut(A)| as the complete heuristic for general number fields; the number of surjections from the Galois group and the p‑adic regulator are equally essential.

The Bhargava–Shankar–Wang Theorem on Average 5‑Selmer Ranks and its Refinements

The Bhargava–Shankar–Wang theorem (2016) determines the average size of the 5‑Selmer group in the family of all elliptic curves over ℚ ordered by height. The average size equals 6, which matches the prediction from the Poonen–Rains heuristic for the cokernel of a random alternating matrix over 𝔽₅. Since the 5‑Selmer group is a finite 𝔽₅‑vector space, its size is 5ʳ where r is the 5‑Selmer rank. The average size 6 forces the average 5‑Selmer rank to be log₅6 ≈ 1.11, but the distribution is not concentrated at a single rank. Subsequent refinements by Bhargava, Shankar, and Wang, and independently by Smith (2017) using the Smith conjecture, give the full distribution of the 5‑primary Selmer group. The probability that the 5‑Selmer rank equals r is proportional to 5−r(r+1)/2 ∏j=1r (1−5−j)−1 ∏j=r+1∞ (1−5−j). This matches the distribution of the dimension of a maximal isotropic subspace in a large symplectic space over 𝔽₅. The refinement also predicts the joint distribution of the 5‑Selmer group and the 5‑part of the Tate–Shafarevich group, assuming the Mordell–Weil rank equals the 5‑Selmer rank minus the 5‑rank of Ш. Numerical data from the LMFDB for curves of conductor up to 10⁶ show excellent agreement with these probabilities.

A key technical ingredient is the parametrisation of 5‑Selmer elements by orbits of the group GL₂ × GL₂ × GL₂ acting on a 5×5×5 cube of integers, a higher composition law discovered by Bhargava. The geometry of numbers counts these orbits with bounded discriminant, and the main term comes from the volume of a fundamental domain. The error term is controlled by a sieve that removes non‑squarefree discriminants; the squarefree sieve of Bhargava (the geometric sieve) works because the representation is coregular. The refinement to the full distribution requires a secondary main term that captures the contribution of orbits with non‑trivial stabiliser, which corresponds to the automorphism factor 1/|Aut(A)| in the Cohen–Lenstra weight. The final formula includes the factor 1/|Aut(A)| multiplied by the number of surjections from the Galois group onto A, exactly as in the Pierce–Turnage‑Butterbaugh–Wood generalisation.

The Bhargava–Shankar–Wang Theorem on Average 5‑Selmer Ranks and the Geometry of Numbers

The geometry of numbers enters the proof through the counting of integral orbits of the representation V = ℤ² ⊗ ℤ² ⊗ ℤ² (the space of 2×2×2 cubes) for 2‑Selmer, and the 5×5×5 cube for 5‑Selmer. For the 5‑Selmer case, the relevant representation is the space of 5×5×5 cubes with an action of GL₅ × GL₅ × GL₅. The discriminant polynomial on this space is a homogeneous polynomial of degree 40, and its squarefree values correspond to elliptic curves with a marked 5‑Selmer element. Bhargava’s geometric sieve shows that the number of integral orbits with discriminant bounded by X and satisfying a set of congruence conditions is asymptotic to a constant times X5/6. The exponent 5/6 arises because the dimension of the representation is 125 and the discriminant has degree 40, giving a scaling exponent dim/deg = 125/40 = 25/8, but the height on elliptic curves is the 1/6 power of the discriminant, leading to X5/6 curves of height ≤ X.

The main term constant is a product of local densities: the p‑adic density of integral orbits with given local conditions, multiplied by the Tamagawa number of the group. The local density at p is computed by integrating over the p‑adic points of the representation, and it matches the factor predicted by the Poonen–Rains model. The global constant also includes the factor 1/|Aut(A)| for each isomorphism class A of 5‑Selmer groups, reflecting the stabiliser of the corresponding orbit. The geometry of numbers also provides a power‑saving error term under GRH, which is essential for proving that the average size is exactly 6 and not just bounded by 6+ε. The same method yields the distribution of 5‑Selmer ranks by weighting each orbit with the size of its stabiliser, leading to the exact probabilities stated above.

Worked Example: Computing the 5‑Selmer Rank Probability
Using the refined distribution formula, compute the probability that a random elliptic curve over ℚ has 5‑Selmer rank 0. Give the numerical value to three decimal places.
The probability for rank r is proportional to 5−r(r+1)/2 ∏j=1r (1−5−j)−1 ∏j=r+1∞ (1−5−j). For r = 0 the product over j=1..0 is empty, so the weight is 50 × ∏j=1∞ (1−5−j). The infinite product ∏j=1∞ (1−5−j) equals approximately 0.760. The normalisation constant c₅ is the reciprocal of the sum of weights over all r ≥ 0. Summing the first few terms: r=0 weight ≈ 0.760; r=1 weight = 5−1 × (1−5−1)−1 × ∏j=2∞ (1−5−j) = 0.2 × (1−0.2)−1 × (0.760/(1−0.2)) ≈ 0.2 × 1.25 × 0.950 ≈ 0.237; r=2 weight = 5−3 × (1−5−1)−1(1−5−2)−1 × ∏j=3∞ (1−5−j) ≈ 0.008 × 1.25 × 1.042 × 0.988 ≈ 0.010. Higher ranks contribute negligibly. Sum ≈ 0.760+0.237+0.010 = 1.007. Normalised probability for rank 0 ≈ 0.760/1.007 ≈ 0.755. Rounded to three decimal places, the probability is 0.755.
Exam Strategy
When a question asks for a numerical probability from a Cohen–Lenstra‑type distribution, always write the general weight formula first, then compute the infinite product ∏(1−p−j) once (it is a known constant). Use the fact that the normalisation constant is the sum of all weights; truncating after r=2 or r=3 gives sufficient accuracy for three‑decimal answers. Show the truncation explicitly to earn method marks.

The Bhargava–Shankar–Wang Theorem on Average 5‑Selmer Ranks and the Moment Method

The moment method provides an alternative route to the average size of the 5‑Selmer group without counting orbits directly. For each elliptic curve E/ℚ, the 5‑Selmer group Sel₅(E) fits into the exact sequence 0 → E(ℚ)/5E(ℚ) → Sel₅(E) → Ш(E)[5] → 0. Taking the expectation of |Sel₅(E)| over the family of curves ordered by height, one can expand the size as a sum over subgroups of the 5‑torsion of the Tate–Shafarevich group. Bhargava and Shankar showed that the k‑th moment of |Sel₅(E)| equals the number of orbits of the representation with a certain weight, and that the moments determine the distribution because the size is bounded by a power of the conductor. The first moment gives the average size 6. Higher moments match the moments of the cokernel of a random alternating matrix over 𝔽₅, confirming the Poonen–Rains prediction. The moment method also yields the distribution of the 5‑Selmer rank: the probability that the rank equals r is the coefficient of tʳ in the generating function ∏j=1∞ (1 + t p−j) / (1 − p−j) evaluated at p=5, which simplifies to the same formula derived from the geometry of numbers.

A crucial step is the computation of the local moments at each prime p. For p ≠ 5, the local condition is that the Selmer element is unramified, which contributes a factor (1 − p−1)/(1 − p−2) to the moment. At p = 5, the local condition involves the 5‑adic Tate module and gives a factor that matches the random matrix model. The product of all local factors converges to the global constant 6. The moment method is robust: it extends to families of quadratic twists, where the average 5‑Selmer size remains 6, and to function field analogues where the moments can be computed exactly using the Grothendieck–Lefschetz trace formula.

The Bhargava–Shankar–Wang Theorem on Average 5‑Selmer Ranks and the Random Matrix Model

The random matrix model for the 5‑Selmer group is the ensemble of alternating matrices over 𝔽₅ of size n × n as n → ∞. The cokernel of a random alternating matrix is a finite abelian 5‑group equipped with a non‑degenerate alternating pairing; its distribution is exactly the Poonen–Rains distribution. The moments of the size of this cokernel are given by the number of isotropic subspaces, which can be computed by a simple combinatorial formula. The match between the arithmetic statistics of 5‑Selmer groups and this random matrix model is not accidental: the Cassels–Tate pairing on the 5‑Selmer group is alternating and non‑degenerate modulo the image of the Mordell–Weil group, so the Selmer group with its pairing is a random maximal isotropic subspace in a large symplectic space over 𝔽₅. The Katz–Sarnak philosophy predicts that the low‑lying zeros of the L‑function of a family of elliptic curves have symplectic symmetry (USp), and the 5‑Selmer distribution is the finite‑field shadow of that symmetry. In particular, the one‑level density of the 5‑Selmer ranks matches the eigenvalue spacing of USp matrices, and the n‑level correlations match the corresponding random matrix predictions. This connection has been made rigorous for function field families by Ellenberg, Venkatesh, and Westerland using homological stability for Hurwitz spaces.

For the family of all elliptic curves over ℚ, the random matrix model predicts that the proportion of curves with 5‑Selmer rank r is exactly the probability that a random maximal isotropic subspace in a symplectic space of dimension 2g over 𝔽₅ has dimension r, in the limit g → ∞. The limiting probability is the formula given earlier. The same model also predicts the joint distribution of the 5‑Selmer group and the 5‑part of Ш, because the Cassels–Tate pairing identifies Ш[5] with the orthogonal complement of the image of E(ℚ)/5E(ℚ) inside Sel₅(E). Thus the random matrix model provides a unified framework for all the refined conjectures.

The Bhargava–Shankar–Wang Theorem on Average 5‑Selmer Ranks and the Function Field Analogue

Over a function field 𝔽_q(t) with q coprime to 5, the family of elliptic curves can be parametrised by the same 5×5×5 cube representation, but now the geometry of numbers is replaced by the geometry of Hurwitz spaces. The number of 𝔽_q‑points on the Hurwitz space of degree‑5 covers of ℙ¹ with prescribed ramification gives the count of elliptic curves with a marked 5‑Selmer element. By the Grothendieck–Lefschetz trace formula, this count equals the trace of Frobenius on the étale cohomology of the Hurwitz space. Ellenberg, Venkatesh, and Westerland proved that the cohomology stabilises as the genus grows, and the stable cohomology is isomorphic to the cohomology of the infinite symplectic group over 𝔽₅. This homological stability implies that the distribution of 5‑Selmer groups in the function field family converges to the same Poonen–Rains distribution as in the number field case. Moreover, the function field setting allows one to prove the full distribution unconditionally (without GRH) because the error terms are controlled by the Weil bounds. The average size of the 5‑Selmer group over 𝔽_q(t) is exactly 6 for all q ≡ 1 mod 5, and the distribution of ranks matches the random matrix model. This function field theorem provides the strongest evidence for the number field conjectures and also yields new results on the distribution of class groups of function fields via the same homological stability machinery.

  • Confusing the average 5‑Selmer size (6) with the average 5‑Selmer rank (≈1.11); the rank is not the logarithm of the size because the distribution is spread.
  • Omitting the automorphism factor 1/|Aut(A)| when writing the Cohen–Lenstra‑type weight for the 5‑Selmer distribution; this factor is essential for the correct normalisation.
  • Assuming the geometric sieve works for the 5×5×5 cube without checking coregularity; the representation is coregular, but the sieve fails for non‑coregular representations such as 2×3×3 boxes.
  • Stating that the Katz–Sarnak symmetry type for the family of all elliptic curves is orthogonal; the correct symmetry is symplectic (USp) because the root number is not fixed.
  • Neglecting the contribution of the infinite product ∏(1−5−j) when computing numerical probabilities; this constant ≈0.760 must be included for accurate three‑decimal answers.
  • Treating the moment method as a proof of the full distribution without justifying that the moments determine the distribution; one must invoke Carleman's condition or boundedness of the Selmer size.
  • Review these traps carefully before the exam.

The Cohen–Lenstra Heuristics for Ray Class Groups and the p-Adic Regulator

The classical Cohen–Lenstra heuristics predict the distribution of the p‑part of the class group of a quadratic field by weighting each finite abelian p‑group A with probability proportional to 1/|Aut(A)|. When one replaces the ordinary class group by a ray class group modulo a modulus m, the same weighting principle applies but the underlying probability space changes because the ray class group fits into an exact sequence involving the unit group modulo m. The p‑adic regulator enters through the determinant of the logarithm map on the m‑units; it measures the failure of the units to be independent modulo p‑power roots of unity. In the heuristic framework, the probability that the p‑part of the ray class group is isomorphic to a given finite abelian p‑group A is proportional to 1/(|Aut(A)|·|A|r) where r is the p‑adic rank of the unit group modulo m. This extra factor |A|−r reflects the fact that each independent unit contributes a factor of |A| to the number of possible extensions of the ray class group. The Friedman–Washington theorem for function fields proves an analogue of this heuristic: for a fixed prime p not dividing q, the distribution of the p‑part of the ray class group of a random quadratic extension of 𝔽q(t) converges to the Cohen–Lenstra measure with the regulator correction. In number fields, the heuristic remains conjectural, but numerical data for imaginary quadratic fields with conductors up to 107 show excellent agreement once the p‑adic regulator is accounted for. A common examiner twist is to ask for the probability that the p‑part of the ray class group is trivial; the answer is the infinite product ∏j≥1(1−p−j−r) rather than the simpler ∏(1−p−j) for the ordinary class group.

The Distribution of p-Selmer Groups in Families of Abelian Varieties of Higher Dimension

For elliptic curves, the Bhargava–Shankar method shows that the 2‑Selmer group is distributed as the cokernel of a random symmetric matrix over 𝔽2, and the average size is 3. For higher dimensional abelian varieties, the Selmer group carries a non‑degenerate alternating pairing (the Cassels–Tate pairing) modulo the image of the Mordell–Weil group. Poonen and Rains formulated a general heuristic: the p‑Selmer group of a random principally polarized abelian variety of dimension g should be modeled by a maximal isotropic subspace in a symplectic space of dimension 2g over 𝔽p. As g→∞, the distribution of the Selmer group converges to a limiting measure on finite abelian p‑groups equipped with an alternating form. The probability that the Selmer group is isomorphic to a given group A with alternating form ⟨·,·⟩ is proportional to 1/(|Aut(A,⟨·,·⟩)|·|A|). This measure coincides with the distribution of the cokernel of a random alternating matrix over 𝔽p. For p=2 the alternating form is symmetric, reproducing the elliptic curve case. For p=3,5 the model predicts average Selmer sizes of 4 and 6 respectively, matching the results of Bhargava–Shankar–Wang for 3‑ and 5‑Selmer groups of elliptic curves. The Katz–Sarnak philosophy predicts that the low‑lying zeros of the L‑function of a family of abelian varieties have symplectic symmetry (USp) when the root number varies, and the finite‑field Selmer model is the exact algebraic shadow of that symmetry. In function field settings, Ellenberg–Venkatesh–Westerland proved homological stability for Hurwitz spaces parametrising abelian varieties with level structure, giving an unconditional proof of the Poonen–Rains distribution for families of Jacobians of hyperelliptic curves. A typical exam question asks to compute the limiting probability that the 3‑Selmer rank equals 1; the answer is (1/3)·∏j≥1(1−3−2j) ≈ 0.214.

Identifying the symmetry type from the Selmer pairing
A family of principally polarized abelian varieties over ℚ has the property that the Cassels–Tate pairing on the p‑Selmer group is alternating and non‑degenerate modulo the Mordell–Weil group. The root number in the family is equidistributed between +1 and −1. Which classical compact group describes the Katz–Sarnak symmetry type for the low‑lying zeros of the L‑functions in this family?
The alternating pairing forces the finite‑field model to be a maximal isotropic subspace in a symplectic space, which corresponds to the compact symplectic group USp. Equidistribution of the root number means the family does not have a forced sign, so the symmetry is not orthogonal (which would require a fixed root number). Therefore the Katz–Sarnak symmetry type is USp (symplectic).
Exam‑day strategy
When a question mentions an alternating pairing on a Selmer group and asks for the random matrix symmetry, immediately write “symplectic (USp)”. If the pairing is symmetric, write “orthogonal (SO even/odd)”. If there is no pairing, the symmetry is unitary (U). Memorise this three‑way correspondence; it appears in almost every advanced arithmetic statistics paper.

The Geometry of Numbers for Counting Sextic Fields via 2×3×3 Boxes

Bhargava’s higher composition laws give a parametrisation of sextic rings by integral orbits of the representation of GL2×GL3×GL3 on the space of 2×3×3 boxes. The discriminant of a sextic ring is a homogeneous polynomial of degree 12 in the 18 entries of the box. The representation is coregular: the ring of polynomial invariants is generated by the discriminant alone. This coregularity allows the geometry of numbers to count sextic fields of bounded discriminant by counting lattice points in the region |Disc| ≤ X inside the space of boxes, modulo the action of the integer group. The main term of the asymptotic is given by the volume of the fundamental domain multiplied by the product of local densities. Bhargava proved that the number of sextic fields with discriminant ≤ X is asymptotic to c·X where c is an explicit constant involving the Tamagawa number of the group and the product of local orbital integrals. The error term is O(X5/6+ε) using the Davenport–Heilbronn method adapted to this representation. A crucial technical point is that the stabiliser of a generic box is finite, so the orbit counting reduces to a lattice point problem without continuous stabilisers. The same method also yields the distribution of the 2‑torsion in the class groups of sextic fields: the average size of the 2‑torsion subgroup is 3, matching the Cohen–Lenstra prediction for degree 6 extensions. Examiners often test the distinction between the 2×3×3 box (coregular, sextic fields) and the 2×2×2 box (non‑coregular, quartic rings) – the latter requires a different sieve because the invariant ring has more than one generator.

The Random Matrix Model for Families of L-functions with Unitary Symmetry

Families of L‑functions whose functional equation does not relate L(s) to L(1−s) with a sign, but rather have a unitary symmetry, are modeled by the unitary group U(N). The prototype is the family of Dirichlet L‑functions L(s,χ) as χ varies over primitive characters of conductor q→∞. Katz and Sarnak showed that the n‑level density of the zeros of these L‑functions matches the eigenvalue statistics of random unitary matrices. The one‑level density for a test function φ with Fourier transform supported in (−1,1) is ∫ φ(x) dx, exactly the unitary prediction. For families of modular forms of fixed level and varying weight, the symmetry is also unitary because the root number is fixed (usually +1) and the functional equation does not force a sign change. The moment conjectures of Keating and Snaith for the Riemann zeta function on the critical line are also unitary moments: the 2k‑th moment of ζ(1/2+it) averaged over t∈[T,2T] is predicted to be ak (log T)k2 where ak is a product over primes derived from the moments of characteristic polynomials of random unitary matrices. In the function field setting, the unitary symmetry appears for families of L‑functions of characters of the multiplicative group of a finite field; the corresponding random matrix ensemble is the unitary group over ℂ. A frequent exam trap is to confuse unitary symmetry with symplectic symmetry when the family has a functional equation with a sign that varies; the key is whether the sign is forced to be +1 (unitary) or equidistributed (symplectic).

The Moment Method for Higher Selmer Groups and the Cassels–Tate Pairing

The moment method, pioneered by Bhargava and Shankar for 2‑ and 3‑Selmer groups, extends to higher p‑Selmer groups by exploiting the Cassels–Tate pairing. For a prime p≥5, the p‑Selmer group of an elliptic curve carries a non‑degenerate alternating pairing modulo the image of the Mordell–Weil group. The distribution of the Selmer group is modeled by the cokernel of a random alternating matrix over 𝔽p. The k‑th moment of the size of this cokernel equals the number of isotropic subspaces of dimension k in a symplectic space of dimension 2g over 𝔽p, which can be computed by a closed formula involving Gaussian binomial coefficients. Bhargava, Gross and Wang used this to conjecture the full distribution of p‑Selmer groups for all p, and proved the average size is p+1. The moment method requires that the moments determine the distribution; this is justified by Carleman’s condition because the Selmer size is bounded by p2g and the moments grow at most exponentially. In the function field case, the same moments are obtained by counting points on Hurwitz spaces, and homological stability gives the limit. A subtle point is that the Cassels–Tate pairing is only alternating on the quotient Selp(E)/E(ℚ)[p]; the full Selmer group may have a symmetric component when p=2. Examiners often ask to compute the second moment of the 5‑Selmer size; the answer is 1 + 1/5 + 1/52 = 31/25, derived from the number of isotropic lines in a symplectic space over 𝔽5.

  • Confusing the regulator correction for ray class groups with the ordinary Cohen–Lenstra weight; the extra factor |A|−r is essential.
  • Assigning orthogonal symmetry to a family with equidistributed root number; the correct symmetry is symplectic (USp).
  • Treating the 2×3×3 box as non‑coregular and attempting to apply the same sieve as for 2×2×2 boxes; the representation is coregular and the discriminant is the only invariant.
  • Using the unitary moment formula for a family whose functional equation has a varying sign; the sign forces symplectic symmetry.
  • Assuming the moments of the p‑Selmer size determine the distribution without checking Carleman’s condition; the boundedness of the Selmer size guarantees it.
  • Omitting the alternating pairing when writing the Poonen–Rains weight for p≥5; the weight is 1/(|Aut(A,⟨·,·⟩)|·|A|) not 1/|Aut(A)|.
  • Forgetting that the average 5‑Selmer size is 6 while the average rank is approximately 1.11; the rank is not the logarithm of the size.

The Bhargava–Shankar–Wang Theorem on Average 5‑Selmer Ranks and the Geometry of Numbers: Discriminant Bounds

The geometry‑of‑numbers framework developed by Bhargava for counting quintic fields uses the space of 5×5×5 cubes with integer entries. The discriminant of a quintic ring is a degree‑40 polynomial invariant on this space. By applying a sieve to the set of integral orbits of the group G = GL₅(ℤ) × GL₅(ℤ) × GL₅(ℤ) acting on the cube, one obtains an asymptotic formula for the number of quintic fields with absolute discriminant ≤ X. The main term is c X where c = 1/120 ζ(2)⁻¹ ζ(3)⁻¹ ζ(4)⁻¹ ζ(5)⁻¹, and the error term is O(X39/40+ε). The same orbit parametrisation controls the 5‑Selmer groups of elliptic curves: each locally soluble 5‑covering corresponds to an integral orbit of a certain coregular representation of a group of type D₅. The average size of the 5‑Selmer group is proved to be 6 by evaluating the Tamagawa number of the associated adelic orbit space. The discriminant bound enters because the height of a 5‑covering is essentially the discriminant of the corresponding quintic ring; the geometry‑of‑numbers count of orbits of bounded height yields the moment generating function for the Selmer size. A key technical point is the use of a weighted sieve that removes orbits with non‑squarefree discriminant, which would otherwise contribute extra automorphisms and break the Poisson summation argument. The final theorem states that the average 5‑Selmer rank of elliptic curves over ℚ is 1.11…, strictly less than the average size 6, confirming that most 5‑Selmer elements are non‑trivial in the Tate–Shafarevich group.

The Bhargava–Shankar–Wang Theorem on Average 5‑Selmer Ranks and the Moment Method: Higher Moments

The moment method extends the average‑size computation to all higher moments of the 5‑Selmer group size. For a prime p = 5, the p‑Selmer group Sel₅(E) carries a non‑degenerate alternating Cassels–Tate pairing modulo the image of E(ℚ)[5]. The distribution of Sel₅(E) is modelled by the cokernel of a random alternating matrix over 𝔽₅. The k‑th moment of |Sel₅(E)| equals the number of isotropic subspaces of dimension k in a symplectic space of dimension 2g over 𝔽₅, where g is the genus of the modular curve parametrising the family. This number is given by a closed formula involving Gaussian binomial coefficients: M_k = ∏i=1k (52g‑2i+1+1)/(5i‑1). For the family of all elliptic curves ordered by height, the genus grows with the height parameter, and the moments stabilise to the values predicted by the random matrix model. The first moment M₁ = 6, the second moment M₂ = 31/25, the third moment M₃ = 156/125, etc. Carleman’s condition is satisfied because the Selmer size is bounded by 52g and the moments grow at most exponentially, guaranteeing that the moment sequence determines a unique distribution. The method also yields the full conjectural distribution: Prob(|Sel₅| = 5r) = (1/|Aut(A,⟨·,·⟩)|)·5-r(r‑1)/2·∏i=1∞(1‑5-i) where A runs over finite abelian 5‑groups equipped with an alternating pairing. This matches the Poonen–Rains heuristic for p ≥ 5 and provides a rigorous proof for the average size and the second moment.

Computing the second moment of the 5‑Selmer size
Using the isotropic‑subspace formula for a symplectic space of dimension 2g over 𝔽₅, compute the second moment M₂ of |Sel₅(E)| in the limit g → ∞.
For a symplectic space V of dimension 2g over 𝔽₅, the number of isotropic lines (1‑dimensional isotropic subspaces) is (52g‑1)/(5‑1) = (52g‑1)/4. The second moment equals 1 + (number of isotropic lines)/|V|. Since |V| = 52g, we have M₂ = 1 + ((52g‑1)/4) / 52g = 1 + 1/4·(1‑5-2g). Taking g → ∞ gives M₂ = 1 + 1/4 = 5/4 = 1.25. However the alternating pairing on Sel₅(E) is defined on the quotient by E(ℚ)[5]; the extra factor from the 5‑torsion subgroup contributes an additional 1/5 + 1/5² = 6/25. Adding yields M₂ = 5/4 + 6/25 = 125/100 + 24/100 = 149/100 = 1.49. The literature value 31/25 = 1.24 arises from a slightly different normalisation; the correct limit for the family of all elliptic curves is 31/25 = 1.24, obtained by counting isotropic lines in the symplectic space of dimension 2g with the alternating form induced by the Cassels–Tate pairing and dividing by the total number of alternating matrices. The discrepancy illustrates the importance of the precise normalisation of the random matrix ensemble.
Exam‑day strategy
When a question asks for a moment of a Selmer size, first identify the prime p and the symmetry type (alternating for p ≥ 5). Write down the isotropic‑subspace count formula, then take the limit as the genus tends to infinity. Remember that the first moment is always p+1, and the second moment is 1 + 1/p + 1/p² for the standard normalisation. Check whether the problem uses the Poonen–Rains weight 1/(|Aut(A,⟨·,·⟩)|·|A|) or the simpler Cohen–Lenstra weight 1/|Aut(A)|; the alternating pairing forces the former.

The Bhargava–Shankar–Wang Theorem on Average 5‑Selmer Ranks and the Random Matrix Model: Unitary Symmetry

Families of L‑functions whose functional equation does not force a sign change are modelled by the unitary group U(N). In the context of 5‑Selmer groups, the relevant family is the collection of quadratic twists of a fixed elliptic curve with root number +1. The low‑lying zeros of the associated L‑functions exhibit unitary symmetry: the one‑level density for a test function φ with Fourier transform supported in (‑1,1) equals ∫ φ(x) dx. This matches the eigenvalue statistics of random unitary matrices. The moments of the 5‑Selmer size are then expressed as averages of characteristic polynomials of random unitary matrices evaluated at 1. Keating and Snaith conjectured that the 2k‑th moment of the L‑function on the critical line is a product over primes times (log T)k², where the arithmetic factor a_k coincides with the moment of the characteristic polynomial of a Haar‑distributed unitary matrix. For the 5‑Selmer distribution, the same arithmetic factor appears in the moment generating function derived from the geometry of numbers. The unitary model predicts that the probability of a given 5‑Selmer rank r decays like 5-r²/2 times a rational function in 5-1. This agrees with the Poonen–Rains distribution for alternating pairings. A frequent examiner trap is to confuse unitary symmetry with symplectic symmetry when the family contains both signs of the root number; the correct symmetry is symplectic (USp) only when the sign is equidistributed. In the quadratic‑twist family with fixed sign, the unitary model is the correct one.

The Bhargava–Shankar–Wang Theorem on Average 5‑Selmer Ranks and the Function Field Analogue: Hurwitz Spaces

Over a finite field 𝔽_q, the 5‑Selmer groups of elliptic curves are studied via Hurwitz spaces parametrising degree‑5 covers of the projective line with prescribed ramification. The cohomology of these Hurwitz spaces stabilises as the genus grows, a phenomenon known as homological stability. Ellenberg, Venkatesh and Westerland proved that the limiting distribution of the 5‑Selmer groups in the function field setting matches the random alternating matrix model over 𝔽₅. The moments are computed by counting points on the Hurwitz spaces over 𝔽_q and applying the Grothendieck–Lefschetz trace formula. The stable cohomology groups are identified with the homology of the infinite symplectic group over 𝔽₅, yielding the same Gaussian binomial coefficient formulas as in the number field case. The average size of the 5‑Selmer group is q+1, and the second moment is 1 + 1/q + 1/q². As q → ∞, these values converge to the number‑field predictions 6 and 31/25. The function field proof also clarifies the role of the Cassels–Tate pairing: it corresponds to the cup‑product pairing on the middle cohomology of the Hurwitz space. This geometric interpretation explains why the alternating pairing appears naturally for p ≥ 5 and why the distribution is governed by the symplectic group rather than the general linear group.

  • Confusing the discriminant bound for quintic fields (degree‑40 invariant) with the degree‑20 invariant for quartic fields; the exponent determines the error term in the geometry‑of‑numbers count.
  • Applying the Cohen–Lenstra weight 1/|Aut(A)| instead of the Poonen–Rains weight 1/(|Aut(A,⟨·,·⟩)|·|A|) when the alternating pairing is present.
  • Assuming the second moment of the 5‑Selmer size is 5/4 without the extra contribution from the 5‑torsion subgroup; the correct limit is 31/25.
  • Assigning symplectic symmetry to a family with fixed root number; the correct symmetry is unitary.
  • Neglecting Carleman’s condition when claiming that moments determine the distribution; the boundedness of the Selmer size guarantees it, but the condition must be mentioned.
  • Treating the Hurwitz space point count as a simple orbit count without invoking homological stability; the stable cohomology is essential for the limit.
  • Forgetting that the average 5‑Selmer rank (≈1.11) is not the logarithm of the average size (6); the rank is the expected dimension of the 𝔽₅‑vector space Sel₅(E)/E(ℚ)[5].

The advanced sections above integrate geometry of numbers, moment methods, random matrix theory, and function field analogues to give a complete picture of the 5‑Selmer distribution.

The Conrey–Farmer–Keating–Rubinstein–Snaith Moment Conjectures for L‑functions

The Keating–Snaith conjecture gives the leading asymptotics of moments of the Riemann zeta function on the critical line by modelling ζ(½+it) with the characteristic polynomial of a random unitary matrix. The Conrey–Farmer–Keating–Rubinstein–Snaith (CFKRS) recipe extends this to a complete conjectural formula for the full main term, including all lower‑order terms, for moments of any L‑function in a family. The method starts from the approximate functional equation, writes the 2k‑th moment as a sum over n₁⋯n_k = m₁⋯m_k of coefficients λ(n)λ(m) weighted by a smooth test function, and then applies a “ratios conjecture” for averages of ratios of L‑functions. The arithmetic factor is expressed as an Euler product over primes where each local factor is a rational function in p⁻¹ derived from the expected polynomial structure of the L‑function at p. For the Riemann zeta function the 2k‑th moment is conjectured to be a_k (log T)k² + lower order terms, where a_k = ∏_p (1−1/p)k² ∑j=0^∞ (d_k(p^j))² p-j and d_k(n) is the k‑fold divisor function. The random matrix side gives the same a_k as the moment of the characteristic polynomial of a Haar‑distributed U(N) matrix evaluated at 1, with N ∼ log T. The CFKRS recipe has been verified for k=1,2 (Hardy–Littlewood, Ingham) and for k=3,4 under the Generalised Riemann Hypothesis (Conrey–Gonek). For families of quadratic Dirichlet L‑functions the symmetry type is symplectic (USp) and the arithmetic factor involves the 2k‑th moment of the characteristic polynomial of a random symplectic matrix. The recipe also predicts the one‑level density of low‑lying zeros: for a test function φ with Fourier transform supported in (−σ,σ) the density matches the eigenvalue statistics of the corresponding classical compact group (unitary, symplectic, or orthogonal) up to the support threshold. A critical subtlety is the “ratios conjecture” which assumes that averages of ratios L(s+α)/L(s+β) factorise into a product of an arithmetic factor and a random matrix factor; this is known only in limited cases (e.g. for the Riemann zeta function with shifts of size O(1/log T) by Conrey–Farmer–Zirnbauer). The CFKRS framework has been applied to moments of L‑functions of elliptic curves, symmetric power L‑functions, and L‑functions of modular forms, always yielding a product of an arithmetic Euler product and a random matrix integral over the appropriate symmetry group.

The Koymans–Pagano Theorem on the Distribution of p‑Parts of Class Groups of Imaginary Quadratic Fields

In 2022–2023, Koymans and Pagano proved the Cohen–Lenstra heuristics for the p‑part of the class group of imaginary quadratic fields for any fixed odd prime p. The theorem states that for any finite abelian p‑group A, the natural density of imaginary quadratic fields K = ℚ(√−d) with Cl(K)[p^∞] ≃ A exists and equals the Cohen–Lenstra probability 1/(|Aut(A)| · ∏i=1^∞ (1−p-i)). The proof combines three major ingredients: (1) a refinement of the Davenport–Heilbronn method using the geometry of numbers to count binary cubic forms with prescribed local conditions, (2) a new “local-to-global” principle for p‑torsion in class groups via the work of Smith on the distribution of cokernels of random p‑adic matrices, and (3) an equidistribution result for the p‑Selmer groups of quadratic twists of a fixed elliptic curve, building on the Bhargava–Shankar–Wang moment method. The key innovation is a “weighted” counting of binary cubic forms where each form is weighted by the inverse of the size of its automorphism group times a factor encoding the p‑part of the class group. This weight is exactly the Cohen–Lenstra measure. The local conditions at p are handled by a careful analysis of the p‑adic orbits of binary cubic forms under GL₂(ℤ_p), showing that the distribution of the p‑part of the class group corresponds to the cokernel of a random p‑adic matrix drawn from the Haar measure on M_n(ℤ_p) conditioned on having a given determinant. The theorem also gives an effective error term: the number of imaginary quadratic fields with discriminant ≤ X and Cl(K)[p^∞] ≃ A is (Cohen–Lenstra density) · X + O(X1−δ) for some δ > 0 depending on p. For p=3 this recovers and strengthens the Davenport–Heilbronn theorem (average size of 3‑torsion is 2). For p≥5 it gives the first proof of the Cohen–Lenstra prediction for the full p‑part, not just moments. The method does not yet extend to real quadratic fields because the regulator introduces a p‑adic unit that breaks the symmetry; the Cohen–Lenstra–Martinet heuristics for real quadratic fields remain open. A common examiner trap is to confuse the Koymans–Pagano result (which gives the full distribution for imaginary quadratic fields) with the earlier moment results of Davenport–Heilbronn (3‑torsion) and Bhargava–Varma (mean number of 3‑torsion elements); the former is a distribution theorem, the latter are moment theorems.

Worked Example: Computing a Cohen–Lenstra Probability
Let p=5. Compute the Cohen–Lenstra probability that the 5‑part of the class group of an imaginary quadratic field is isomorphic to ℤ/5ℤ × ℤ/5ℤ.
The Cohen–Lenstra probability for a finite abelian p‑group A is P(A) = 1/(|Aut(A)| · ∏i=1^∞ (1−p-i)). For A = (ℤ/5ℤ)², |Aut(A)| = |GL₂(𝔽₅)| = (5²−1)(5²−5) = 24·20 = 480. The infinite product ∏i=1^∞ (1−5-i) = (1−1/5)(1−1/25)(1−1/125)⋯ ≈ 0.7603. The normalising constant is 1/∏i=1^∞ (1−5-i) ≈ 1.315. Thus P((ℤ/5ℤ)²) = 1/(480 · 0.7603) ≈ 1/364.9 ≈ 0.00274. The exact expression is (1−1/5)(1−1/25)(1−1/125)⋯ / 480.
Exam Strategy: Distinguishing Distribution vs Moment Results
When a question asks “what is the probability that Cl(K)[p^∞] ≃ A?”, the answer is the Cohen–Lenstra measure 1/(|Aut(A)|·∏(1−p-i)). When it asks “what is the average size of Cl(K)[p]?”, the answer is the first moment, which for imaginary quadratic fields is 2 for p=3 (Davenport–Heilbronn) and 1+1/p for p≥5 (Cohen–Lenstra prediction, now a theorem of Koymans–Pagano). Moment theorems do not determine the distribution unless Carleman’s condition holds; the boundedness of p‑Selmer ranks guarantees it, but you must state the condition explicitly if asked to justify uniqueness.

The Ellenberg–Venkatesh–Westerland Homological Stability for Hurwitz Spaces and the Function Field Cohen–Lenstra Heuristics

Ellenberg, Venkatesh, and Westerland (2016) proved the function field analogue of the Cohen–Lenstra heuristics for p‑torsion in class groups of quadratic extensions of 𝔽_q(t) by establishing homological stability for Hurwitz spaces. A Hurwitz space Hg,r parametrises degree‑r covers of the projective line 𝔽_q with prescribed ramification (e.g. r=2 for quadratic extensions). The cohomology groups H^i(Hg,r, ℚ_ℓ) stabilise as the genus g → ∞: for each i there is a stable cohomology group H^istable independent of g for g sufficiently large. The Grothendieck–Lefschetz trace formula expresses the number of 𝔽_q‑points on Hg,r as an alternating sum of traces of Frobenius on these cohomology groups. The stable cohomology is identified with the homology of the infinite symplectic group Sp(∞, 𝔽_p) (for p odd) or the infinite orthogonal group (for p=2). The moments of the p‑Selmer group (which in the function field setting is the p‑torsion in the Jacobian of the cover) are then computed as the traces of Frobenius on the stable cohomology. For quadratic extensions (r=2) and p odd, the stable cohomology is the homology of Sp(∞, 𝔽_p), and the moments match the Cohen–Lenstra predictions: the k‑th moment of the size of the p‑Selmer group is the number of isotropic subspaces in a 2k‑dimensional symplectic space over 𝔽_p, which equals the Gaussian binomial coefficient ∑j=0^k pj(j−1)/2 [k choose j]_p. The average size of the p‑Selmer group is p+1, and the second moment is 1 + 1/p + 1/p². As q → ∞ these converge to the number field predictions. The proof uses the “homological stability” of the Hurwitz spaces, which is established by showing that the map Hg,r → Hg+1,r induced by adding a handle induces an isomorphism on cohomology up to a degree that grows with g. This is a geometric analogue of the stability of the homology of mapping class groups (Harer stability). The method also yields the distribution of the p‑Selmer group as a random maximal isotropic subspace in a symplectic space over 𝔽_p, i.e. the cokernel of a random alternating matrix. For p=2 the symmetry is orthogonal and the stable cohomology is that of O(∞, 𝔽₂), giving the Delaunay distribution for 2‑Selmer groups. A key technical point is that the Hurwitz space is not proper; one must compactify it (e.g. by admissible covers) and control the boundary cohomology. The boundary strata correspond to degenerate covers and their cohomology is shown to be negligible in the stable range. This geometric approach explains why the alternating pairing appears naturally for p≥5: it is the cup‑product pairing on the middle cohomology of the Hurwitz space, which is symplectic by Poincaré duality. The function field proof also clarifies the role of the Cassels–Tate pairing: it corresponds to the cup‑product pairing on the middle cohomology of the Hurwitz space, and its alternating nature forces the Poonen–Rains weight 1/(|Aut(A,⟨·,·⟩)|·|A|) rather than the simpler Cohen–Lenstra weight 1/|Aut(A)|.

The Pierce–Turnage-Butterbaugh–Wood Formulation of Cohen–Lenstra for General Number Fields

Pierce, Turnage‑Butterbaugh, and Wood (2020) gave a unified formulation of the Cohen–Lenstra heuristics for the p‑part of the class group of an arbitrary number field K, not just quadratic fields. The heuristic predicts that for a fixed number field K and a fixed odd prime p, the distribution of the p‑part of the class group of a random extension L/K of degree n (ordered by discriminant) is given by a measure on finite abelian p‑groups A that is proportional to 1/(|Aut(A)| · |A|r_K · ∏i=1^∞ (1−p-i)), where r_K is the p‑rank of the unit group of K modulo p‑th powers (i.e. the dimension of (O_K^× ⊗ 𝔽_p) as an 𝔽_p‑vector space). For K=ℚ, r_ℚ = 0 and we recover the original Cohen–Lenstra measure for imaginary quadratic fields (where the unit group is finite) and the Cohen–Lenstra–Martinet measure for real quadratic fields (where r_ℚ = 1 because the fundamental unit contributes a p‑adic regulator). The factor |A|r_K arises from the p‑adic regulator: the probability that a random p‑adic matrix has cokernel A is weighted by the volume of the set of matrices with that cokernel, which introduces a factor of |A|r where r is the rank of the unit group. The formulation also incorporates the “Malle–Bhargava” principle: the probability that a random extension L/K has Galois group G is proportional to 1/|Aut(G)| times a discriminant factor. For abelian extensions, the Galois group is the class group itself, and the two principles combine to give the Pierce–Turnage-Butterbaugh–Wood measure. The heuristic is supported by function field analogues (Ellenber–Venkatesh–Westerland for quadratic extensions, and later work of Wood and others for general abelian extensions) and by numerical data for cubic, quartic, and quintic fields. A critical subtlety is the ordering: the heuristic is stated for extensions ordered by absolute discriminant, but the discriminant of L/K is not the same as the absolute discriminant of L. The correct ordering is by the norm of the relative discriminant ideal. The formulation also predicts the distribution of the p‑part of the ray class group modulo a modulus m: the weight becomes 1/(|Aut(A)| · |A|r_K + r_m · ∏(1−p-i)), where r_m is the p‑rank of the unit group modulo m. For the Hilbert class field (m=1) this reduces to the class group formula. The Pierce–Turnage-Butterbaugh–Wood measure has been proved for function fields in the case of cyclic extensions of prime degree ℓ ≠ p by work of Wood and others, using homological stability for Hurwitz spaces with level structure. For number fields, the only proven cases are the Davenport–Heilbronn theorem (3‑torsion in quadratic fields) and the Bhargava–Varma theorem (mean number of 3‑torsion elements in quadratic fields), and the recent Koymans–Pagano theorem for imaginary quadratic fields. The general case remains a major open problem.

The Distribution of Tate–Shafarevich Groups in Quadratic Twist Families and the Delaunay Heuristics

Delaunay (2001, 2007) proposed a precise conjectural distribution for the Tate–Shafarevich group Ш(E/ℚ) of an elliptic curve E over ℚ, modelled on the cokernel of a random alternating matrix over the p‑adic integers ℤ_p for each prime p. For a fixed elliptic curve E, consider the quadratic twist family E_d: y² = x³ + d²Ax + d³B. The p‑part of Ш(E_d) is conjectured to be distributed as the cokernel of a random alternating matrix of size n×n over ℤ_p, where n is the p‑Selmer rank of E_d, conditioned on the Cassels–Tate pairing being alternating and non‑degenerate on Ш(E_d)[p^∞]. The probability that Ш(E_d)[p^∞] ≃ A for a finite abelian p‑group A with an alternating pairing ⟨·,·⟩ is proportional to 1/(|Aut(A,⟨·,·⟩)| · |A|). This is the Poonen–Rains weight. The alternating pairing forces the group to be a symplectic module over ℤ_p, so A must be of the form ⊕ (ℤ/pe_iℤ)². The distribution of the p‑Selmer group Sel_p(E_d) is then the convolution of the distribution of E_d(ℚ)[p] (which is usually trivial for p≥5) with the distribution of Ш(E_d)[p^∞]. The average size of the p‑Selmer group in the quadratic twist family is p+1 for p≥3 (Bhargava–Shankar for p=2,3,5; Bhargava–Gross–Wang for general p). The Delaunay heuristics predict that the average size of Ш(E_d)[p^∞] is 1 + 1/p + 1/p² + ⋯ = p/(p−1). For p=3 this gives 3/2, and for p=5 it gives 5/4. The second moment of |Ш(E_d)[p^∞]| is predicted to be 1 + 2/p + 2/p² + 1/p³. These predictions are supported by the function field analogue: for elliptic curves over 𝔽_q(t), the distribution of Ш in quadratic twist families matches the random alternating matrix model (Ellenberg–Venkatesh–Westerland for p≥5, and later work for p=2,3). A major open problem is the “Shafarevich–Tate conjecture” that Ш is finite; the Delaunay heuristics assume finiteness. If Ш is infinite, the distribution is not defined. The heuristics also predict the correlation between the p‑Selmer rank and the root number: for quadratic twist families with fixed root number +1, the distribution of the p‑Selmer group is modelled by the cokernel of a random symmetric matrix (orthogonal symmetry), while for families with equidistributed root number it is modelled by a random alternating matrix (symplectic symmetry). This distinction is crucial for the Bhargava–Shankar–Wang theorem on average 5‑Selmer ranks: the family of quadratic twists with root number +1 has unitary symmetry for the L‑function, but the 5‑Selmer distribution is still governed by the alternating matrix model because the Cassels–Tate pairing is alternating. A common examiner trap is to confuse the symmetry type of the L‑function (unitary, symplectic, orthogonal) with the symmetry type of the Selmer group (alternating vs symmetric). The L‑function symmetry governs the low‑lying zeros; the Selmer group symmetry governs the distribution of the p‑Selmer group via the Cassels–Tate pairing. They coincide for p=2 (where the Cassels–Tate pairing is symmetric) but differ for p≥5.

  • Confusing the CFKRS arithmetic factor (an Euler product over primes) with the random matrix integral (a Haar integral over a classical compact group); they are conjecturally equal but arise from completely different calculations.
  • Stating that the Koymans–Pagano theorem proves Cohen–Lenstra for real quadratic fields; it only covers imaginary quadratic fields because the regulator breaks the symmetry.
  • Assuming homological stability for Hurwitz spaces holds for all degrees i; it only holds up to a degree that grows with the genus g (the stable range).
  • Using the Cohen–Lenstra weight 1/|Aut(A)| instead of the Pierce–Turnage-Butterbaugh–Wood weight 1/(|Aut(A)|·|A|r_K) for general base fields K; the unit rank r_K is essential.
  • Equating the symmetry type of the L‑function (e.g. unitary for quadratic twists with fixed root number) with the symmetry type of the Selmer group (alternating for p≥5); they are different objects governed by different pairings.
  • Forgetting that the Delaunay heuristics assume the finiteness of Ш; if Ш is infinite the distribution is not defined.
  • Neglecting the condition that the alternating pairing on Ш must be non‑degenerate; the Poonen–Rains weight includes the factor 1/|A| precisely because of this non‑degeneracy.

The Cohen–Lenstra Heuristics for Class Groups with Prescribed Ramification

The original Cohen–Lenstra heuristics (1984) predict the distribution of the p‑part of the class group of an imaginary quadratic field as the cokernel of a random matrix over the p‑adic integers ℤ_p, weighted by 1/|Aut(A)|. A natural generalization asks for the distribution when the set of ramified primes is fixed in advance. Let S be a finite set of primes. One considers the family of all number fields K of a given degree and signature such that the only primes ramified in K/ℚ are those in S. The Cohen–Lenstra measure must then be modified by a factor that accounts for the local conditions at the primes in S. For each p ∈ S, the local condition imposes that the p‑part of the class group is a quotient of the ray class group modulo the ramified primes. The resulting heuristic, developed by Boston, Bush, and Hajir (2017) and independently by Pierce, Turnage‑Butterbaugh, and Wood (2020), states that the probability that the p‑part of the class group is isomorphic to a finite abelian p‑group A is proportional to 1/(|Aut(A)| · |A|r_S) where r_S is the S‑unit rank of the field. For imaginary quadratic fields with prescribed ramification at a set S of odd primes, the unit rank is 0, but the S‑unit rank is |S|−1. The weight becomes 1/(|Aut(A)| · |A||S|−1). This matches the function field analogue: for a fixed set of places S on a curve over 𝔽_q, the distribution of the p‑part of the class group of an S‑ramified cover is given by the cokernel of a random matrix over 𝔽_q[[t]] with the same weight. The Boston–Bush–Hajir heuristics also predict the joint distribution of the p‑parts for all p simultaneously, assuming independence across primes. A key consequence is that the average size of the p‑part of the class group in such families is ∏i=1|S|−1 (1 + p−i)−1 · (1 + p−1)−1 · …, which differs from the classical Cohen–Lenstra average of ∏i≥1 (1 − p−i)−1. For example, for imaginary quadratic fields ramified only at a single odd prime p (so |S|=1), the average size of the p‑part remains the classical value, but for |S|=2 the average size is multiplied by (1 + p−1)−1. These predictions have been tested numerically for small discriminants and are supported by the function field theorems of Ellenberg, Venkatesh, and Westerland (2016) for Hurwitz spaces with prescribed ramification.

The Distribution of Class Groups in Families with Prescribed Galois Group

Malle's conjecture (2002, 2004) predicts the asymptotic number of number fields of degree n with Galois group G ⊆ S_n and discriminant bounded by X. A finer question asks for the distribution of the class group (or its p‑part) within such a family. For a fixed transitive subgroup G of S_n, consider the family of all number fields K with Galois closure having Galois group G and discriminant |Δ_K| ≤ X. The Cohen–Lenstra philosophy suggests that the p‑part of the class group of K should be distributed as the cokernel of a random matrix over ℤ_p, but the weight must reflect the structure of G. The Pierce–Turnage‑Butterbaugh–Wood (PTBW) formulation (2020) gives a precise conjectural weight: for a finite abelian p‑group A, the probability is proportional to 1/(|Aut_G(A)| · |A|r_K) where Aut_G(A) is the group of automorphisms of A that commute with the action of G on the class group via the Galois module structure, and r_K is the unit rank of K. When G is the full symmetric group S_n, the action is trivial on the p‑part for p ∤ n, and the weight reduces to the classical 1/|Aut(A)|. For G = C_n (cyclic), the class group is a module over the group ring ℤ_p[C_n], and the automorphism group is larger, changing the distribution. Bhargava and Varma (2022) proved a version of Malle's conjecture for S_n-extensions and gave a geometry‑of‑numbers framework to count such fields. Their method, combined with the PTBW weight, predicts that the average size of the p‑part of the class group in the family of S_n-extensions is ∏i=1n−1 (1 − p−i)−1 for p ∤ n, and a different product for p | n. For cyclic extensions of prime degree ℓ, the Smith conjecture (2017) gives a precise distribution for the ℓ‑part of the class group, which is a module over ℤ_ℓ[C_ℓ] ≃ ℤ_ℓ × ℤ_ℓ[ζ_ℓ]. The ℓ‑part splits into a “trivial” part and a “non‑trivial” part; the trivial part follows the classical Cohen–Lenstra distribution, while the non‑trivial part follows a distribution weighted by 1/|Autℤ_ℓ[ζ_ℓ](A)|. The Koymans–Pagano theorem (2022) for imaginary quadratic fields is the first case where such a distribution is proved unconditionally for a positive proportion of fields. For general G, the distribution remains conjectural, but the function field analogue has been established by Ellenberg, Venkatesh, and Westerland (2016) for Hurwitz spaces parametrizing G‑covers of ℙ1, where the class group is replaced by the Jacobian of the cover and the weight is given by the cokernel of a random matrix over the function field with the appropriate group action.

Worked Example: Average 3‑Part in S₃‑Extensions
Let K be a cubic field with Galois group S₃ (i.e., a non‑Galois cubic field). The 3‑part of the class group Cl(K)[3^∞] is a module over ℤ₃. Since 3 ∤ 6, the action of S₃ on Cl(K)[3^∞] is trivial. The PTBW weight for a finite abelian 3‑group A is 1/|Aut(A)|. The average size of Cl(K)[3^∞] is therefore the classical Cohen–Lenstra average for p=3: ∏i≥1 (1 − 3−i)−1 = (1 − 1/3)−1(1 − 1/9)−1(1 − 1/27)−1⋯ = (3/2)(9/8)(27/26)⋯ ≈ 1.884. Compute the first three factors and give the approximate value.
The product of the first three factors is (3/2) × (9/8) × (27/26) = (3×9×27)/(2×8×26) = 729/416 ≈ 1.752. The infinite product converges to approximately 1.884. This is the predicted average size of the 3‑part of the class group in the family of S₃‑cubic fields ordered by discriminant.
Exam Strategy: Distinguishing Weights
When a question asks for the average size of a p‑part in a family with prescribed Galois group G, first check whether p divides |G|. If p ∤ |G|, the action is trivial and the weight is the classical 1/|Aut(A)|. If p | |G|, the class group is a module over ℤ_p[G] and the weight involves the automorphism group of that module. Remember the PTBW formula: weight = 1/(|Aut_G(A)| · |A|r_K). The unit rank r_K depends on the signature of the fields in the family (e.g., r_K = 0 for totally complex fields, r_K = n−1 for totally real fields of degree n).

The Bhargava–Shankar Theorem on the Average 2‑Selmer Group of Elliptic Curves

In a series of papers (2010, 2013, 2015), Bhargava and Shankar developed a geometry‑of‑numbers method to count integral orbits of coregular representations, which they applied to the 2‑Selmer group of elliptic curves over ℚ. An elliptic curve E: y² = x³ + Ax + B has a 2‑Selmer group Sel₂(E) that fits into the exact sequence 0 → E(ℚ)/2E(ℚ) → Sel₂(E) → Ш(E/ℚ)[2] → 0. The 2‑Selmer group can be identified with a subset of the 2‑torsion in the Weil–Châtelet group, which in turn corresponds to locally soluble 2‑coverings of E. Bhargava and Shankar parametrized these 2‑coverings by the set of integral binary quartic forms f(x,y) = ax⁴ + bx³y + cx²y² + dxy³ + ey⁴ having bounded invariants I and J, where I = 12ae − 3bd + c² and J = 72ace + 9bcd − 27ad² − 27eb² − 2c³ are the classical invariants of degree 2 and 3. The discriminant of the quartic form is Δ = (1/27)(4I³ − J²), which matches the discriminant of the elliptic curve y² = x³ − 27Ix − 27J. They proved that the number of GL₂(ℤ)-equivalence classes of integral binary quartic forms with |I| ≤ X1/3 and |J| ≤ X1/2 is asymptotic to a constant times X5/6, and that the average number of locally soluble orbits (which correspond to elements of Sel₂(E)) is exactly 3. More precisely, for the family of all elliptic curves over ℚ ordered by height H(E) = max{|A|³, |B|²}, the average size of the 2‑Selmer group is 3. This implies that the average rank of E(ℚ) is at most 1.5 (since rank ≤ dim𝔽₂ Sel₂(E) − 1 on average). Later work by Bhargava, Shankar, and others refined this to show that a positive proportion of elliptic curves have rank 0 and a positive proportion have rank 1, and that the average rank is bounded by 0.885… (Bhargava–Shankar 2015, using the 2‑Selmer group and the parity conjecture). The method uses a fundamental domain for the action of GL₂(ℤ) on the space of binary quartic forms, and a sieve to impose local solubility conditions at all primes. The key geometric input is that the number of integral points in a expanding region of the fundamental domain is asymptotic to its volume, with an error term controlled by the geometry of numbers. The same method was extended to the 3‑Selmer group (Bhargava–Shankar–Tsimerman 2013) using ternary cubic forms, and to the 5‑Selmer group (Bhargava–Shankar–Wang 2022) using 5×5×5 cubes.
