For the 2‑Selmer group, the distribution of the size is not just the average; Bhargava and Shankar also computed the distribution of the 2‑Selmer rank, showing that the probability that dim𝔽₂ Sel₂(E) = r is given by a product formula involving the Cohen–Lenstra measure for the cokernel of a random symmetric matrix over ℤ₂. This matches the Delaunay heuristics for p=2, where the Cassels–Tate pairing is symmetric.

The Work of Melanie Matchett Wood on the Distribution of Class Groups

Melanie Matchett Wood (2010, 2011, 2017) developed a general framework for the distribution of class groups of number fields that extends the Cohen–Lenstra heuristics to arbitrary families. Her approach uses the language of random matrices over the profinite completion of ℤ, and she proved that for any family of number fields that satisfies certain “equidistribution” conditions, the distribution of the class group is given by a universal measure on the space of profinite abelian groups. The key idea is to model the class group as the cokernel of a random matrix M over the profinite integers ℤ = ∏_p ℤ_p, where the entries of M are chosen independently from the Haar measure on ℤ. The probability that coker(M) ≃ A for a finite abelian group A is proportional to 1/|Aut(A)|. Wood showed that this measure is the unique probability measure on the space of profinite abelian groups that is invariant under taking direct sums with a fixed group and satisfies a certain “multiplicativity” property across primes. She then proved that for the family of all quadratic fields (both real and imaginary), the distribution of the class group converges to this measure, provided one weights each field by 1/|Aut(Cl(K))|. For real quadratic fields, the regulator introduces a bias, and the correct weight is 1/(|Aut(Cl(K))| · |Cl(K)|), which is the Cohen–Lenstra–Martinet weight. Wood's framework also applies to families of number fields of higher degree. For example, for the family of all cubic fields ordered by discriminant, she predicted the distribution of the class group and computed the average size of the p‑part for each p. Her predictions were later confirmed by Bhargava, Shankar, and Wang (2022) for the 2‑part and 3‑part of the class group of cubic fields, using the geometry of numbers. Wood also studied the distribution of the class group in families of function fields. For a fixed curve C over 𝔽_q, the Jacobian Jac(C) is a finite abelian group, and as C varies in a family (e.g., hyperelliptic curves of genus g), the distribution of Jac(C) converges to the same universal measure, but with a different weighting due to the automorphisms of the curve. This function field result was proved by Ellenberg, Venkatesh, and Westerland (2016) using homological stability for Hurwitz spaces. Wood's work also includes a “non‑abelian” version, where the class group is replaced by the Galois group of the maximal unramified extension (the Hilbert class field tower), and the distribution is given by a measure on profinite groups. This is the Wood–Zureick‑Brown non‑abelian Cohen–Lenstra heuristic (2017), which predicts the distribution of the Galois group of the maximal unramified pro‑p extension of a number field.

The Function Field Analogue for Higher Rank Groups

The classical function field analogue of the Cohen–Lenstra heuristics, due to Friedman and Washington (1989), concerns the distribution of the class group (i.e., the Jacobian) of a random hyperelliptic curve over 𝔽_q. They showed that as the genus g → ∞, the distribution of the p‑part of the Jacobian converges to the cokernel of a random g×g matrix over ℤ_p, weighted by 1/|Aut(A)|. This matches the Cohen–Lenstra prediction for imaginary quadratic fields. A far‑reaching generalization asks for the distribution of the cokernel of a random matrix in a classical compact group other than the full general linear group. For example, the Katz–Sarnak philosophy predicts that the low‑lying zeros of L‑functions in a family with symmetry type G (where G is one of the classical compact groups: unitary U(N), symplectic USp(2N), orthogonal SO(2N) or SO(2N+1)) are distributed like the eigenvalues of a random matrix in G. The corresponding arithmetic statistic is the distribution of the cokernel of a random matrix in the p‑adic analogue of G. For instance, for a family of elliptic curves over 𝔽_q(t) with symplectic symmetry (the generic case), the p‑Selmer group is conjectured to be distributed as the cokernel of a random alternating matrix over ℤ_p, which is the p‑adic analogue of the symplectic group. This is the Poonen–Rains model (2012). For families with orthogonal symmetry (e.g., quadratic twists with fixed root number), the p‑Selmer group is distributed as the cokernel of a random symmetric matrix. For families with unitary symmetry (e.g., all elliptic curves over 𝔽_q(t) without fixing the root number), the distribution is given by the cokernel of a random matrix in the unitary group over ℤ_p, which is more subtle because the unitary group is not defined over ℤ_p but over a quadratic extension. The work of Ellenberg, Venkatesh, and Westerland (2016) established the function field Cohen–Lenstra heuristics for Hurwitz spaces, which parametrize G‑covers of ℙ1 for a finite group G. The Jacobian of the cover is a module over the group ring ℤ_p[G], and its distribution is given by the cokernel of a random matrix in the centralizer of G in GL_n(ℤ_p). This provides a function field proof of the PTBW weight for families with prescribed Galois group. For higher rank groups, such as GL_n with n > 1, the relevant arithmetic objects are not class groups but rather the cohomology groups of local systems on the curve. The distribution of these cohomology groups as the curve varies is conjectured to be given by the cokernel of a random matrix in the appropriate p‑adic group, and this is related to the geometric Langlands program. The work of Bhargava, Gross, and Wang (2017) on the average size of the p‑Selmer group of elliptic curves over ℚ uses the function field analogue as a guide: they compute the average size of the p‑Selmer group in the family of all elliptic curves over 𝔽_q(t) and find it to be p+1 for p ≥ 3, matching the number‑field prediction. The function field proof uses the geometry of the moduli stack of elliptic curves and the Grothendieck–Lefschetz trace formula, which expresses the average as a sum of traces of Frobenius on the cohomology of the stack. This cohomology is computed using the Weil conjectures and the theory of perverse sheaves, and the result is a product of local factors that matches the random matrix integral over the symplectic group.

  • Confusing the Boston–Bush–Hajir weight 1/(|Aut(A)|·|A||S|−1) with the classical Cohen–Lenstra weight 1/|Aut(A)| when the ramification set S has size > 1.
  • Assuming the PTBW weight 1/|Aut_G(A)| applies for all primes p, including those dividing |G|; the automorphism group Aut_G(A) must be taken in the category of ℤ_p[G]‑modules, which is larger than Aut(A) when p | |G|.
  • Stating that the average 2‑Selmer size is 3 for all families of elliptic curves; the Bhargava–Shankar result holds for the family ordered by naive height, but for quadratic twist families the average is 3 only when the root number is equidistributed, and it is 2 or 4 for fixed root number ±1 families.
  • Equating the function field Jacobian distribution (Friedman–Washington) with the number field class group distribution without accounting for the unit rank factor |A|r_K in the weight.
  • Believing that the Katz–Sarnak symmetry type of the L‑function (unitary, symplectic, orthogonal) directly determines the symmetry type of the Selmer group for all p; for p=2 the Cassels–Tate pairing is symmetric (orthogonal), while for p≥5 it is alternating (symplectic), regardless of the L‑function symmetry.
  • Neglecting that the Wood universal measure on profinite abelian groups is the unique measure satisfying multiplicativity and direct‑sum invariance, and that the Cohen–Lenstra measure is its projection to the p‑part.
  • Assuming the Ellenberg–Venkatesh–Westerland homological stability holds for all degrees i; it holds only in a stable range i ≤ g/2 (or similar), and the Cohen–Lenstra distribution is obtained by taking g → ∞ first, then i fixed.

The Wood Universal Measure on Profinite Abelian Groups and its Projections to p‑Parts

In 2017 Melanie Matchett Wood constructed a canonical probability measure on the space of isomorphism classes of profinite abelian groups. This measure, now called the Wood universal measure, is characterized by two natural axioms: multiplicativity under direct products and invariance under direct‑sum decomposition. Concretely, if a profinite abelian group decomposes as a direct product of its p‑primary parts, the universal measure factors as the product of its projections to each p‑part. The projection to the p‑part is exactly the Cohen–Lenstra measure, which assigns to a finite abelian p‑group A the weight 1/|Aut(A)| normalized by the appropriate constant. Wood proved that this universal measure is the unique probability measure on the category of profinite abelian groups satisfying the two axioms, thereby placing the Cohen–Lenstra heuristics in a broader structural framework.

The construction proceeds by taking the inverse limit of the finite abelian groups equipped with the Cohen–Lenstra weights. For each prime p, the Cohen–Lenstra measure on finite abelian p‑groups extends uniquely to a measure on the space of pro‑p abelian groups. The product over all primes yields a measure on the space of all profinite abelian groups. This measure governs not only class groups of number fields but also the distribution of Selmer groups, Tate–Shafarevich groups, and the Jacobians of curves over function fields. In the function field setting, the work of Ellenberg, Venkatesh, and Westerland (2016) shows that the distribution of the Jacobian of a random curve over a finite field, viewed as a module over the group ring of a finite group G, converges to the Wood universal measure on the category of ℤ_p[G]‑modules. This provides a conceptual proof of the Pierce–Turnage‑Butterbaugh–Wood (PTBW) weight for families with prescribed Galois group.

A key technical point is that the automorphism group appearing in the weight must be taken in the correct category. For a ℤ_p[G]‑module A, the relevant automorphism group is Autℤ_p[G](A), which can be strictly larger than Aut(A) when p divides |G|. Neglecting this distinction leads to an incorrect weight, a trap that appears frequently in examinations. The universal measure also clarifies the role of the p‑adic regulator: in families where the unit rank r_K is positive, the Cohen–Lenstra weight acquires an extra factor |A|r_K, which is precisely the projection of the universal measure twisted by the regulator character. Wood’s framework thus unifies the classical Cohen–Lenstra heuristics, the Friedman–Washington heuristics for function fields, and the Poonen–Rains model for Selmer groups under a single measure‑theoretic umbrella.

The Distribution of Class Groups in Families of Cyclic Extensions of Prime Power Degree

The Cohen–Lenstra heuristics originally addressed the distribution of class groups of quadratic fields. For cyclic extensions of prime degree p, the situation is more subtle because the unit rank of the base field influences the weight. In 2017, Alexander Smith proved that for a fixed odd prime p, the p‑part of the class group of a random cyclic extension of degree p of a given number field K (ordered by discriminant) follows a distribution that can be described as the cokernel of a random matrix in a certain p‑adic Lie group. The weight attached to a finite abelian p‑group A is proportional to 1/(|Aut(A)|·|A|r_K), where r_K is the unit rank of K. This factor |A|r_K reflects the contribution of the p‑adic regulator and matches the prediction of the Friedman–Washington heuristics in the function field analogue.

The result was extended to cyclic extensions of degree p^n (prime power degree) by Koymans and Pagano (2022). They showed that as n grows, the distribution of the p‑part of the class group converges to a measure on the space of ℤ_p‑modules that is the projection of the Wood universal measure twisted by the regulator character. The proof uses a combination of class field theory, the geometry of numbers, and the moment method. In the function field setting, Ellenberg, Venkatesh, and Westerland established the analogous result for ℤ_p‑covers of the projective line, using homological stability for Hurwitz spaces. Their work shows that the limiting distribution is the cokernel of a random matrix in the centralizer of the Galois group inside GL_n(ℤ_p), which for a cyclic group of order p^n is the group of units of the group ring ℤ_p[Cp^n].

An important nuance is that the Smith conjecture (now a theorem) predicts that the average size of the p‑part of the class group in a family of cyclic extensions of degree p is exactly p+1, independent of the base field, provided the family is ordered by discriminant and the unit rank is accounted for. For p=2 the situation differs because the 2‑part of the class group is influenced by the ambiguous class number formula. The Fouvry–Klüners theorem (2007) on the 4‑rank of class groups of quadratic fields is a special case of this theory. Examiners often test the distinction between the weight 1/|Aut(A)| for imaginary quadratic fields (where r_K=0) and the weight 1/(|Aut(A)|·|A|r_K) for real quadratic fields or for cyclic extensions of totally real fields.

Worked Example: Weight Factor in Cyclic Extensions
Let K be a totally real number field of degree 3 with unit rank r_K = 2. Consider the family of cyclic extensions L/K of degree 5 ordered by discriminant. According to the Smith–Koymans–Pagano theorem, what is the weight assigned to a finite abelian 5‑group A in the limiting distribution of the 5‑part of the class group of L?
The weight is proportional to 1/(|Aut(A)|·|A|r_K). Since r_K = 2, the weight is 1/(|Aut(A)|·|A|2). The normalization constant is the reciprocal of the sum of these weights over all isomorphism classes of finite abelian 5‑groups. This matches the projection of the Wood universal measure twisted by the regulator character |A|r_K.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a question asks for the limiting distribution of class groups in a family of cyclic extensions, first identify the base field’s unit rank r_K. The weight always contains the factor |A|r_K in the denominator. If the base field is totally complex (r_K = 0), the factor disappears and the weight reduces to the classical Cohen–Lenstra weight 1/|Aut(A)|. Remember that for p=2 the weight formula changes because the 2‑part is governed by the ambiguous class number formula; the average size of the 2‑part is not given by the same p+1 rule.

The Distribution of Selmer Groups in Families of Elliptic Curves over Number Fields

The groundbreaking work of Bhargava and Shankar (2015) established that the average size of the 2‑Selmer group of elliptic curves over ℚ, ordered by naive height, is 3. This result was extended to the average size of the p‑Selmer group for p=3,5 by Bhargava, Shankar, and Tsimerman (2016) and by Bhargava, Shankar, and Wang (2020) respectively. For a general number field K, the geometry‑of‑numbers approach can be adapted to count elliptic curves over K ordered by a suitable height. The main technical obstacle is the presence of units of infinite order in the ring of integers of K, which affects the fundamental domain for the action of GL2(O_K) on the space of binary quartic forms (for 2‑Selmer) or the relevant higher‑degree forms for p‑Selmer.

In 2021, work of Klagsbrun, Mazur, and Rubin on quadratic twist families over number fields showed that the distribution of the p‑Selmer group in a quadratic twist family with fixed root number is given by the cokernel of a random symmetric matrix (for root number +1) or a random alternating matrix (for root number −1) over ℤ_p, provided p is odd. For p=2 the Cassels–Tate pairing is symmetric, so the model is always symmetric regardless of root number. This matches the Katz–Sarnak philosophy: the symmetry type of the L‑function (orthogonal for quadratic twists with fixed root number, symplectic for the full family) dictates the random matrix ensemble, and the p‑adic analogue of that ensemble governs the Selmer group.

A further refinement comes from the Poonen–Rains model (2012), which predicts that for a family of elliptic curves over a number field with no prescribed root number, the p‑Selmer group is distributed as the cokernel of a random matrix in the unitary group over ℤ_p. The unitary group is defined over a quadratic extension of ℤ_p, making the model more delicate. Recent work of Bhargava, Gross, and Wang (2017) computed the average size of the p‑Selmer group in the function field setting and found it to be p+1 for all p≥3, confirming the random matrix prediction. The number field analogue remains conjectural for p≥7, but the geometry‑of‑numbers machinery developed for p=2,3,5 is expected to generalize.

The Average Size of the p‑Selmer Group for p ≥ 7 and the Geometry of Numbers

For primes p≥7, the parametrization of p‑Selmer elements by orbits of a representation of a reductive group (as in the work of Bhargava and his collaborators for p=2,3,5) is not yet available. However, the Bhargava–Gross–Wang heuristics (2017) give a precise conjectural formula for the average size of the p‑Selmer group in any family of elliptic curves over a global field. In the function field case, they proved that the average size is p+1 for all p≥3 by evaluating the trace of Frobenius on the cohomology of the moduli stack of elliptic curves. The computation uses the Grothendieck–Lefschetz trace formula and the Weil conjectures, and the result factors as a product of local integrals over the symplectic group Sp2g(ℤ_p), which equals p+1.

In the number field setting, the geometry‑of‑numbers method would require a parametrization of p‑Selmer elements by integral orbits of a prehomogeneous vector space. For p=2,3,5 such parametrizations exist (binary quartic forms, pairs of ternary quadratic forms, and 5×5×5 cubes respectively). For p≥7, no such parametrization is known, and it is an open problem whether one exists. Nevertheless, the moment method developed by Bhargava and Shankar can be applied to the moments of the p‑Selmer group once a suitable height zeta function is constructed. The Bhargava geometric sieve is used to restrict to curves with squarefree discriminant, which is essential for the Selmer group to be well‑behaved.

The random matrix model predicts that the distribution of the p‑Selmer group in the family of all elliptic curves over ℚ (ordered by height) is the cokernel of a random alternating matrix over ℤ_p, i.e. the p‑adic symplectic ensemble. The average size of the cokernel of a random alternating matrix of size 2g×2g over ℤ_p, as g→∞, is exactly p+1. This matches the function field average and provides strong evidence for the conjecture. For quadratic twist families with fixed root number, the symmetry type is orthogonal, and the average size of the p‑Selmer group is predicted to be 2 for root number +1 and 4 for root number −1 (for odd p). These predictions have been verified for p=3,5 in the number field case and for all p in the function field case.

  • Confusing the Wood universal measure (on profinite abelian groups) with the Cohen–Lenstra measure (its p‑part projection); the universal measure is the product over all primes, while Cohen–Lenstra is a single prime.
  • Forgetting the regulator factor |A|r_K in the weight for cyclic extensions of fields with positive unit rank; this factor is absent for imaginary quadratic fields but present for totally real base fields.
  • Assuming the Smith conjecture gives the same weight for p=2 as for odd p; the 2‑part is governed by the ambiguous class number formula and the weight involves the 2‑rank of the unit group.
  • Stating that the average 2‑Selmer size is 3 for every family of elliptic curves over a number field; the Bhargava–Shankar result holds for the family ordered by naive height over ℚ, but for quadratic twist families the average depends on the root number.
  • Equating the symmetry type of the L‑function (unitary, symplectic, orthogonal) with the symmetry type of the Selmer group for all p; for p=2 the Cassels–Tate pairing is symmetric (orthogonal) while for p≥5 it is alternating (symplectic), independent of the L‑function symmetry.
  • Neglecting that the Poonen–Rains model for Selmer groups uses the cokernel of a random alternating matrix only for families with symplectic symmetry; for orthogonal symmetry the model uses a random symmetric matrix, and for unitary symmetry a random matrix in the unitary group over a quadratic extension of ℤ_p.
  • Believing that a parametrization of p‑Selmer elements by integral orbits exists for all primes p; such parametrizations are known only for p=2,3,5, and the existence for p≥7 is an open problem.

The Minimalist Conjecture for Elliptic Curves and the Work of Park–Poonen–Voight–Wood

The minimalist conjecture asserts that when elliptic curves over ℚ are ordered by naive height, exactly 50 % have rank 0, 50 % have rank 1, and 0 % have rank ≥ 2. This implies an average rank of 1/2. The conjecture originates from the parity conjecture (which follows from the Birch and Swinnerton-Dyer conjecture and the finiteness of the Tate–Shafarevich group) and the philosophy that the 2‑Selmer group should behave like a random maximal isotropic subspace in a symplectic vector space over 𝔽₂. In 2016, Park, Poonen, Voight, and Wood gave a precise probabilistic model: they consider the 2‑Selmer group as the cokernel of a random symmetric matrix over ℤ₂ (orthogonal symmetry) and the rank as the dimension of a random maximal isotropic subspace for the Cassels–Tate pairing. Their model predicts not only the minimalist conjecture but also the full distribution of 2‑Selmer ranks and the distribution of the 2‑primary part of the Tate–Shafarevich group. The model is consistent with the Bhargava–Shankar theorem that the average size of the 2‑Selmer group is 3, and with the Bhargava–Shankar–Wang result that the average size of the 2‑Selmer group in quadratic twist families depends on the root number (average 3 for root number +1, average 6 for root number −1). The minimalist conjecture remains unproven; the best unconditional upper bound on the average rank is 0.885… due to Bhargava and Shankar (2015), improved to 0.83… by Bhargava, Elkies, and Shnidman (2023) using a refinement of the geometry‑of‑numbers method. The conjecture is also supported by extensive numerical data compiled by Stein, Watkins, and others, which show the proportion of rank 0 curves approaching 50 % as the height bound increases.

The Bhargava Theorems on the Density of Discriminants of Sₙ‑Extensions

Bhargava’s doctoral work (2001–2005) established the asymptotic formula for the number of Sₙ‑extensions of ℚ with absolute discriminant bounded by X for n = 2, 3, 4, 5. For n = 2 (quadratic fields), the classical result is N(S₂, X) ∼ (1/ζ(2)) X. For n = 3 (cubic fields), Davenport and Heilbronn (1971) proved N(S₃, X) ∼ (1/3ζ(3)) X using the geometry of numbers on the space of binary cubic forms. Bhargava reinterpreted this via the action of GL₂(ℤ) on Sym³(ℤ²) and extended the method to n = 4 (quartic fields) using the action of GL₂(ℤ) × GL₃(ℤ) on ℤ² ⊗ Sym²(ℤ³) (pairs of ternary quadratic forms), obtaining N(S₄, X) ∼ c₄ X with c₄ = ζ(2)ζ(3)ζ(4)⁻¹ × (product of local densities). For n = 5 (quintic fields), Bhargava used the action of GL₅(ℤ)³ on ℤ⁵ ⊗ ℤ⁵ ⊗ ℤ⁵ (5×5×5 cubes) to prove N(S₅, X) ∼ c₅ X. The constants cₙ are products of local masses that match the Malle conjecture prediction N(G, X) ∼ c(G) X¹ᐟᵃᵍ (log X)ᵇ⁽ᴳ⁾⁻¹ with a(Sₙ) = 1 and b(Sₙ) = 1. For n ≥ 6, no prehomogeneous vector space parametrizes Sₙ‑extensions, and the problem remains open. The Malle conjecture predicts N(Sₙ, X) ∼ cₙ X for all n, but the secondary term and error term are unknown. Recent work of Bhargava, Shankar, and Wang (2022) on the average size of the 5‑Selmer group uses the 5×5×5 cube parametrization, illustrating the power of these geometric correspondences. The geometry‑of‑numbers method requires counting integral orbits of bounded height in a prehomogeneous vector space, which is achieved by averaging over a fundamental domain for the group action and applying a sieve to restrict to maximal orders (squarefree discriminant). The Bhargava geometric sieve is essential for removing non‑maximal orders and for handling the convergence of the Euler product of local densities.

Worked Example: Average 2‑Selmer Size vs Minimalist Conjecture
Explain why the Bhargava–Shankar theorem (average 2‑Selmer size = 3) is consistent with the minimalist conjecture (50 % rank 0, 50 % rank 1), and compute the predicted average size of the 2‑torsion in the Tate–Shafarevich group under the minimalist model.
Under the minimalist conjecture, rank 0 curves have 2‑Selmer group of size 1 (trivial) or 2 (if Sha[2] ≠ 0), and rank 1 curves have 2‑Selmer group of size 2 or 4. The Cassels–Tate pairing is alternating on the 2‑Selmer group modulo the image of E(ℚ)/2E(ℚ). For rank 0, the 2‑Selmer group is isomorphic to Sha[2], which is a finite abelian 2‑group with alternating pairing, so its size is a square. The minimalist model of Park–Poonen–Voight–Wood takes the 2‑Selmer group to be the cokernel of a random symmetric matrix over ℤ₂, conditioned on the parity of the rank. The average size of the cokernel of a random symmetric matrix over ℤ₂ is 3. This matches Bhargava–Shankar. Under the minimalist conjecture, the average rank is 1/2, so the average dimension of E(ℚ)/2E(ℚ) is 1/2. The exact sequence 0 → E(ℚ)/2E(ℚ) → Sel₂(E) → Sha[2] → 0 gives |Sel₂| = 2ʳ · |Sha[2]|. Taking averages and using the fact that the 2‑Selmer group is equidistributed as the cokernel of a random symmetric matrix, the average of |Sha[2]| is 3 / 2¹ᐟ² = 3/√2 ≈ 2.12. However, the minimalist model predicts the full distribution: the probability that Sha[2] is trivial is ∏k≥1 (1 − 2⁻ᵏ) ≈ 0.288, and the average size of Sha[2] is 2. The consistency is that the average 2‑Selmer size 3 decomposes as (average 2ʳ) × (average |Sha[2]|) with correlation; the random matrix model captures this correlation exactly.
Exam Strategy: Distinguishing Heuristics from Theorems
In arithmetic statistics, examiners often test whether you can separate proven theorems (Bhargava–Shankar average 2‑Selmer = 3, Davenport–Heilbronn average 3‑torsion = 4) from conjectural heuristics (minimalist conjecture, Cohen–Lenstra for real quadratic fields, Malle for n≥6). Memorise the exact status: Bhargava’s Sₙ density theorems are theorems for n≤5; for n≥6 they are conjectural. The minimalist conjecture is a conjecture supported by a random matrix model and data, not a theorem. The Cohen–Lenstra heuristics for imaginary quadratic fields are conjectures; for function fields they are theorems (Friedman–Washington, Ellenberg–Venkatesh–Westerland). Always note the field (number field vs function field) and the ordering (discriminant vs height) when stating a result.

The Sato–Kimura Classification of Prehomogeneous Vector Spaces and the Parametrization of Orders

A prehomogeneous vector space (PVS) is a triple (G, ρ, V) where G is a reductive algebraic group, V is a finite‑dimensional vector space, and ρ: G → GL(V) is a rational representation with a Zariski‑open orbit. Sato and Kimura (1977) classified the irreducible PVS over ℂ up to castling equivalence, finding 36 distinct cases. This classification provides the theoretical foundation for Bhargava’s higher composition laws. The space of binary quadratic forms (Gauss composition) corresponds to the PVS (GL₂ × GL₂, Sym²(ℂ²) ⊗ ℂ²). The space of binary cubic forms (cubic rings) corresponds to (GL₂, Sym³(ℂ²)). The space of pairs of ternary quadratic forms (quartic rings) corresponds to (GL₂ × GL₃, ℂ² ⊗ Sym²(ℂ³)). The space of 5×5×5 cubes (quintic rings) corresponds to (GL₅ × GL₅ × GL₅, ℂ⁵ ⊗ ℂ⁵ ⊗ ℂ⁵). Each PVS has a relative invariant polynomial (the discriminant) whose zero set is the complement of the open orbit. The integral orbits of G(ℤ) on V(ℤ) parametrize isomorphism classes of orders in étale algebras of degree n, with the discriminant of the order matching the invariant. The classification explains why such parametrizations exist for n = 2,3,4,5 but not for n ≥ 6: there is no irreducible PVS whose open orbit corresponds to Sₙ‑extensions for n ≥ 6. The Sato–Kimura list also includes the PVS for sextic rings (2×3×3 boxes, corresponding to (GL₂ × GL₃ × GL₃, ℂ² ⊗ ℂ³ ⊗ ℂ³)) which parametrize sextic rings with a quadratic resolvent, and the PVS for octic rings (4×4×4 cubes). The geometry‑of‑numbers method counts integral orbits of bounded height by integrating over a fundamental domain for G(ℝ) on V(ℝ) and applying a sieve to select maximal orders. The Shintani zeta function associated to a PVS provides an analytic counterpart: ζ(s) = ∑x∈G(ℤ)V(ℤ) |Disc(x)|⁻ˢ, which has meromorphic continuation and a functional equation, yielding asymptotic formulas with error terms.

The Geometry of Numbers via Shintani Zeta Functions and the Datskovsky–Wright Theorems

Shintani (1972) introduced zeta functions for prehomogeneous vector spaces to count integral orbits by discriminant. For a PVS (G, V) with relative invariant F, the Shintani zeta function is ζ(s) = ∑x∈G(ℤ)V(ℤ) |F(x)|⁻ˢ. Shintani proved that ζ(s) has meromorphic continuation to ℂ with a simple pole at s = 1/deg(F) and satisfies a functional equation relating s to 1/deg(F) − s. Datskovsky and Wright (1988) applied this to the space of binary cubic forms over a global field K, proving that the number of cubic extensions of K with discriminant norm ≤ X is asymptotic to c_K X, with an explicit constant c_K given by a product of local densities. Their method works for any global field (number field or function field) and gives a power‑saving error term O(X¹⁻ᵟ). The Shintani zeta function for binary cubic forms factors as a product of local zeta integrals over K_v, and the residue at s=1 gives the main term. This analytic approach complements Bhargava’s geometry‑of‑numbers method: both yield the same main term, but the Shintani method provides better error terms and works uniformly for all global fields. For quartic fields, the Shintani zeta function for pairs of ternary quadratic forms was studied by Yukie (1993) and later by Bhargava, Shankar, and Wang. The Datskovsky–Wright theorem for cubic fields over function fields implies the function field analogue of the Davenport–Heilbronn theorem, which was used by Ellenberg, Venkatesh, and Westerland to prove the function field Cohen–Lenstra heuristics via homological stability of Hurwitz spaces. The Shintani zeta function also encodes the distribution of local invariants (splitting types) via the Euler product, and its poles control the secondary terms in the asymptotic expansion.

The Distribution of p‑Selmer Groups in Families of Elliptic Curves over Global Fields of Positive Characteristic

Over function fields 𝔽_q(t), the distribution of p‑Selmer groups in families of elliptic curves can be studied using the Grothendieck–Lefschetz trace formula on the moduli stack of elliptic curves. Bhargava, Gross, and Wang (2017) proved that for any prime p ≥ 3, the average size of the p‑Selmer group in the family of all elliptic curves over 𝔽_q(t) (ordered by height) is exactly p + 1. The proof evaluates the trace of Frobenius on the ℓ‑adic cohomology of the moduli stack ℳ1,1 and uses the Weil conjectures to compute the local factors. The result factors as a product over places of integrals over the symplectic group Sp₂g(ℤ_p), which evaluates to p + 1. This matches the random matrix prediction that the p‑Selmer group is distributed as the cokernel of a random alternating matrix over ℤ_p (the p‑adic symplectic ensemble). For quadratic twist families with fixed root number, the symmetry type changes: for root number +1 (orthogonal symmetry) the average p‑Selmer size is 2, and for root number −1 (symplectic symmetry) it is 4, for odd p. These predictions were verified by Klagsbrun, Mazur, and Rubin (2016) for p = 3,5 in the number field case and for all p in the function field case. The function field setting also allows the use of the Katz–Sarnak philosophy: the monodromy group of the family determines the symmetry type (unitary, symplectic, orthogonal) and hence the random matrix ensemble. For the family of all elliptic curves over 𝔽_q(t), the monodromy is the full symplectic group, giving the symplectic ensemble. For quadratic twist families, the monodromy is an orthogonal group, giving the orthogonal ensemble. The distribution of the Tate–Shafarevich group in these families is modeled by the Delaunay heuristics, which predict that the probability of a given finite abelian p‑group A occurring as Sha[p^∞] is proportional to |A|⁻¹ |Aut(A)|⁻¹ times a regulator factor. In the function field case, these heuristics become theorems for p ≠ char(𝔽_q) via the work of Ellenberg–Venkatesh–Westerland on homological stability for Hurwitz spaces.

  • Confusing the minimalist conjecture (a conjecture on rank distribution) with the Bhargava–Shankar theorem (a theorem on average 2‑Selmer size); the former implies the latter but is strictly stronger.
  • Stating that Bhargava proved the Malle conjecture for Sₙ‑extensions for all n; he proved the asymptotic N(Sₙ,X) ∼ cₙ X only for n = 2,3,4,5.
  • Assuming the Sato–Kimura classification provides a PVS for every degree n; it gives PVS for n = 2,3,4,5,6 (with resolvent), 8, but not for general n.
  • Believing the Shintani zeta function method and the geometry‑of‑numbers method give different main terms; they give the same main term but the Shintani method yields better error terms.
  • Equating the symmetry type of the L‑function (unitary/symplectic/orthogonal) with the symmetry type of the p‑Selmer group for all p; for p = 2 the Cassels–Tate pairing is symmetric (orthogonal) while for p ≥ 3 it is alternating (symplectic), independent of the L‑function symmetry.
  • Neglecting that the function field average p‑Selmer size p + 1 holds for all p ≥ 3, but the number field analogue is only proven for p = 2,3,5.
  • Overlooking the role of the geometric sieve in Bhargava’s method: it is essential for restricting to maximal orders (squarefree discriminant) and for the convergence of the Euler product of local densities.
The arithmetic statistics of Selmer groups, class groups, and number fields continues to be driven by the interplay between geometry of numbers, random matrix theory, and the function field analogy.

The Cohen–Lenstra Heuristics for Class Groups with Prescribed Splitting Types

The original Cohen–Lenstra heuristics (1984) predict the distribution of the p‑part of the class group of an imaginary quadratic field by assigning to each finite abelian p‑group A a probability proportional to |Aut(A)|⁻¹. Cohen and Martinet (1990) extended this to real quadratic fields by incorporating the p‑adic regulator. A further refinement, due to Wood (2010) and later to Pierce, Turnage‑Butterbaugh, and Wood (2018), treats families of number fields with prescribed splitting behaviour at a finite set of primes. In these families the probability measure on finite abelian p‑groups is multiplied by a local factor that records the splitting type of the prime in the field. For a prime ℓ ≠ p the local factor is the probability that a random matrix in GLₙ(ℤₚ) has a given characteristic polynomial modulo ℓ; for ℓ = p the factor involves the p‑adic regulator and the valuation of the discriminant. The resulting measure remains a product of local densities, and the global constant is the reciprocal of the product of all local factors. This framework has been used to prove, for example, that the 3‑rank of class groups of quadratic fields with prescribed splitting at 2 follows the Cohen–Lenstra distribution with an explicit correction factor. The method also applies to ray class groups, where the modulus introduces additional local conditions at the primes dividing the modulus.

The Distribution of 2‑Selmer Groups in Families of Elliptic Curves over Number Fields

Bhargava and Shankar (2015) proved that the average size of the 2‑Selmer group of an elliptic curve over ℚ, ordered by height, is exactly 3. Their proof uses the geometry of numbers on the space of binary quartic forms, which parametrises 2‑coverings of elliptic curves. The distribution of the 2‑Selmer group is modelled by the cokernel of a random symmetric matrix over the 2‑adic integers ℤ₂, with the probability of a finite abelian 2‑group A being proportional to |A|⁻¹|Aut(A)|⁻¹. Subsequent work by Smith (2017) and by Kane (2019) determined the full distribution in the family of all elliptic curves and in quadratic twist families. In a quadratic twist family with fixed root number +1 (orthogonal symmetry) the average 2‑Selmer size is 2, while for root number −1 (symplectic symmetry) it is 4. These results match the random matrix predictions of the Poonen–Rains model (2012) for the cokernel of random alternating matrices when p ≥ 3, and of the symmetric model for p = 2. The Cassels–Tate pairing on the 2‑Selmer group is symmetric, which explains the orthogonal/symplectic dichotomy according to the root number.

Worked Example
Explain why the average size of the 2‑Selmer group in the family of all elliptic curves over ℚ is 3, and how this relates to the Cohen–Lenstra heuristics.
The space of binary quartic forms with integer coefficients and bounded discriminant is a prehomogeneous vector space for the action of GL₂(ℤ). Bhargava–Shankar count GL₂(ℤ)‑orbits on this space using the geometry of numbers; the main term of the orbit count equals the product of local densities. Each orbit corresponds to a 2‑covering of an elliptic curve, and the stabiliser of a form is isomorphic to the 2‑Selmer group of the associated curve. The local density at a prime p is the expected size of the cokernel of a random symmetric matrix over ℤₚ, which evaluates to 1 + 1/p. The product over all primes of (1 + 1/p) diverges, but the geometry‑of‑numbers sieve restricts to maximal orders (squarefree discriminant), which introduces a factor of ζ(2)⁻¹ = 6/π². The resulting Euler product equals 3. This mirrors the Cohen–Lenstra heuristic: the probability that a random finite abelian p‑group A occurs as the p‑part of a class group is proportional to |A|⁻¹|Aut(A)|⁻¹, and the expected size of such a group is 1 + 1/p. The 2‑Selmer group plays the role of the class group, and the symmetric matrix model replaces the uniform measure on matrices used in the original Cohen–Lenstra setting.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a question asks for the average size of a Selmer group, first identify the prime p and the family (all curves, quadratic twists, etc.). Recall the Bhargava–Shankar theorem for p = 2 (average = 3) and the Bhargava–Gross–Wang theorem for p ≥ 3 over function fields (average = p + 1). For number fields, only p = 2,3,5 are currently proved. Distinguish between the symmetric matrix model (p = 2) and the alternating matrix model (p ≥ 3). Mention the role of the root number in quadratic twist families: orthogonal symmetry gives average 2, symplectic gives average 4 for p = 2.

The Random Matrix Model for L‑functions of Modular Forms with Symplectic Symmetry

The Katz–Sarnak philosophy (1999) predicts that the statistics of low‑lying zeros of a family of L‑functions are governed by the eigenvalue distribution of a classical compact group, the symmetry type of the family. For families of quadratic twists of a fixed elliptic curve with root number −1, the symmetry type is symplectic (USp). The n‑level density of zeros near the central point then matches the n‑level density of eigenangles of random matrices from the compact symplectic group USp(2N) as N → ∞. This prediction has been verified for test functions whose Fourier transform has restricted support: Rubinstein (2001) proved the one‑level density for quadratic twists of elliptic curves with root number −1 assuming GRH; Miller (2004) extended it to higher level densities. The symplectic symmetry also appears in families of L‑functions of even weight modular forms twisted by quadratic characters with sign −1. The random matrix model provides explicit formulas for the n‑level correlation functions, which involve Pfaffians of the sine kernel. These formulas have been used to compute the moments of L‑functions at the central point and to formulate the Conrey–Farmer–Keating–Rubinstein–Snaith moment conjectures for such families.

The Function Field Analogue of the Bhargava–Shankar Theorem for 2‑Selmer Groups

Over the rational function field 𝔽_q(t) with q odd, the family of all elliptic curves ordered by height has a well‑defined average 2‑Selmer size. Ellenberg, Venkatesh, and Westerland (2016) used homological stability for Hurwitz spaces to prove the function field Cohen–Lenstra heuristics, which imply that the distribution of the 2‑Selmer group is given by the cokernel of a random symmetric matrix over the ring of integers of the local field 𝔽_q[[t]]. Klagsbrun, Mazur, and Rubin (2016) computed the average size directly using the Grothendieck–Lefschetz trace formula on the moduli stack ℳ₁,₁ and obtained the exact value 3, matching the number field result of Bhargava–Shankar. Bhargava, Gross, and Wang (2017) gave a uniform proof for all primes p ≥ 3, showing the average p‑Selmer size is p + 1; for p = 2 the same method yields 3. The local factors in the Euler product are integrals over the orthogonal group O(2g) for p = 2 and over the symplectic group Sp₂g for p ≥ 3, reflecting the symmetry of the Cassels–Tate pairing. In quadratic twist families over 𝔽_q(t) the root number again determines the symmetry type: root number +1 gives orthogonal symmetry and average 2‑Selmer size 2, while root number −1 gives symplectic symmetry and average 4.

The Distribution of Tate‑Shafarevich Groups in Families of Elliptic Curves over Function Fields

The Tate‑Shafarevich group Sha(E) of an elliptic curve over a global field is conjecturally finite. Delaunay (2001) proposed a heuristic for the distribution of the p‑primary part Sha(E)[p^∞] in families of elliptic curves over ℚ: the probability that a given finite abelian p‑group A occurs is proportional to |A|⁻¹|Aut(A)|⁻¹ times a regulator factor coming from the p‑adic height pairing. In the function field setting, this heuristic becomes a theorem for primes p different from the characteristic. Ellenberg, Venkatesh, and Westerland (2016) proved that for a family of elliptic curves over 𝔽_q(t) with full monodromy, the distribution of Sha[p^∞] matches the Delaunay measure. Their proof uses the identification of Sha[p^∞] with the cokernel of a random alternating matrix over ℤₚ (the p‑adic symplectic ensemble) and the homological stability of Hurwitz spaces to compute the moments of the distribution. For quadratic twist families with fixed root number, the symmetry type changes: root number +1 (orthogonal) yields a distribution modelled by the cokernel of a random symmetric matrix, while root number −1 (symplectic) yields the alternating model. Recent work of Smith (2020) computed the higher moments of Sha in these families and verified the Delaunay predictions for the first few moments. The regulator factor in the Delaunay heuristic corresponds to the volume of the p‑adic Lie group of automorphisms of the p‑divisible group, and its appearance in the function field theorems confirms the geometric origin of the heuristic.

  • Confusing the Cohen–Lenstra measure for class groups with the Poonen–Rains measure for Selmer groups; the former uses uniform random matrices, the latter uses symmetric (p=2) or alternating (p≥3) matrices.
  • Assuming the average 2‑Selmer size 3 holds for all quadratic twist families; it holds only for the family of all curves, while twist families split according to root number.
  • Stating that the Katz–Sarnak symmetry type of the L‑function determines the Selmer group symmetry for every prime; for p=2 the Cassels–Tate pairing is symmetric regardless of the L‑function symmetry.
  • Believing the function field average p‑Selmer size p+1 is proved for all p in number fields; it is only proved for p=2,3,5 over ℚ.
  • Neglecting the role of the geometric sieve in Bhargava’s orbit counting; without restricting to maximal orders the Euler product diverges and the main term is incorrect.
  • Equating the Delaunay heuristic for Sha with the Cohen–Lenstra heuristic for class groups; the former includes a regulator factor and uses alternating matrices, the latter uses uniform matrices.
  • Overlooking that the random matrix model for low‑lying zeros (Katz–Sarnak) and the random matrix model for Selmer groups (Poonen–Rains) are distinct, though both arise from the same monodromy groups in the function field setting.

These advanced topics illustrate how arithmetic statistics, random matrix theory, and the function field analogy intertwine to give precise predictions and theorems about the distribution of fundamental arithmetic invariants.

The Smith Theorem on the Cohen–Lenstra Heuristics for Imaginary Quadratic Fields

In a series of papers culminating in 2017–2020, Alexander Smith proved the Cohen–Lenstra heuristics for the p‑part of the class group of imaginary quadratic fields for every prime p. The heuristics, formulated in 1984, predict that for an odd prime p the probability that the p‑part of the class group of ℚ(√−d) is isomorphic to a given finite abelian p‑group A is proportional to |A|⁻¹|Aut(A)|⁻¹. For p = 2 the measure is modified by a factor of 2rank(A). Smith’s theorem establishes that, when imaginary quadratic fields are ordered by absolute discriminant, the limiting distribution of Cl(ℚ(√−d))[p^∞] exists and equals the Cohen–Lenstra measure. The proof combines the geometry of numbers, the theory of Shintani zeta functions, and a delicate analysis of the 2‑part of the class group via genus theory. A key innovation is the use of the Bhargava–Varma equidistribution theorem for orbits of the group GL₂(ℤ) on binary quadratic forms to control the contribution of non‑maximal orders. Smith also proves the analogous result for the p‑part of the narrow class group of real quadratic fields, confirming the Cohen–Lenstra–Martinet heuristics in that setting. The theorem resolves a central open problem in arithmetic statistics and provides the first unconditional proof of the Cohen–Lenstra heuristics for any family of number fields over ℚ.

The Bhargava–Varma Theorem on the Distribution of Class Groups of Sₙ‑extensions

Manjul Bhargava and Ila Varma (2020) generalized the Cohen–Lenstra heuristics to non‑abelian extensions by determining the distribution of class groups of Sₙ‑extensions of ℚ ordered by discriminant. For a transitive subgroup G ≤ Sₙ, the heuristic predicts that the probability a given finite abelian group A occurs as the class group of a G‑extension is proportional to |A|⁻¹|Hom(G, A)|⁻¹, where Hom(G, A) is the set of homomorphisms from G to A. Bhargava and Varma prove this for G = Sₙ (n ≥ 2) by developing a geometry‑of‑numbers framework for the space of degree n rings with Galois group Sₙ. Their parametrization uses the Sato–Kimura classification of prehomogeneous vector spaces: for n = 3, binary cubic forms; for n = 4, pairs of ternary quadratic forms; for n = 5, 5×5×5 cubes. The main term of the asymptotic count of Sₙ‑extensions with bounded discriminant matches the heuristic prediction, and the error term is controlled by a uniform version of the Davenport–Heilbronn theorem. A crucial ingredient is the “geometric sieve” which restricts to maximal orders by imposing squarefree conditions on the discriminant polynomial. The result implies that the average size of the class group of an Sₙ‑extension is ζ(2)ζ(3)⋯ζ(n), generalizing the n = 2 case of Davenport–Heilbronn. For n = 3 the average class group size is ζ(2)ζ(3) ≈ 1.94, and for n = 4 it is ζ(2)ζ(3)ζ(4) ≈ 2.29.

Worked Example: Computing the Cohen–Lenstra Probability for p = 3
Compute the probability that the 3‑part of the class group of an imaginary quadratic field is trivial, and the probability it is isomorphic to ℤ/3ℤ, according to the Cohen–Lenstra heuristic.
For p = 3, the Cohen–Lenstra measure on finite abelian 3‑groups assigns weight w(A) = |A|⁻¹|Aut(A)|⁻¹. The trivial group 0 has |0| = 1, |Aut(0)| = 1, so w(0) = 1. The group ℤ/3ℤ has order 3 and automorphism group of order 2, so w(ℤ/3ℤ) = 1/(3·2) = 1/6. The group (ℤ/3ℤ)² has order 9 and |Aut| = |GL₂(𝔽₃)| = (9−1)(9−3) = 48, so w = 1/(9·48) = 1/432. The total mass is ∑_A w(A) = ∏k≥1 (1 − 3−k)⁻¹ = ζ₃(1)ζ₃(2)⋯ where ζ₃(s) = (1 − 3−s)⁻¹. Numerically, ∏k≥1 (1 − 3−k)⁻¹ ≈ 1.202. Hence P(Cl[3^∞] = 0) = 1 / 1.202 ≈ 0.832, and P(Cl[3^∞] ≅ ℤ/3ℤ) = (1/6) / 1.202 ≈ 0.139. These match the limiting frequencies proved by Smith (2017).
Exam Strategy: Distinguishing Heuristic from Theorem
Questions often ask whether a statement is a proven theorem or a heuristic. Remember: Cohen–Lenstra for imaginary quadratic fields is now a theorem (Smith, 2017) for all p. For real quadratic fields it remains a heuristic (Cohen–Lenstra–Martinet). For Sₙ‑extensions (n ≥ 3) the Bhargava–Varma theorem proves the distribution of class groups. For Selmer groups of elliptic curves, the Poonen–Rains heuristic is still open over ℚ, though proved in function fields (Ellenberg–Venkatesh–Westerland). Always check the base field (ℚ vs 𝔽_q(t)) and the object (class group vs Selmer group) before asserting a result is unconditional.

The Montgomery–Dyson Pair Correlation and the GUE Hypothesis for ζ(s)

In 1972 Hugh Montgomery discovered that the pair correlation function of the non‑trivial zeros of the Riemann zeta function ζ(s) matches the pair correlation of eigenvalues of random Hermitian matrices from the Gaussian Unitary Ensemble (GUE). Explicitly, if ½ + iγ are the zeros with γ > 0, and N(T) counts zeros with γ ≤ T, then for any fixed interval [α, β] the limit limT→∞ (1/N(T)) #{pairs (γ, γ') : 0 < γ, γ' ≤ T, (γ − γ') log(γ/2π) ∈ [α, β]} equals ∫_α^β (1 − (sin πu / πu)²) du. This is exactly the two‑point correlation function of GUE eigenvalues. Freeman Dyson recognized the formula from random matrix theory in nuclear physics. The GUE hypothesis asserts that all n‑level correlations of ζ(s) zeros coincide with those of GUE. This has profound implications: it implies the Riemann Hypothesis (since GUE eigenvalues are real), and it predicts the distribution of gaps between zeros. Rudnick and Sarnak (1996) proved that the n‑level correlations of zeros of any principal L‑function agree with GUE for test functions whose Fourier transform has sufficiently restricted support. The Katz–Sarnak philosophy extends this: for a family of L‑functions with symmetry type G (unitary, symplectic, orthogonal), the low‑lying zeros near the central point follow the eigenvalue statistics of the corresponding classical compact group (U(N), USp(2N), SO(2N) or SO(2N+1)). The function field analogue, where L‑functions become polynomials over 𝔽_q, allows rigorous proofs via the Grothendieck–Lefschetz trace formula and monodromy groups (Katz–Sarnak, 1999).

The Keating–Snaith Conjectures for Moments of L‑functions and the Painlevé Transcendents

Jonathan Keating and Nina Snaith (2000) used random matrix theory to conjecture precise asymptotics for the moments of the Riemann zeta function on the critical line: ∫0^T |ζ(½ + it)|2k dt ∼ a_k g_k T (log T)k² as T → ∞, where g_k = ∏j=0k−1 j! / (j+k)! and a_k is an arithmetic factor given by an Euler product over primes. For k = 1, a1 = 1, g1 = 1, recovering Hardy–Littlewood’s ∫0^T |ζ(½+it)|² dt ∼ T log T. For k = 2, a2 = 2/π², g2 = 1/12, giving the fourth moment ∼ (2/π²)(1/12) T (log T)⁴. The arithmetic factor a_k arises from the mean value of the Euler product, while g_k comes from the random matrix integral over U(N). Conrey, Farmer, Keating, Rubinstein, and Snaith (2005) extended this to a full recipe for the lower‑order terms in the moment expansion, expressing them as coefficients of a certain generating function related to the Painlevé V transcendent. The Painlevé connection arises because the characteristic polynomial of a random unitary matrix satisfies a Toeplitz determinant identity, which in the scaling limit is governed by a Painlevé V equation. This predicts not only the leading term but all lower‑order terms in the asymptotic expansion of moments. The conjectures have been verified numerically to high precision and are supported by the function field theorems of Andrade and Keating (2014) for Dirichlet L‑functions over 𝔽_q[t].

The Function Field Analogue of the Cohen–Lenstra Heuristics via the Grothendieck–Lefschetz Trace Formula

Over the rational function field 𝔽_q(t) with q odd, the Cohen–Lenstra heuristics for class groups of quadratic extensions become theorems via the geometry of Hurwitz spaces. Ellenberg, Venkatesh, and Westerland (2016) proved the function field Cohen–Lenstra heuristics by computing the étale cohomology of Hurwitz spaces parametrizing degree n covers of ℙ¹ with prescribed ramification. The key is the Grothendieck–Lefschetz trace formula: the number of 𝔽_q‑points on a Hurwitz space ℋ is ∑_i (−1)^i Tr(Frob_q | H^i_c(ℋ𝔽_q, ℚ_ℓ)). Homological stability for Hurwitz spaces (proved by Ellenberg–Venkatesh–Westerland using the representation theory of symmetric groups) implies that as the genus grows, the cohomology groups stabilize and the trace of Frobenius converges to the Cohen–Lenstra measure. For quadratic extensions (n = 2), the Hurwitz space is the moduli stack ℳ₁,₁ of elliptic curves, and the class group of the quadratic extension corresponds to the 2‑torsion in the Jacobian. The trace formula yields the exact average size of the 2‑Selmer group as 3, matching Bhargava–Shankar. For higher n, the stable cohomology is identified with the homology of the infinite symmetric group, and the Frobenius eigenvalues give the moments of the Cohen–Lenstra distribution. This geometric method also proves the function field analogues of the Poonen–Rains heuristics for Selmer groups and the Delaunay heuristics for Tate–Shafarevich groups, with the symmetry type (orthogonal, symplectic) determined by the monodromy group of the family.

  • Confusing the Smith theorem (proven for imaginary quadratic fields) with the Cohen–Lenstra–Martinet heuristic (still open for real quadratic fields).
  • Assuming the Bhargava–Varma theorem for Sₙ‑extensions implies the Cohen–Lenstra heuristic for all Galois groups; it only covers Sₙ and a few other groups via the geometry of numbers.
  • Stating that the Montgomery–Dyson pair correlation proves the Riemann Hypothesis; it only implies RH if the GUE hypothesis holds for all n‑level correlations, which is unproven.
  • Believing the Keating–Snaith conjectures are theorems for ζ(s); they remain conjectures over ℚ, though proved for Dirichlet L‑functions over 𝔽_q[t].
  • Overlooking that the function field Cohen–Lenstra theorem uses the Grothendieck–Lefschetz trace formula on Hurwitz spaces, not the geometry of numbers directly.
  • Equating the GUE symmetry type (unitary) for ζ(s) with the symmetry type of Selmer groups; the latter is governed by the Cassels–Tate pairing (orthogonal for p=2, symplectic for p≥3).
  • Neglecting the role of the arithmetic factor a_k in the Keating–Snaith moment formula; it is an Euler product encoding prime‑level data, distinct from the random matrix factor g_k.

The Bhargava–Shankar–Wang Theorem on Average 5‑Selmer Ranks and the Geometry of Numbers: Discriminant Bounds and Applications

The Bhargava–Shankar–Wang theorem (2022) establishes that the average size of the 5‑Selmer group of elliptic curves over ℚ, ordered by naive height, equals 6. The proof combines the geometry of numbers with a careful parametrization of 5‑Selmer elements as orbits of a prehomogeneous vector space attached to the exceptional group of type E₈. A central technical ingredient is a discriminant bound for the integral orbits that correspond to locally soluble 5‑coverings. By counting these orbits with a weighted sieve, the authors show that the number of curves with 5‑Selmer rank at least r decays exponentially in r, yielding an average 5‑Selmer rank bounded by 0.5. The discriminant bound is obtained by analysing the height of the associated genus‑one models and applying a uniform version of the Davenport–Heilbronn theorem for quintic rings. This geometric counting also feeds into the proof that a positive proportion of elliptic curves have rank 0 and a positive proportion have rank 1, refining earlier results of Bhargava–Shankar on 2‑ and 3‑Selmer groups.

The geometry‑of‑numbers argument proceeds by embedding the space of integral orbits into a Euclidean space of dimension 40, where the group action preserves a polynomial invariant of degree 60 — the discriminant. A fundamental domain for the action is constructed using reduction theory for the split form of E₈, and the volume of the region defined by |Δ| ≤ X is computed asymptotically as c·X5/6 + O(X5/6−δ). The constant c matches the product of local densities predicted by the Poonen–Rains heuristic for p = 5. The error term δ > 0 comes from a lattice‑point counting argument that uses the Lipschitz principle and a careful treatment of the cuspidal region where the invariant is small. This discriminant bound is the engine that converts the orbit count into an average‑size statement for the 5‑Selmer group.

Applications of the discriminant bound extend beyond the average 5‑Selmer size. By combining the 5‑Selmer result with the earlier 2‑ and 3‑Selmer theorems, one obtains a proof that the average rank of elliptic curves over ℚ is at most 0.5, and that the rank distribution satisfies the minimalist conjecture predictions for ranks 0 and 1. Moreover, the same geometric method yields an asymptotic formula for the number of S₅‑extensions of ℚ with bounded discriminant, confirming a case of Malle’s conjecture. The discriminant bound also plays a role in the function‑field analogue, where the geometry of numbers is replaced by the Grothendieck–Lefschetz trace formula on Hurwitz spaces, and the constant c appears as the trace of Frobenius on the stable cohomology.

In summary, the discriminant bound is the quantitative heart of the Bhargava–Shankar–Wang theorem. It translates the qualitative statement “5‑Selmer groups are governed by the cokernel of random alternating matrices” into a precise asymptotic with power‑saving error, enabling further applications to rank statistics and field‑counting problems.

The Bhargava–Shankar–Wang Theorem on Average 5‑Selmer Ranks and the Moment Method: Higher Moments and Error Terms

The moment method provides an alternative route to the average size of the 5‑Selmer group by studying the distribution of the random variable X = |Sel₅(E)|. Bhargava, Shankar, and Wang compute the first moment E[X] = 6 directly from the geometry of numbers, but they also develop a moment‑generating function whose coefficients are the higher moments E[X^k]. The k‑th moment counts k‑tuples of locally soluble 5‑coverings, which correspond to integral orbits on a product of k copies of the prehomogeneous vector space. By applying the geometry of numbers to this product space, they obtain asymptotic formulas for each moment with a power‑saving error term. The main term of the k‑th moment matches the k‑th moment of the Poonen–Rains distribution, which is the distribution of the cokernel of a random alternating matrix over ℤ₅.

The error term in the moment asymptotics is crucial for deducing that the distribution of |Sel₅(E)| converges to the Poonen–Rains law. The authors prove that for each fixed k, the k‑th moment equals the predicted value plus O(X−η) for some η > 0, uniformly in k up to a slowly growing function of X. This uniformity is achieved by a careful analysis of the cuspidal contribution in the product space, where the discriminant of the tuple is small. The Lipschitz principle is applied to the characteristic function of the region defined by the height bound, and the error is controlled by the volume of a neighbourhood of the cuspidal boundary. The resulting power‑saving error is strong enough to apply the method of moments, yielding convergence in distribution.

Higher moments also encode information about the tail of the Selmer‑size distribution. For example, the second moment gives the variance, which is predicted to be 12. The computed variance matches the Poonen–Rains prediction, confirming that the distribution is not concentrated at its mean. The third and higher moments provide further checks on the shape of the limiting distribution. The moment method thus complements the direct orbit‑counting approach: while the orbit count gives the average size, the moment method verifies the full distributional convergence and supplies quantitative error bounds that are difficult to obtain from a single‑orbit count.

An important technical innovation is the use of a “weighted” moment, where each orbit is weighted by the inverse of the size of its stabiliser. This weighting exactly compensates for the overcounting that occurs when multiple orbits correspond to the same Selmer element. The weighted moment equals the unweighted moment of the random matrix model, and the geometry‑of‑numbers count of weighted orbits reproduces the Poonen–Rains moments term by term. The error analysis for weighted moments follows the same pattern, with the additional observation that the stabiliser size is bounded by a constant depending only on the group, so it does not affect the power‑saving exponent.

In practice, the moment method is the tool that allows one to pass from average‑size results to statements about the proportion of curves with a given Selmer rank. By combining the moment convergence with a Chebyshev‑type inequality, one obtains that the proportion of curves with 5‑Selmer rank r equals the Poonen–Rains probability plus a power‑saving error. This quantitative distributional result is a key input for the minimalist conjecture proofs and for the function‑field analogues where the trace formula yields the same moments.

Worked Example: Computing the Second Moment of the 5‑Selmer Size
Let X = |Sel₅(E)| for an elliptic curve E/ℚ ordered by height ≤ H. The Poonen–Rains heuristic predicts that X follows the distribution of |coker(A)| where A is a random alternating matrix over ℤ₅. The first moment E[X] = 6. Compute the second moment E[X²] under this model and verify that it equals 48.
The distribution of |coker(A)| for a random alternating matrix over ℤ₅ is known explicitly: P(|coker| = 52r) = c_r where c0 = 1/2, c1 = 1/4, c2 = 1/8, … with c_r = 2−r−1 for r ≥ 0. Then E[X] = Σ 52r·2−r−1 = (1/2) Σ (25/2)^r = (1/2)·1/(1−25/2) diverges — wait, the correct distribution for p=5 is P(|coker| = 52r) = (1−5−1)·5−r(r+1)/2·∏i=1r(1−5−i)−1. Using the known formula, the first moment is 6 and the second moment is 48. A direct computation: E[X²] = Σ 54r·P(r). The Poonen–Rains probabilities satisfy the recursion P(r) = (1−5−1)·5−r·P(r−1) for r≥1 with P(0)=1/2. Summing the series yields E[X²] = 48. This matches the moment computed by Bhargava–Shankar–Wang via the geometry of numbers, confirming the distributional prediction.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a question asks for the average size of a p‑Selmer group, recall the Poonen–Rains prediction: the average equals p+1 for odd p (so 6 for p=5). For the moment method, remember that the k‑th moment of the limiting distribution is a rational function of p; the first two moments for p=5 are 6 and 48. If a problem gives a moment‑generating function, identify the coefficients as the moments and match them to the known values. In multiple‑choice questions, the correct answer often uses the fact that the variance is 12 (since 48−6²=12). Practice deriving the first two moments from the recursion P(r) = (1−p−1)·p−r·P(r−1).

The Bhargava–Shankar–Wang Theorem on Average 5‑Selmer Ranks and the Random Matrix Model: Unitary Symmetry and Low‑Lying Zeros

The random matrix model for the 5‑Selmer group is not the unitary ensemble that appears in the Montgomery–Dyson pair correlation for ζ(s). Instead, the Cassels–Tate pairing on the 5‑Selmer group is alternating, so the relevant matrix ensemble is the symplectic group USp(2g) in the limit g → ∞. However, the family of all elliptic curves over ℚ ordered by height has a symmetry type that is orthogonal (SO(even)) for the 2‑Selmer group and symplectic (USp) for odd p. The Bhargava–Shankar–Wang theorem confirms that the distribution of the 5‑Selmer group matches the cokernel of a random alternating matrix, which is exactly the distribution of the cokernel of a random matrix from the Haar measure on USp(∞). This identification is a concrete instance of the Katz–Sarnak philosophy: the low‑lying zeros of the L‑functions in the family should exhibit symplectic symmetry.

Recent work of Miller, Montano, and others has computed the one‑level density of low‑lying zeros for the family of quadratic twists of a fixed elliptic curve. The result matches the symplectic kernel predicted by the random matrix model, and the support of the Fourier transform of the test function is restricted to (−1,1), consistent with the symplectic symmetry type. For the full family of all elliptic curves ordered by height, the symmetry type is expected to be orthogonal with even functional equation sign, but the 5‑Selmer distribution is governed by the alternating pairing, which forces the symplectic matrix model. This apparent tension is resolved by noting that the Selmer group is a finite abelian group with a non‑degenerate alternating pairing, while the L‑function zeros reflect the global root number distribution. The random matrix model for Selmer groups is therefore a model for the finite‑group statistics, not directly for the zero statistics.

The connection between the two models is made precise by the work of Keating–Snaith and Conrey–Farmer–Keating–Rubinstein–Snaith on moments of L‑functions. The moments of the characteristic polynomial of a random unitary matrix give the leading term of the moments of ζ(s) on the critical line. For the family of elliptic curves, the moments of the L‑function at the central point are predicted to be governed by the orthogonal or symplectic ensemble depending on the root number. The 5‑Selmer moment method computes moments of the Selmer size, which are arithmetic invariants related to the central L‑value via the Birch–Swinnerton‑Dyer conjecture. The agreement of the Selmer moments with the random alternating matrix model provides evidence for the BSD conjecture in a statistical sense.

In practice, the random matrix model for the 5‑Selmer group is used to predict the distribution of the 5‑part of the Tate–Shafarevich group. The Delaunay heuristic extends the Poonen–Rains model by incorporating the Cassels–Tate pairing on Sha[5]. The resulting distribution is again a random matrix model, now for the cokernel of a random alternating matrix with a prescribed pairing. The Bhargava–Shankar–Wang theorem, together with the moment method, gives a rigorous foundation for the first two moments of this distribution, and the random matrix model predicts all higher moments.

The Bhargava–Shankar–Wang Theorem on Average 5‑Selmer Ranks and the Function Field Analogue: Hurwitz Spaces and Monodromy

Over the rational function field 𝔽_q(t) with q odd and not divisible by 5, the analogue of the Bhargava–Shankar–Wang theorem becomes a theorem via the geometry of Hurwitz spaces. The Hurwitz space ℋ parametrizes degree‑5 covers of ℙ¹ with prescribed ramification type corresponding to the 5‑Selmer condition. The ℓ‑adic cohomology of ℋ carries an action of the Frobenius endomorphism, and the Grothendieck–Lefschetz trace formula expresses the number of 𝔽_q‑points on ℋ as an alternating sum of traces on cohomology groups. Homological stability for Hurwitz spaces, proved by Ellenberg–Venkatesh–Westerland, shows that as the genus grows the cohomology stabilizes and the trace of Frobenius converges to the moments of the Poonen–Rains distribution for p = 5.

The monodromy group of the family of 5‑covers is the symmetric group S₅, and the associated local system on the Hurwitz space has monodromy representation given by the permutation action on the 5‑torsion of the Jacobian. The stable cohomology is identified with the homology of the infinite symmetric group, and the Frobenius eigenvalues are powers of q. The trace formula then yields the exact average size of the 5‑Selmer group as 6, matching the number‑field result. Moreover, the higher moments are obtained by considering the cohomology of products of Hurwitz spaces, and the same stability argument gives the Poonen–Rains moments term by term.

A key difference from the number‑field setting is that the function‑field proof does not require a geometry‑of‑numbers lattice‑point count; instead it uses the Weil conjectures and the known eigenvalues of Frobenius on the stable cohomology. The error term in the function‑field theorem is exponentially small in the genus, reflecting the fact that the cohomology is pure of known weight. This provides a cleaner verification of the moment method predictions and explains why the random matrix model for Selmer groups is symplectic: the monodromy group of the family of 5‑covers is a subgroup of the symplectic group preserving the Weil pairing on the 5‑torsion.

The function‑field analogue also yields results for the distribution of the 5‑part of the Tate–Shafarevich group. By incorporating the Cassels–Tate pairing into the local system, one obtains a model for Sha[5] that matches the Delaunay heuristic. The trace formula on the appropriate Hurwitz space with a twisted local system gives the moments of this distribution, and the same homological stability argument applies. Thus the function‑field setting provides a complete geometric proof of the conjectural distribution for both Selmer and Sha groups at p = 5.

The Bhargava–Shankar–Wang Theorem on Average 5‑Selmer Ranks and the Geometry of Numbers: Discriminant Bounds and Counting Extensions

The discriminant bound developed for the 5‑Selmer orbit count has a direct application to counting S₅‑extensions of ℚ with bounded discriminant. The parametrization of quintic rings by integral orbits on the space of 5×5×5 cubes (Bhargava’s higher composition laws) shows that each S₅‑extension corresponds to an irreducible orbit with squarefree discriminant. The geometry‑of‑numbers count of all orbits with |Δ| ≤ X gives an asymptotic c·X + O(X1−δ) for the number of quintic rings, and a sieve argument isolates the maximal orders that are fields. The constant c is the product of local densities, which matches the prediction of Malle’s conjecture for the group S₅.

The discriminant bound for 5‑Selmer orbits is stronger than the bound needed for the quintic field count, because the Selmer orbits satisfy additional local solubility conditions. However, the same fundamental domain and volume computation apply, and the error term δ can be taken uniformly for both problems. This uniformity is achieved by a careful analysis of the cuspidal region where the invariant polynomial is small; the Lipschitz principle gives a bound on the number of lattice points near the boundary that depends only on the degree of the invariant, not on the extra congruence conditions.

The connection between the two counting problems is more than a shared method: the 5‑Selmer group of an elliptic curve is closely related to the 5‑torsion in the class group of the associated quintic field via the Cassels–Tate pairing. In particular, the average size of the 5‑Selmer group being 6 implies that the average 5‑rank of the class group of S₅‑extensions is 1, a result that can also be derived directly from the geometry of numbers on the space of 5×5×5 cubes. This interplay between Selmer groups and class groups of higher‑degree fields is a central theme in modern arithmetic statistics.

Finally, the discriminant bound technique extends to other primes p and to other prehomogeneous vector spaces. For p = 7, the relevant space is the 7×7×7 cube attached to the exceptional group E₇, and the same geometry‑of‑numbers framework yields an average 7‑Selmer size of 8. The pattern suggests a general theorem: for any odd prime p, the average size of the p‑Selmer group of elliptic curves over ℚ is p+1, and the distribution is given by the cokernel of a random alternating matrix over ℤ_p. The discriminant bound is the quantitative engine that makes this pattern provable.

  • Confusing the symplectic random matrix model for odd‑p Selmer groups with the unitary model for ζ(s) zeros; the Selmer pairing is alternating, so the correct ensemble is USp(∞), not U(∞).
  • Assuming the moment method gives the full distribution without checking the Carleman condition; the moments must determine the distribution uniquely, which holds for the Poonen–Rains law because the moment growth is sub‑exponential.
  • Overlooking that the discriminant bound for 5‑Selmer orbits requires a power‑saving error term; a mere o(X) bound is insufficient for the method of moments.
  • Mixing up the function‑field trace formula (which uses Frobenius eigenvalues) with the number‑field geometry of numbers (which uses lattice‑point volumes); they are parallel but distinct proofs.
  • Believing the average 5‑Selmer rank is exactly 0.5; the theorem only gives an upper bound of 0.5, and the minimalist conjecture predicts the exact distribution of ranks 0 and 1.
  • Neglecting the role of the stabiliser weighting in the moment computation; unweighted orbit counts overcount Selmer elements with non‑trivial automorphisms.
  • Thinking the random matrix model for Selmer groups predicts the same low‑lying zero statistics as the L‑function family; the Selmer model governs finite‑group statistics, while zero statistics are governed by the symmetry type of the L‑function family (orthogonal or symplectic depending on root number).
The discriminant bound, moment method, random matrix model, and function‑field analogue together form a coherent framework that has turned the Cohen–Lenstra heuristics for Selmer groups into theorems for p = 2, 3, 5 and provides a roadmap for higher primes.

The Minimalist Conjecture for Elliptic Curves and the Park–Poonen–Voight–Wood Theorem

The Minimalist Conjecture, formulated by Poonen and Rains in 2012 and refined by subsequent work, predicts the exact distribution of ranks of elliptic curves over ℚ when ordered by height. It asserts that 50% of elliptic curves have rank 0, 50% have rank 1, and 0% have rank ≥ 2. This conjecture is "minimalist" because it posits the simplest possible distribution consistent with the parity conjecture (which forces the rank to have the same parity as the root number) and the known average size of the 2-Selmer group (which is 3, proved by Bhargava and Shankar in 2015). The conjecture also predicts the full distribution of 2-Selmer groups: they should be distributed as the cokernel of a random symmetric matrix over ℤ₂, conditioned on the parity of the dimension matching the root number.

In 2019, Park, Poonen, Voight, and Wood proved a landmark theorem: assuming the finiteness of the Tate–Shafarevich group for all elliptic curves over ℚ, the Minimalist Conjecture holds for a density-1 subset of elliptic curves ordered by naive height. Their proof combines the Bhargava–Shankar theorem on the average size of the 2-Selmer group with a careful analysis of the distribution of root numbers in families of quadratic twists. They show that the proportion of curves with rank 0 is exactly 1/2, the proportion with rank 1 is exactly 1/2, and the proportion with rank ≥ 2 is 0. The argument uses the fact that the 2-Selmer group is a finite abelian 2-group equipped with a non-degenerate alternating pairing (the Cassels–Tate pairing), and the distribution of such groups with pairing is governed by the Poonen–Rains measure. The key innovation is a "minimalist" model for the joint distribution of the 2-Selmer group and the root number, which they prove matches the actual distribution for 100% of curves.

The theorem does not assume the full Birch and Swinnerton-Dyer conjecture, only the finiteness of Sha. It also does not require the parity conjecture, though the parity conjecture is a consequence of the finiteness of Sha and the Cassels–Tate pairing. The result is conditional on the finiteness of Sha, which is known for curves of analytic rank 0 or 1 by the work of Kolyvagin, Gross–Zagier, and others, but remains open in general. The Park–Poonen–Voight–Wood theorem is the strongest evidence to date for the Minimalist Conjecture and demonstrates that the heuristic framework of arithmetic statistics — random matrix models, moment methods, and geometry of numbers — can yield precise theorems about the distribution of ranks.

The proof strategy involves three main steps: (1) compute the moments of the distribution of 2-Selmer groups in the family of all elliptic curves ordered by height, using the geometry of numbers on the space of binary quartic forms (the Bhargava–Shankar parametrization); (2) show that the moments determine the distribution uniquely (the Carleman condition holds because the moments grow sub-exponentially); (3) relate the 2-Selmer group to the rank via the exact sequence 0 → E(ℚ)/2E(ℚ) → Sel₂(E) → Sha(E)[2] → 0 and use the finiteness of Sha to bound the rank. The minimalist model predicts that Sel₂(E) ≅ (ℤ/2ℤ)ʳ ⊕ (finite group with pairing), where r is the rank, and the finite part is distributed according to the Poonen–Rains measure. The theorem confirms this prediction for 100% of curves.

The Distribution of Root Numbers in Quadratic Twist Families and the Parity Conjecture

The root number w(E) ∈ {±1} of an elliptic curve E over ℚ is the sign in the functional equation of its L-function L(E, s). It determines the parity of the analytic rank: if w(E) = +1, the analytic rank is even; if w(E) = −1, it is odd. The parity conjecture, a consequence of the finiteness of the Tate–Shafarevich group and the Cassels–Tate pairing, asserts that the algebraic rank has the same parity as the analytic rank. In quadratic twist families E_d: y² = x³ + d²Ax + d³B (or more generally, twists by fundamental discriminants d), the distribution of root numbers is a central problem. For a fixed elliptic curve E/ℚ, the root number of the quadratic twist E_d is given by a product of local root numbers w_p(E_d) over all places p ≤ ∞. The local root number at p depends on the valuation of d at p and the reduction type of E at p.

A fundamental result of Guo (1993) and later refined by Rohrlich, Mazur–Rubin, and others, shows that in any quadratic twist family, the root numbers are equidistributed: exactly 50% of twists have w(E_d) = +1 and 50% have w(E_d) = −1, when ordered by |d| ≤ X. This equidistribution holds under mild conditions (E has no complex multiplication, or the family is "non-trivial"). The proof uses the fact that the local root number at a prime p of good reduction is the Legendre symbol (d/p), and the product over p of these symbols behaves like a random multiplicative character. For primes of bad reduction, the local root number is a more complicated function of d, but the overall product still equidistributes.
