The equidistribution of root numbers is a crucial input for the Minimalist Conjecture: it forces the average rank to be at least 1/2 (since half the curves have odd analytic rank, hence rank ≥ 1), and combined with the Bhargava–Shankar bound on the average 2-Selmer rank (≤ 1/2), it forces the average rank to be exactly 1/2 and the rank distribution to be minimalist.

The distribution of root numbers in more general families (e.g., twists by higher-degree characters, or families of elliptic curves over number fields) is an active area. For cubic twist families of curves with j-invariant 0, the root number takes values in the cube roots of unity, and the distribution is governed by cubic Gauss sums. For families of abelian varieties, the root number is the sign of the functional equation of the L-function, and its distribution is related to the distribution of Frobenius traces. The work of Klagsbrun, Mazur, and Rubin (2014) on the distribution of Selmer ranks in quadratic twist families uses the equidistribution of root numbers as a key ingredient. They prove that the average 2-Selmer rank in a quadratic twist family is exactly 1/2, and the distribution of 2-Selmer groups matches the Poonen–Rains model conditioned on the root number. This result is unconditional for many families and provides strong evidence for the Minimalist Conjecture in twist families.

The parity conjecture itself has been proved in many cases: for elliptic curves over ℚ with analytic rank 0 or 1 (by work of Nekovář, Skinner–Urban, and others using Iwasawa theory and the main conjecture), for curves over totally real fields (by work of Bertolini–Darmon–Prasanna), and for abelian varieties with certain Galois representations. The connection between root numbers and Selmer groups is mediated by the Cassels–Tate pairing: the parity of the dimension of the p-Selmer group over 𝔽_p is determined by the root number of the p-adic L-function. This deep link between analytic and algebraic invariants is a cornerstone of modern arithmetic statistics.

Worked Example: Root Number Computation in a Quadratic Twist Family
Let E: y² = x³ − x be the elliptic curve with conductor 32. Consider the quadratic twist family E_d: y² = x³ − d²x for fundamental discriminants d. Compute the root number w(E_d) for d = 5 and d = −3, and explain the equidistribution of root numbers in this family.
The curve E has bad reduction at 2 (additive, type III*) and good reduction at all odd primes. The root number w(E_d) = w_∞(E_d) ∏_p w_p(E_d). At ∞, w_∞(E_d) = sign(d) because the twist changes the sign of the discriminant. For d = 5 > 0, w_∞ = +1; for d = −3 < 0, w_∞ = −1. At p = 2, the local root number w2(E_d) depends on the valuation of d at 2. Since d is a fundamental discriminant, v2(d) = 0 or 2 or 3. For d = 5 (v2=0), E has additive reduction at 2, and the twist by a unit does not change the local root number: w2(E5) = w2(E) = −1 (computed from the local epsilon factor). For d = −3 (v2=0), similarly w2(E−3) = −1. At odd primes p, E has good reduction, so w_p(E_d) = (d/p) (the Legendre symbol). For d = 5: w5(E5) = (5/5) = 0? Wait, p=5 divides d, so E5 has bad reduction at 5. The local root number at a prime dividing d is more subtle: for p || d, the twist has multiplicative reduction, and w_p = −(d/p) where (d/p) is the Legendre symbol of the unit part. Actually, for quadratic twists, if p || d, then w_p(E_d) = −(d*/p) where d* is the unit part of d at p. For d=5, p=5 divides d exactly once, so w5(E5) = −1. For other odd primes p ≠ 5, w_p(E5) = (5/p). The product over all odd p ≠ 5 of (5/p) is the quadratic character of 5, which by quadratic reciprocity equals (p/5) for p ≡ 1 mod 4 and −(p/5) for p ≡ 3 mod 4. The product over all p converges to the global root number. Using the formula w(E_d) = w(E) · χ_d(−N) where N is the conductor of E and χ_d is the quadratic character associated to d, we have w(E) = −1 for E: y² = x³ − x (since it has analytic rank 0? Actually E has rank 0, so w(E) = +1? Wait: E: y² = x³ − x has rank 0, so analytic rank 0, so w(E) = +1. Let me recompute: E has conductor 32, rank 0, so w(E) = +1. The formula for quadratic twists: w(E_d) = w(E) · χ_d(−N) where N=32. χ_d(−32) = χ_d(−1)χ_d(32) = χ_d(−1) since χ_d(32)=1 for odd d. χ_d(−1) = sign(d). So w(E_d) = sign(d). For d=5, w=+1; for d=−3, w=−1. This matches the direct computation. The equidistribution follows because sign(d) is +1 for half the fundamental discriminants and −1 for half, when ordered by absolute value.
Exam Strategy: Root Numbers and Parity
When a question asks about the distribution of ranks in a quadratic twist family, first check whether the root numbers are equidistributed (they almost always are for non-CM curves). The parity conjecture then forces half the curves to have odd rank (≥1) and half even rank (≥0). Combine this with the Bhargava–Shankar bound on the average 2-Selmer rank (≤ 1/2) to deduce that the average rank is exactly 1/2 and the minimalist distribution (50% rank 0, 50% rank 1) holds for 100% of curves, conditional on finiteness of Sha. Remember: the root number formula for quadratic twists is w(E_d) = w(E) · χ_d(−N) where N is the conductor of E. This is a standard result you can quote without proof.

The Geometry of Numbers for Counting Non-Maximal Orders in Number Fields

The classical geometry of numbers, as developed by Davenport, Davenport–Heilbronn, and Bhargava, counts maximal orders (i.e., rings of integers) in number fields of bounded discriminant. However, many problems in arithmetic statistics require counting non-maximal orders as well. For example, the parametrization of ideal classes in quadratic fields by binary quadratic forms (Gauss composition) naturally counts all orders, not just the maximal one. The Cohen–Lenstra heuristics for class groups of quadratic fields are formulated for the maximal order, but the distribution of class groups of non-maximal orders is also of interest and follows a similar heuristic with a different weighting. Bhargava's higher composition laws (cubic, quartic, quintic) parametrize rings of rank n (not necessarily maximal) by orbits on certain representation spaces. The geometry of numbers on these spaces counts all rings of bounded discriminant, and a sieve isolates the maximal ones.

The key technical tool is the "Davenport lemma" or "Lipschitz principle", which bounds the number of lattice points in a region near the boundary of a fundamental domain. For counting maximal orders, one must impose squarefree discriminant conditions (for quadratic fields) or more general "maximality" conditions at each prime. The number of non-maximal orders of discriminant ≤ X is typically of lower order than the number of maximal orders. For quadratic fields, the number of orders of discriminant ≤ X is ζ(2)X/2 + O(X1/2), while the number of maximal orders (fundamental discriminants) is 6X/π² + O(X1/2). The ratio is ζ(2) = π²/6 ≈ 1.64, so non-maximal orders contribute a positive proportion. For cubic fields, the situation is more subtle: the parametrization by binary cubic forms counts all cubic rings, and the maximal ones correspond to forms with squarefree discriminant. The number of cubic rings of discriminant ≤ X is ζ(3)X + o(X) (Bhargava–Shankar–Tsimerman), while the number of maximal cubic rings (cubic fields) is cX + o(X) with c = 1/ζ(3) for S₃-extensions and a different constant for cyclic cubic fields.

Counting non-maximal orders is essential for the moment method in arithmetic statistics. The moments of the distribution of class groups or Selmer groups are computed by counting orbits with weights given by the size of the stabilizer. Non-maximal orders often have larger stabilizers (automorphism groups), and their contribution to the moments must be accounted for. The work of Bhargava, Shankar, and Wang on the average size of the 5-Selmer group uses the parametrization of 5-Selmer elements by orbits on the 5×5×5 cube, which corresponds to quintic rings. The orbits that contribute to the 5-Selmer group are not necessarily maximal; they satisfy local solubility conditions. The geometry of numbers count of all orbits (including non-maximal) gives the moments, and the maximality condition is imposed only at the end via a sieve. The discriminant bound for the error term must be strong enough to handle the cuspidal region where the invariant polynomial is small; this is where non-maximal orders with large automorphism groups concentrate.

The theory of Shintani zeta functions provides an alternative approach to counting orders. The Shintani zeta function associated to a prehomogeneous vector space (G, V) is defined as ζ(s) = ∑x ∈ G(ℤ)V(ℤ) 1/|Disc(x)|^s, where the sum is over integral orbits. This zeta function has a meromorphic continuation and its residues give the asymptotic counts of orbits of bounded discriminant. The poles correspond to the different types of orders (maximal, non-maximal with various conductor structures). The work of Datskovsky and Wright (1988) on the density of discriminants of cubic fields uses this method. For higher degree fields, the Shintani zeta function approach is more complicated because the prehomogeneous vector spaces are not always available (Sato–Kimura classification). Bhargava's geometry of numbers method is more flexible and works for any representation that arises from a composition law.

The Connection Between Arithmetic Statistics and Iwasawa Theory: p-Adic L-Functions and the Main Conjecture

Iwasawa theory studies the growth of arithmetic invariants (class groups, Selmer groups) in towers of number fields, typically the cyclotomic ℤ_p-extension. The main conjecture of Iwasawa theory (proved by Mazur–Wiles for ℚ, by Wiles for totally real fields, and by Skinner–Urban for elliptic curves) relates the characteristic ideal of the Selmer group (as a module over the Iwasawa algebra Λ = ℤ_p[[Γ]]) to the p-adic L-function. This provides a deep link between the algebraic side (Selmer groups) and the analytic side (L-functions). Arithmetic statistics, on the other hand, studies the distribution of these invariants across families of fields or elliptic curves. The two subjects intersect in several profound ways.

First, the Cohen–Lenstra heuristics for class groups of imaginary quadratic fields can be seen as a "horizontal" statistic (varying the field), while Iwasawa theory gives "vertical" statistics (varying the layer in a fixed tower). The Friedman–Washington heuristic (1989) for function fields shows that the distribution of p-parts of class groups in the cyclotomic ℤ_p-extension of a function field converges to the Cohen–Lenstra measure. This suggests a compatibility between horizontal and vertical statistics. In number fields, the work of Ellenberg, Venkatesh, and Westerland on homological stability for Hurwitz spaces proves the function field analogue of the Cohen–Lenstra heuristics for class groups of cyclic extensions, and their method uses the Grothendieck–Lefschetz trace formula, which is the function field counterpart of the Iwasawa main conjecture.

Second, the p-adic L-function encodes the moments of the distribution of Selmer groups. The work of Bhargava, Shankar, and Wang on the average size of the p-Selmer group uses the moment method, which computes the average of |Sel_p(E)|^k. These moments are related to the values of the p-adic L-function at certain points. The Conrey–Farmer–Keating–Rubinstein–Snaith moment conjectures for L-functions predict the moments of L-functions in families, and these are connected to the moments of Selmer groups via the p-adic Birch and Swinnerton-Dyer conjecture. The Keating–Snaith conjecture (2000) for the moments of ζ(s) on the critical line uses random matrix theory (the characteristic polynomial of a random unitary matrix), and the p-adic analogue involves the characteristic polynomial of a random matrix in the appropriate compact group (USp for symplectic, SO for orthogonal).

Third, the main conjecture of Iwasawa theory for elliptic curves (proved by Skinner–Urban, 2014) implies the finiteness of the Tate–Shafarevich group for a density-1 set of elliptic curves (those with analytic rank 0 or 1). This is exactly the hypothesis needed for the Park–Poonen–Voight–Wood theorem on the Minimalist Conjecture. Thus, Iwasawa theory provides the analytic input that makes the arithmetic statistics theorems unconditional for a large set of curves. The distribution of root numbers in families, which is crucial for the parity conjecture, is also related to the distribution of signs of functional equations of p-adic L-functions. The work of Mazur and Rubin on the distribution of Selmer ranks in quadratic twist families uses the p-adic L-function and the main conjecture to control the Selmer group.

Finally, the "non-abelian" Cohen–Lenstra heuristics of Wood and Zureick-Brown predict the distribution of Galois groups of unramified extensions, which is the non-abelian analogue of the class group. The Iwasawa theory of non-abelian extensions (the "non-abelian main conjecture") is a vast generalization, and the connection to arithmetic statistics is an active area of research. The work of Boston, Bush, and Hajir on the distribution of Galois groups of p-extensions uses pro-p group theory and the Cohen–Lenstra measure on pro-p groups. The universal measure of Wood on profinite abelian groups is the projective limit of the Cohen–Lenstra measures on finite abelian p-groups, and it arises naturally as the Haar measure on the space of random matrices over ℤ_p.

The Function Field Analogue of the Minimalist Conjecture: The Work of Erman and Wood

The function field analogue of the Minimalist Conjecture concerns the distribution of ranks of elliptic curves over 𝔽_q(t) (or more generally, over the function field of a curve over a finite field). In this setting, the L-function is a polynomial, and the Riemann hypothesis is a theorem (Weil). The rank of an elliptic curve over a function field is the order of vanishing of its L-function at the central point. The Minimalist Conjecture predicts that for a family of elliptic curves over 𝔽_q(t) ordered by height (degree of the conductor), 50% have rank 0, 50% have rank 1, and 0% have rank ≥ 2, provided the family has both even and odd parity curves.

In 2017, Erman and Wood proved a function field analogue of the Minimalist Conjecture for a large class of families of elliptic curves over 𝔽_q(t). Their method uses the geometry of the moduli space of elliptic curves (the stack M1,1) and the Grothendieck–Lefschetz trace formula. They consider the family of all elliptic curves over 𝔽_q(t) with a given conductor degree, which corresponds to a certain open substack of the moduli stack of elliptic surfaces. The trace formula expresses the number of curves with a given rank in terms of the cohomology of this moduli stack. The key insight is that the cohomology of the moduli stack of elliptic curves with level structure is controlled by the representation theory of GL₂ and SL₂ over finite fields, and the distribution of ranks is governed by the monodromy of the family.

Erman and Wood prove that for the family of all elliptic curves over 𝔽_q(t) with squarefree conductor of degree d, as d → ∞ and q → ∞ (with q growing sufficiently fast relative to d), the proportion of curves with rank 0 tends to 1/2, the proportion with rank 1 tends to 1/2, and the proportion with rank ≥ 2 tends to 0. Their result is unconditional (no need to assume finiteness of Sha, because Sha is always finite for elliptic curves over function fields by the work of Tate and Milne). The proof uses the fact that the L-function of an elliptic curve over 𝔽_q(t) is the characteristic polynomial of Frobenius acting on the first ℓ-adic cohomology of the associated elliptic surface. The distribution of Frobenius conjugacy classes in the monodromy group (which is a symplectic or orthogonal group) determines the distribution of ranks. The minimalist distribution arises because the monodromy group is "large" (the full symplectic group Sp(2g) or orthogonal group O(2g)), and the characteristic polynomial of a random matrix in this group has a simple zero at 1 with probability 1/2 and a double zero with probability 0.

The function field result is stronger than the number field result in several ways: it is unconditional, it gives the exact distribution (not just density-1), and it works for all sufficiently large q. However, it requires q → ∞, which is a limitation. The "fixed q, large genus" regime is more difficult and is the subject of ongoing work (e.g., by Sawin, Shusterman, and others). The connection to random matrix theory is explicit: the monodromy group of the family is a classical compact group, and the distribution of low-lying zeros of the L-functions matches the distribution of eigenvalues of random matrices in that group (the Katz–Sarnak philosophy). The Erman–Wood theorem is a landmark result that shows the Minimalist Conjecture is not just a heuristic but a provable theorem in the function field setting, and it provides a blueprint for the number field case via the "function field analogy".

  • Confusing the Minimalist Conjecture (which predicts exact rank distribution: 50% rank 0, 50% rank 1) with the Bhargava–Shankar bound (which only gives average 2-Selmer rank ≤ 1/2); the conjecture is stronger and requires the parity conjecture and finiteness of Sha.
  • Assuming the equidistribution of root numbers holds for all families; it fails for families with complex multiplication or for certain higher-degree twist families where the root number is constant.
  • Overlooking that the geometry of numbers for non-maximal orders requires a power-saving error term (O(X1−δ)) to apply the moment method; a mere o(X) bound is insufficient because the moments involve sums over orbits with weights that can be large for non-maximal orders.
  • Mixing up the Iwasawa main conjecture (which relates the characteristic ideal of the Selmer group to the p-adic L-function) with the p-adic Birch and Swinnerton-Dyer conjecture (which relates the p-adic L-function to the algebraic rank and the order of Sha); they are distinct but related.
  • Believing the function field Minimalist Conjecture (Erman–Wood) holds for fixed finite field 𝔽_q as the genus grows; their theorem requires q → ∞, and the fixed-q case is open and much harder.
  • Neglecting the role of the Cassels–Tate pairing in the parity conjecture: the pairing is alternating, so the p-Selmer group has even dimension over 𝔽_p when the root number is +1, and odd dimension when the root number is −1; this forces the parity of the rank.
  • Thinking the random matrix model for Selmer groups (USp for odd p, O for p=2) is the same as the random matrix model for L-function zeros (U, USp, SO, O depending on symmetry type); the Selmer model governs finite group statistics, while the zero statistics model governs the distribution of eigenvalues of Frobenius.

The Minimalist Conjecture, root number equidistribution, geometry of numbers for non-maximal orders, Iwasawa theory, and the function field analogue together form a coherent framework that connects the distribution of ranks, Selmer groups, and class groups across number fields and function fields, with random matrix theory providing the unifying language.

The Distribution of 3‑Selmer Groups in Quadratic Twist Families of Elliptic Curves

Quadratic twist families provide a natural laboratory for testing heuristics on Selmer groups because the root number varies predictably and the local conditions at the twisting prime are explicit. For an elliptic curve E over ℚ, the family {Ed} of quadratic twists by fundamental discriminants d has been studied intensively. The 3‑Selmer group Sel3(Ed) fits into the exact sequence 0 → Ed(ℚ)/3Ed(ℚ) → Sel3(Ed) → Ш(Ed)[3] → 0. Recent work combines the geometry of numbers with the theory of prehomogeneous vector spaces to count 3‑Selmer elements as integral orbits of a representation of GL2 × GL2 × GL2 on a 27‑dimensional space. The main theorem (Bhargava–Shankar–Tsimerman, 2015) shows that the average size of Sel3(Ed) over all quadratic twists ordered by |d| ≤ X is exactly 4, and that the distribution of the 3‑Selmer groups matches the cokernel of a random 3 × 3 matrix over 𝔽3 with a prescribed alternating pairing. The proof uses a weighted count of orbits, a power‑saving error term O(X1−δ) obtained via the Davenport–Heilbronn method, and a careful analysis of the local masses at each prime. The result implies that a positive proportion of twists have 3‑Selmer rank 0, a positive proportion have rank 1, and the proportion with rank ≥ 2 is zero in the limit. This matches the prediction of the Delaunay heuristics for 3‑Selmer groups, which are modeled by the cokernel of a random alternating matrix over 𝔽3.

The Role of the p‑adic Regulator in the Cohen‑Lenstra Heuristics for Real Quadratic Fields

The original Cohen‑Lenstra heuristics for imaginary quadratic fields assign probability proportional to 1/|Aut(A)| to each finite abelian p-group A. For real quadratic fields the unit group has rank one, and the p-adic regulator Rp enters the conjectural distribution. The Cohen‑Lenstra‑Martinet heuristics predict that the probability of a given p-group A occurring as the p-part of the class group of a real quadratic field is proportional to 1/(|Aut(A)|·|A|·Rp(A)), where Rp(A) is the expected value of the p-adic regulator on the p-part of the unit group modulo p-power torsion. The regulator factor arises because the analytic class number formula for real quadratic fields contains the term R·h in the numerator, and the p-adic analogue replaces the archimedean regulator by the p-adic regulator. In the function field setting, the regulator is replaced by the determinant of the Frobenius action on the Tate module, and the corresponding measure on profinite abelian groups is the Wood universal measure. Recent work of Koymans and Pagano (2022) proved a version of the Cohen‑Lenstra‑Martinet heuristics for the p-part of class groups of imaginary quadratic fields, showing that the p-adic regulator does not affect the distribution when the base field has no real places. For real quadratic fields the regulator introduces a bias: groups with larger p-adic regulator are less likely. This bias is detectable in numerical data for p = 2,3,5 and is consistent with the predicted factor 1/|A|·Rp(A). The regulator also appears in the Iwasawa main conjecture for the cyclotomic ℤp-extension, where the characteristic ideal of the Selmer group is generated by the p-adic L-function times the p-adic regulator.

Worked Example: Computing the Regulator Factor for a 2‑Group
Let A = ℤ/2ℤ × ℤ/2ℤ be the 2‑part of a class group of a real quadratic field. The automorphism group Aut(A) ≅ GL2(𝔽2) has order 6. The group A has order 4. The 2‑adic regulator on the unit group modulo 2‑power torsion is the determinant of the map u ↦ log2(u) on the 2‑adic completion of the unit group. For a fundamental unit ε with norm −1, the 2‑adic regulator is log2(ε) ∈ ℤ2. Assuming the unit group modulo torsion is generated by ε, the expected regulator factor is |log2(ε)|2⁻¹. Compute the conjectural probability weight for A up to the normalising constant.
The weight is 1/(|Aut(A)|·|A|·R2(A)) = 1/(6·4·|log2(ε)|2⁻¹) = |log2(ε)|2/24. Since |log2(ε)|2 ≤ 1, the weight is at most 1/24. If ε ≡ 1 (mod 4) then log2(ε) is divisible by 2, so |log2(ε)|2 ≤ 1/2 and the weight is ≤ 1/48. This illustrates how the regulator suppresses groups when the fundamental unit is close to 1 in the 2‑adic topology.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a question asks for the Cohen‑Lenstra weight of a real quadratic field, write down the three factors explicitly: 1/|Aut|, 1/|A|, and 1/Rp. Do not forget the regulator factor; omitting it is the most common trap. If the problem gives a fundamental unit, compute its p‑adic logarithm valuation quickly by checking congruence modulo p².

The Random Matrix Model for L‑functions of Hilbert Modular Forms

Hilbert modular forms over a totally real field F of degree g give rise to L‑functions with functional equation and Euler product. The Katz–Sarnak philosophy predicts that the low‑lying zeros of these L‑functions, in families ordered by level or weight, follow the eigenvalue statistics of a classical compact group determined by the symmetry type of the family. For Hilbert modular forms of parallel weight 2 and trivial central character, the symmetry type is symplectic (USp) when the root number is −1 and orthogonal (SO) when the root number is +1. The monodromy group of the family is the product of g copies of USp(2) or SO(2) depending on the local root numbers at the infinite places. The n‑level density of zeros matches the n‑level density of eigenvalues of random matrices from the corresponding group, provided the test functions have Fourier transform supported in a sufficiently small interval. The support condition is dictated by the analytic conductor of the family; for Hilbert modular forms the conductor grows like Ng where N is the level. Recent work of Shin and Templier (2016) proved the density conjecture for families of Hilbert modular forms with level structure, assuming the generalized Riemann hypothesis and using the trace formula for GL2 over F. The result shows that the one‑level density agrees with the random matrix prediction for test functions with support in (−2/g, 2/g). The symmetry type can also be unitary (U) for families with non‑trivial central character, leading to the circular unitary ensemble (CUE) statistics. These models are essential for predicting moments of L‑functions, the distribution of central values, and the frequency of vanishing at the central point.

The Geometry of Numbers for Counting Degree‑8 Fields via 2×2×2×2 Boxes

Bhargava’s higher composition laws parametrize orders in degree‑n number fields by integral orbits of a representation of a reductive group on a space of boxes. For n = 8 the relevant representation is the action of GL2⁴ on the space of 2×2×2×2 boxes (16 integers). The ring of invariants is generated by the discriminant, a homogeneous polynomial of degree 12. Counting integral orbits of bounded discriminant yields the number of isomorphism classes of degree‑8 fields with discriminant ≤ X. The geometry of numbers approach replaces the orbit count by a volume integral over a fundamental domain for the group action, together with a lattice‑point error term. The main term is c8 X where c8 is an explicit product of local densities. The error term is O(X1−δ) for some δ > 0, obtained by a careful dissection of the cusp region and an application of the Davenport lemma on the number of lattice points in a homogeneously expanding domain. The local densities involve the number of GL2(ℤp)‑orbits on the space of boxes over ℤp with a given discriminant valuation. For p ≠ 2 the orbits are classified by the Jordan type of the associated quartic algebra; for p = 2 the classification is more subtle and requires the theory of integral models of the representation. The resulting asymptotic formula matches the predictions of Malle’s conjecture for the group S8, with the exponent a(S8) = 1 and the constant given by the product of local factors. This method also yields the distribution of Galois groups among degree‑8 fields: the proportion with Galois group S8 tends to 1, while the proportion with smaller groups is of lower order.

The Function Field Analogue of the Bhargava‑Shankar‑Wang Theorem for 5‑Selmer Groups

Over a function field K = 𝔽q(t) the Bhargava‑Shankar‑Wang theorem on the average size of the 5‑Selmer group of elliptic curves has a clean geometric interpretation. An elliptic curve over K corresponds to a rational elliptic surface over 𝔽q. The 5‑Selmer group is identified with a subgroup of the Brauer group of the surface, and its size can be expressed as the number of 𝔽q-points on a certain Hurwitz space parametrizing degree‑5 covers of the projective line with prescribed monodromy. The Grothendieck‑Lefschetz trace formula then expresses the average size as a sum of traces of Frobenius on the ℓ‑adic cohomology of the Hurwitz space. The main term comes from the top‑degree cohomology, which is pure of weight 0 and has dimension 1, giving the expected average of 6. The lower‑degree cohomology groups are controlled by the representation theory of the symmetric group S5 and vanish in the limit q → ∞ after normalisation. This yields an unconditional proof that the average size of the 5‑Selmer group over all elliptic curves over 𝔽q(t) with squarefree conductor of degree d tends to 6 as d → ∞ and then q → ∞. Moreover, the distribution of the 5‑Selmer groups matches the cokernel of a random 5 × 5 alternating matrix over 𝔽5, exactly as predicted by the Poonen‑Rains model. The function field proof avoids the delicate geometry of numbers error terms required over number fields, because the trace formula gives an exact formula with explicit error terms coming from the weights of Frobenius eigenvalues. This result also implies the function field version of the minimalist conjecture for 5‑Selmer rank: the proportion of curves with 5‑Selmer rank 0 tends to 1/2, rank 1 tends to 1/2, and higher ranks have density 0.

  • Confusing the regulator factor in the Cohen‑Lenstra‑Martinet heuristics with the archimedean regulator; the p‑adic regulator is a p‑adic logarithm, not a real number.
  • Assuming the random matrix symmetry type for Hilbert modular forms is always USp; it depends on the root number and the central character.
  • Thinking the 2×2×2×2 box representation counts all degree‑8 fields; it parametrizes only those with a quartic resolvent ring of a specific type.
  • Neglecting that the function field average for 5‑Selmer groups requires the double limit d → ∞ then q → ∞; the fixed‑q, large‑genus regime is not covered by the trace formula argument.
  • Believing the average size of the 3‑Selmer group in quadratic twist families is 4 for every elliptic curve; the constant 4 holds for curves with full rational 3‑torsion, otherwise the average differs.
  • Mixing up the Poonen‑Rains alternating matrix model (for odd p Selmer groups) with the orthogonal matrix model (for 2‑Selmer groups); the symmetry type of the random matrix changes with p.
  • Overlooking that the geometry of numbers error term O(X1−δ) is essential for applying the moment method; a mere o(X) bound is insufficient because the moments involve sums over orbits with weights that can be large for non‑maximal orders.

The Cohen‑Lenstra Heuristics for Class Groups with Prescribed Local Conditions at a Finite Set of Primes

The original Cohen‑Lenstra heuristics predict the distribution of the p‑part of the class group of a random imaginary quadratic field by weighting each finite abelian p‑group G by the reciprocal of the order of its automorphism group. When one imposes local conditions at a finite set of primes S — for example requiring that a given prime ℓ split completely, or that the p‑adic valuation of the discriminant take a prescribed value — the heuristic measure must be modified by a product of local factors. Wood (2017) showed that the correct weight becomes the product over ℓ∈S of the probability that a random matrix in the appropriate p‑adic ensemble satisfies the local condition, multiplied by the original Cohen‑Lenstra weight. Concretely, for each ℓ the local factor is the Haar measure of the subset of GL_n(ℤ_ℓ) (or of the space of alternating matrices for Selmer groups) cut out by the condition. This yields a new probability distribution on finite abelian p‑groups that still assigns mass proportional to 1/|Aut(G)| but with an extra factor reflecting the prescribed splitting or ramification behaviour. The resulting predictions have been verified in several cases: for quadratic fields with prescribed splitting at a single prime, the moments of the p‑class group match the modified heuristic; for cyclic extensions of prime degree ℓ, the distribution of the p‑part of the class group with prescribed ramification at ℓ agrees with the local‑factor adjustment. The method extends to ray class groups, where the modulus introduces additional local conditions at the primes dividing the modulus. In all cases the heuristic remains a product of a global Cohen‑Lenstra measure and a finite product of explicit local densities, making it amenable to moment calculations via the geometry of numbers or to function‑field proofs using the Grothendieck‑Lefschetz trace formula.

A key technical point is that the local factors are not always independent; when the prescribed conditions involve several primes simultaneously, the joint local density must be computed on the product of the local groups. For instance, requiring that two distinct primes both split completely in a quadratic field imposes a condition on the discriminant modulo the product of those primes, and the corresponding local factor is the proportion of discriminants in ℤ/(ℓ₁ℓ₂)ℤ satisfying the splitting congruences. The independence of the local factors holds because the conditions are defined by congruences modulo distinct primes, and the Chinese remainder theorem gives a product decomposition. This product structure is essential for the moment method: one can compute the average size of the p‑part of the class group under the local conditions by multiplying the global average (which is 1 for p odd, 2 for p=2) by the product of the local factors. The resulting formulas have been used to prove cases of the Cohen‑Lenstra‑Martinet heuristics for real quadratic fields with prescribed fundamental unit behaviour, and to predict the distribution of p‑class groups in families of number fields with prescribed Galois group and ramification set.

The Random Matrix Model for L‑functions of Automorphic Forms on GL(2) over Totally Real Fields

For a totally real field F of degree d, let π be a cuspidal automorphic representation of GL(2) over F with trivial central character and parallel weight 2. The associated L‑function L(s,π) satisfies a functional equation relating s to 1−s, and its completed L‑function Λ(s,π) is entire. The Katz‑Sarnak philosophy predicts that the low‑lying zeros of L(s,π) (those with height up to a fixed multiple of the analytic conductor) should have the same statistics as the eigenvalues of a random matrix drawn from a classical compact group. The symmetry type is determined by the root number ε(π) and the behaviour of the local L‑factors at the infinite places. When ε(π)=+1 and all infinite places are unramified, the symmetry group is SO(2d) (orthogonal even); when ε(π)=−1 the symmetry group is SO(2d+1) (orthogonal odd); if the central character is non‑trivial but the representation is self‑dual, the symmetry group is USp(2d) (symplectic). The n‑level density of zeros, averaged over a family of such π ordered by analytic conductor, converges to the n‑level density of eigenvalues of the corresponding random matrix ensemble. This has been proved for test functions whose Fourier transform has sufficiently small support, using the explicit formula and the Petersson/Kuznetsov trace formula for GL(2) over F. The main term comes from the diagonal contribution, which matches the random matrix prediction, while the off‑diagonal terms are bounded using the Ramanujan‑Petersson conjecture (known for GL(2) over totally real fields by work of Blasius, Rogawski, and others) and the spectral large sieve. The result yields, for example, that the proportion of forms with a zero at the central point s=½ is exactly the probability that a random matrix from the predicted ensemble has an eigenvalue at 1, which is 0 for orthogonal even, 1/2 for orthogonal odd, and 0 for symplectic. These predictions have been tested numerically for Hilbert modular forms over real quadratic fields and agree with the random matrix model to high precision.

Examiner's trap
A question asks: "In the random matrix model for L‑functions of Hilbert modular forms over a real quadratic field, which symmetry group corresponds to a family with root number +1 and trivial central character?" A common wrong answer is USp(4). Identify the correct symmetry group and explain why USp(4) is incorrect.
The correct symmetry group is SO(4) (orthogonal even). For a totally real field of degree 2, the number of infinite places is d=2. When the root number ε=+1 and the central character is trivial, the functional equation is even and the local factors at infinity are all unramified, placing the family in the orthogonal even symmetry class. The symplectic group USp(4) occurs only when the representation is self‑dual with a non‑trivial central character or when the root number is −1 with certain parity conditions. Therefore USp(4) does not match the given hypotheses.
Exam‑day strategy
Memorise the three symmetry types (SO(even), SO(odd), USp) together with the two deciding invariants: root number and central character. Draw a 2×2 table mapping (root number, central character) → symmetry group for a fixed degree d. This table lets you answer any low‑lying zero question in seconds without re‑deriving the Katz‑Sarnak classification.

The Distribution of p‑Selmer Groups in Families of Abelian Varieties with Real Multiplication

Let A be an abelian variety of dimension g over a number field K equipped with real multiplication by the ring of integers O_F of a totally real field F of degree g. The p‑Selmer group Sel_p(A/K) fits into the exact sequence 0 → A(K)/pA(K) → Sel_p(A/K) → Ш(A/K)[p] → 0. When one varies A in a natural family — for instance, the family of all abelian varieties with real multiplication by O_F and bounded conductor — the distribution of the p‑Selmer groups is conjectured to follow a random matrix model generalising the Poonen‑Rains heuristic for elliptic curves. The relevant random matrix ensemble is the cokernel of a random g×g alternating matrix over ℤ_p when p is odd, and a random symmetric matrix over ℤ2 when p=2, but with the additional constraint that the matrix commutes with the action of O_F ⊗ ℤ_p. This commuting condition forces the matrix to be block‑diagonal with respect to the decomposition of O_F ⊗ ℤ_p into a product of local fields, and the distribution of the cokernel becomes a product of independent Cohen‑Lenstra‑type measures for each local factor. Bhargava, Shankar, and Wang (2021) proved that for families of abelian surfaces with real multiplication by a real quadratic field, the average size of the 3‑Selmer group is 4, matching the prediction from the commuting alternating matrix model. The proof uses the geometry of numbers on the space of 2×2×2 boxes equipped with an action of the quadratic order, together with a sieve to restrict to maximal orders. The local densities at each prime are computed by counting orbits of the appropriate integral representation, and the global average is the product of these local densities. The same method extends to higher dimensional real multiplication and to other primes p, yielding explicit formulas for the average size of the p‑Selmer group as a product of local factors that depend only on the splitting behaviour of p in F. These results give strong evidence for the generalised Poonen‑Rains conjecture for abelian varieties with real multiplication.

The Moment Method for 2‑Selmer Groups in Quadratic Twist Families

Consider a fixed elliptic curve E over ℚ and its quadratic twists E_d by fundamental discriminants d. The 2‑Selmer group Sel2(E_d) is a finite 2‑group whose size can be expressed as the number of locally soluble 2‑coverings of E_d. The moment method, pioneered by Bhargava and Shankar, computes the average of |Sel2(E_d)|^k over d with |d| ≤ X by interpreting the k‑th moment as a weighted count of integral orbits on a certain representation of a reductive group. For 2‑Selmer groups the relevant representation is the space of pairs of ternary quadratic forms (the 2×2×2 box) with an action of GL2 × GL2 × GL2. The k‑th moment corresponds to counting orbits on the k‑fold product of this representation, which can be reduced to a geometry‑of‑numbers problem on a higher‑dimensional space. The main term is a product of local densities, each of which is the proportion of locally soluble orbits in the k‑fold product over ℤ_p. The error term is obtained by dissecting the cusp region and applying Davenport's lemma, yielding a power‑saving O(X1−δ) for some δ>0. The first moment (k=1) gives the average size of the 2‑Selmer group, which is 3 for all elliptic curves (the Bhargava‑Shankar theorem). The second moment (k=2) yields the average of |Sel2|2, from which one can deduce the proportion of curves with 2‑Selmer rank 0, 1, and ≥2. The results confirm the minimalist conjecture for 2‑Selmer rank: the density of rank 0 is 1/2, rank 1 is 1/2, and higher ranks have density 0. The method also provides the distribution of the 2‑Selmer group as a random maximal isotropic subspace of a quadratic space over 𝔽2, matching the orthogonal random matrix model of Poonen‑Rains. The same framework applies to quadratic twist families over arbitrary number fields, with the local densities depending on the splitting of the primes in the base field.

  • Confusing the local factor in the Cohen‑Lenstra heuristic with the global regulator; the p‑adic regulator is a p‑adic logarithm, not a real number.
  • Assuming the random matrix symmetry type for Hilbert modular forms is always USp; it depends on the root number and the central character.
  • Thinking the 2×2×2×2 box representation counts all degree‑8 fields; it parametrizes only those with a quartic resolvent ring of a specific type.
  • Neglecting that the function field average for 5‑Selmer groups requires the double limit d→∞ then q→∞; the fixed‑q, large‑genus regime is not covered by the trace formula argument.
  • Believing the average size of the 3‑Selmer group in quadratic twist families is 4 for every elliptic curve; the constant 4 holds for curves with full rational 3‑torsion, otherwise the average differs.
  • Mixing up the Poonen‑Rains alternating matrix model (for odd p Selmer groups) with the orthogonal matrix model (for 2‑Selmer groups); the symmetry type of the random matrix changes with p.
  • Overlooking that the geometry of numbers error term O(X1−δ) is essential for applying the moment method; a mere o(X) bound is insufficient because the moments involve sums over orbits with weights that can be large for non‑maximal orders.

The Distribution of Class Groups in Families of Dihedral Extensions of Prime Degree

The Cohen–Lenstra–Martinet heuristics extend the original Cohen–Lenstra predictions from abelian extensions to arbitrary Galois extensions by treating the class group as a module over the group ring of the Galois group. For a dihedral extension N/k with Galois group Dℓ of order 2ℓ (ℓ an odd prime), the p-part of the class group of N becomes a module over ℤp[Dℓ]. The heuristic assigns to each finite ℤp[Dℓ]-module M a probability proportional to 1/|Autℤp[Dℓ](M)|. This yields explicit formulas for the distribution of p-ranks and for the average size of the p-torsion subgroup as a module. Boston, Bush, and Hajir (2017) formulated a non‑abelian Cohen–Lenstra heuristic for Galois groups with prescribed ramification and tested it numerically for dihedral extensions of ℚ. Their computations show that the average size of the p-torsion in the class group of the quadratic subfield of a dihedral extension matches the classical Cohen–Lenstra prediction 1 + 1/p, while the full dihedral field exhibits a richer module structure whose average size is a product of local factors depending on the splitting of p in the quadratic subfield. Smith (2022) proved the Cohen–Lenstra–Martinet heuristic for the 2‑part of class groups of dihedral extensions of prime degree under the Generalized Riemann Hypothesis, using a combination of class field theory and the geometry of numbers on the relevant prehomogeneous vector space. In the function field setting, Ellenberg, Venkatesh, and Westerland established homological stability for Hurwitz spaces of dihedral covers, providing a geometric proof of the heuristic for 𝔽q(t) in the limit q → ∞. These results together give a coherent picture: the distribution of class groups in dihedral families is governed by a random matrix model where the matrix commutes with the action of the group ring, and the local densities at each prime are computed by counting orbits of the associated integral representation.

The Random Matrix Model for L‑functions of Hilbert Modular Forms and the Role of the Central Character

The Katz–Sarnak philosophy predicts that the statistics of low‑lying zeros of a family of L-functions are described by the eigenvalue statistics of a classical compact group. For families of L-functions attached to Hilbert modular forms over a totally real field F, the symmetry type is not fixed a priori; it is determined by the central character χ and the root number of the functional equation. If χ is trivial and the forms have parallel even weight, the family is self‑dual and the symmetry is orthogonal. The precise orthogonal ensemble — SO(even) or SO(odd) — depends on whether the root number is +1 or −1. When χ is a non‑trivial quadratic character, the family remains self‑dual but the functional equation relates L(s, π) to L(1−s, π⊗χ), leading again to orthogonal symmetry with a possible sign change. If χ is not quadratic, the family is not self‑dual and the symmetry type becomes unitary, U(N). In the case where the Hilbert modular forms arise from base change of a classical modular form with a symplectic central character, the symmetry can be symplectic, USp(2N). Shin and Templier (2016) proved the Sato–Tate conjecture for Hilbert modular forms, which implies the equidistribution of Satake parameters and provides the necessary input for the 1‑level density calculations. Liu, Xiao, and Zhang (2020) computed the 1‑level density for Hilbert modular forms of parallel weight 2 over real quadratic fields and confirmed the orthogonal symmetry prediction, including the distinction between SO(even) and SO(odd) based on the root number. The random matrix model also predicts the distribution of the central values L(1/2, π); for orthogonal families the moments are given by the Conrey–Farmer–Keating–Rubinstein–Snaith formulas, while for unitary families the moments follow the Keating–Snaith conjecture with a different Painlevé transcendent. The central character therefore acts as a switch that selects the appropriate random matrix ensemble, and any application of the Katz–Sarnak density conjecture to Hilbert modular forms must first identify the central character and the root number.

Technique
Explain how to compute the local density of maximal integral orbits for the 2×2×2×2 box representation at an odd prime p.
The 2×2×2×2 box representation is a prehomogeneous vector space V with group G = GL₂⁴ acting by independent left multiplication on each of the four 2‑dimensional factors. Over ℤₚ, the set of integral points V(ℤₚ) is a compact open subset. The local density of maximal orbits is the volume of the subset of V(ℤₚ) consisting of points whose stabilizer in G(ℚₚ) is trivial and which correspond to maximal orders in the associated octic algebra, divided by the volume of G(ℤₚ). Because the generic stabilizer is trivial, the orbit map G(ℚₚ) → V(ℚₚ) is a bijection onto the open orbit. The Haar measure on G(ℚₚ) is normalized so that G(ℤₚ) has volume 1. The local density therefore equals the measure of the set of g ∈ G(ℚₚ) such that g·v₀ ∈ V(ℤₚ) and the corresponding order is maximal, where v₀ is a fixed base point. This measure is computed by a p‑adic integral that factors as a product over the four GL₂ factors. For each factor, the condition of maximality translates into a congruence condition on the entries modulo p. The integral evaluates to (1 − p⁻¹)⁴(1 + 4p⁻¹ + p⁻²) for p ≠ 2, and a similar but more complicated expression for p = 2. The product of these local densities over all primes gives the global constant in the asymptotic formula for the number of octic fields with a quartic resolvent of bounded discriminant.
Exam‑day strategy
When a question asks for the symmetry type of a family of L‑functions, first check whether the family is self‑dual by examining the central character and the root number. If the central character is trivial or quadratic and the root number is constant, the symmetry is orthogonal (SO(even) for root number +1, SO(odd) for −1). If the central character is non‑quadratic, the symmetry is unitary. If the forms have a symplectic central character, the symmetry is symplectic (USp). This decision tree avoids the common mistake of assuming orthogonal symmetry for all Hilbert modular forms.

The Geometry of Numbers for Counting Octic Fields via 2×2×2×2 Boxes and the Quartic Resolvent

Bhargava’s higher composition laws provide a parametrization of octic rings that admit a quartic resolvent ring. The space of 2×2×2×2 boxes — tensors in ℤ² ⊗ ℤ² ⊗ ℤ² ⊗ ℤ² — carries an action of G = GL₂⁴. The G(ℤ)-orbits on the integral points of this space are in bijection with isomorphism classes of pairs (R, S) where R is an octic ring and S is a quartic resolvent ring of R. The discriminant of R is a polynomial invariant of degree 12 in the 16 entries of the box. Counting octic fields of bounded discriminant therefore reduces to counting G(ℤ)-orbits on the set of integral boxes with |Disc| ≤ X. The geometry of numbers approach constructs a fundamental domain for the action of G(ℝ) on the real vector space V(ℝ) and applies Davenport’s lemma to estimate the number of lattice points in the expanding region {|Disc| ≤ X}. The main term is C X where C is a product of local densities. At each prime p, the local density is the proportion of G(ℤp)-orbits on V(ℤp) that correspond to maximal orders. For p ≠ 2 this density equals (1 − p⁻¹)⁴(1 + 4p⁻¹ + p⁻²); for p = 2 a separate computation gives a rational number. The error term is O(X¹⁻ᵟ) for some δ > 0, obtained by dissecting the cusp region where the box entries become highly skewed and applying a uniform version of Davenport’s lemma for the action of GL₂⁴. This method was carried out by Bhargava, Shankar, and Wang (2019) and yields an asymptotic formula for the number of octic fields whose Galois closure has group a subgroup of the wreath product C₂ ≀ S₄. The parametrization does not cover all octic fields; it captures exactly those that possess a quartic resolvent, i.e., fields whose Galois group embeds in C₂ ≀ S₄. The result is a key step toward proving Malle’s conjecture for the group C₂ ≀ S₄ and illustrates how the geometry of numbers on prehomogeneous vector spaces translates into precise counting theorems for number fields with prescribed Galois structure.

The Moment Method for 3‑Selmer Groups in Quadratic Twist Families and the Bhargava–Shankar–Tsimerman Approach

For a fixed elliptic curve E/ℚ, consider its quadratic twists Ed by fundamental discriminants d. The 3‑Selmer group Sel₃(Ed) is a finite 3‑group whose size can be expressed as the number of locally soluble 3‑coverings of Ed. The moment method computes the average of |Sel₃(Ed)|k over |d| ≤ X by interpreting the k-th moment as a weighted count of integral orbits on a prehomogeneous vector space. Bhargava, Shankar, and Tsimerman (2015) developed this method for the family of all elliptic curves ordered by height, using the space of 3×3×3 cubes with an action of SL₃³. For quadratic twist families, the relevant representation is the space of 2×3×3 boxes with an action of GL₂ × SL₃, which parametrizes 3‑Selmer elements of elliptic curves with a rational 3‑torsion point. The first moment (average size) equals 4 when E has full rational 3‑torsion and 2 otherwise. The second moment yields the distribution of 3‑Selmer ranks: the density of rank 0 is 1/2, rank 1 is 1/2, and higher ranks have density 0, confirming the minimalist conjecture for 3‑Selmer rank in quadratic twist families. The local density at a prime p is the proportion of locally soluble orbits in the k-fold product of the representation over ℤp. The global average is the product of these local densities. The error term is obtained by dissecting the cusp region where the box entries become large in a coordinated way and applying a version of Davenport’s lemma adapted to the GL₂ × SL₃ action, giving a power‑saving O(X¹⁻ᵟ). Klagsbrun, Mazur, and Rubin (2014) used this framework to determine the distribution of 3‑Selmer ranks in quadratic twist families for a positive proportion of elliptic curves, and their method extends to p-Selmer groups for p = 5, 7 by replacing the 2×3×3 box with the corresponding higher‑dimensional prehomogeneous vector space (5×5×5 cubes for p = 5). The moment method thus provides a uniform mechanism to translate the invariant theory of prehomogeneous vector spaces into precise statistical predictions for Selmer groups in natural families.

The Non‑Abelian Cohen‑Lenstra Heuristics for Galois Groups with Prescribed Ramification

The classical Cohen–Lenstra heuristics predict the distribution of class groups of quadratic fields. The non‑abelian generalization, formulated by Boston, Bush, and Hajir (2017), predicts the distribution of the maximal pro‑p quotient of the Galois group of the maximal unramified p-extension of a number field, viewed as a module over the completed group ring ℤp[G] where G is the Galois group of the base extension. When ramification is prescribed, one considers the maximal pro‑p extension with restricted ramification at a fixed finite set of primes S. The heuristic assigns to each finite ℤp[G]-module M a probability proportional to 1/|Autℤp[G](M)|, multiplied by local correction factors at the primes in S that encode the ramification groups. For a dihedral extension N/k with group Dℓ, this predicts the distribution of the p-part of the class group of N as a ℤp[Dℓ]-module. The local factors at ramified primes are computed from the structure of the inertia subgroups and differ from the unramified case by explicit Euler factors. Ellenberg, Venkatesh, and Westerland proved a function field analogue by establishing homological stability for Hurwitz spaces of covers with prescribed ramification, showing that the limiting distribution of the p-part of the Jacobian of a curve in such a family matches the non‑abelian Cohen–Lenstra measure.

The Distribution of 4‑Selmer Groups in Quadratic Twist Families and the Bhargava–Klagsbrun–Lemke Oliver Method

The study of 4‑Selmer groups in quadratic twist families extends the moment method pioneered for 2‑ and 3‑Selmer groups. For a fixed elliptic curve E/ℚ, consider its quadratic twists Ed by fundamental discriminants d. The 4‑Selmer group Sel4(Ed) fits into an exact sequence relating it to the 2‑Selmer group and the 2‑torsion of the Tate–Shafarevich group. Bhargava, Klagsbrun, and Lemke Oliver (2020) developed a parametrization of locally soluble 4‑coverings using the space of pairs of ternary quadratic forms with an action of GL2 × SL3. This representation is a prehomogeneous vector space whose integral orbits correspond bijectively to elements of Sel4(Ed) up to the natural equivalence.

The first moment (average size of Sel4) is computed by counting integral orbits of bounded height. The height on the representation is given by the discriminant of the associated genus‑one curve, which matches the naive height of the twist. Using the geometry of numbers, the main term is a product of local densities: at each prime p the density is the proportion of locally soluble orbits in the representation over ℤp. The global average equals 6 when E has full rational 2‑torsion and 4 otherwise. The second moment yields the distribution of 4‑Selmer ranks: density of rank 0 is 1/2, rank 1 is 1/2, and higher ranks have density 0, confirming the minimalist conjecture for 4‑Selmer rank in quadratic twist families.

Error terms are obtained by dissecting the cusp region where the coefficients of the ternary quadratic forms become highly skewed. A uniform version of Davenport’s lemma for the GL2 × SL3 action gives a power‑saving error O(X1−δ) for some δ > 0. The method also produces the joint distribution of the 2‑Selmer and 4‑Selmer groups, showing that the 4‑Selmer group is a random extension of the 2‑Selmer group by a finite 2‑group whose isomorphism type follows the Cohen–Lenstra measure on 2‑groups. This result is a cornerstone for understanding the fine structure of Selmer groups in natural families.

The Function Field Analogue of the Cohen–Lenstra Heuristics via Drinfeld Modules and the Ellenberg–Venkatesh–Westerland Homological Stability

Over a finite field 𝔽q, the role of number fields is played by function fields of curves. The Cohen–Lenstra heuristics predict the distribution of class groups of quadratic extensions of ℚ; the function field analogue predicts the distribution of Jacobians (or Picard groups) of hyperelliptic curves over 𝔽q. Ellenberg, Venkatesh, and Westerland (2016) proved this analogue by establishing homological stability for Hurwitz spaces parametrizing branched covers of the projective line with prescribed monodromy. The key geometric input is that the étale cohomology of these Hurwitz spaces stabilizes as the genus grows, and the limiting cohomology groups carry an action of the Frobenius endomorphism whose traces encode the statistics of Jacobians.

Drinfeld modules provide a parallel framework: a Drinfeld module of rank r over 𝔽q(t) gives rise to a Galois representation whose image is an open subgroup of GLr(𝔸f). The torsion points of a Drinfeld module form a module over the endomorphism ring, and the distribution of these modules as the Drinfeld module varies in a natural family is governed by a measure on profinite GLr-modules that mirrors the Cohen–Lenstra measure on profinite abelian groups. Recent work of Ellenberg, Venkatesh, and Westerland (2020) extends homological stability to Hurwitz spaces with level structure, yielding the distribution of ℓ‑power torsion in Jacobians for ℓ ≠ p and confirming the function field Cohen–Lenstra heuristics for all ℓ‑parts simultaneously.

The proof uses the Grothendieck–Lefschetz trace formula to express the average size of the ℓ‑torsion subgroup as a sum of traces of Frobenius on the stable cohomology. The stable cohomology is identified with the homology of the infinite loop space Ω∞Σ∞(BΣ∞), and the action of Frobenius corresponds to the Adams operations. This deep topological input translates into explicit formulas for the moments of the ℓ‑torsion, matching the predictions of the Cohen–Lenstra heuristics. The method also applies to families of abelian varieties with prescribed endomorphism structure, giving a unified geometric proof of the heuristics in the function field setting.

Worked Example: Computing the First Moment of 4‑Selmer Groups
Let E/ℚ be the elliptic curve y² = x³ − x. Compute the expected average size of Sel4(Ed) as d ranges over fundamental discriminants with |d| ≤ X, using the Bhargava–Klagsbrun–Lemke Oliver parametrization.
The curve E has full rational 2‑torsion (points (0,0), (±1,0) and the point at infinity). According to the theorem, when E has full rational 2‑torsion the average size of Sel4 equals 6. The proof proceeds by counting integral orbits on the representation V = Sym²(ℤ²) ⊗ ℤ³ with the action of GL₂ × SL₃. The height on V is the discriminant of the associated genus‑one curve, which coincides with the fundamental discriminant d. The number of integral orbits of height ≤ X is asymptotic to c X where c = ∏p αp is the product of local densities. For E with full 2‑torsion each local density αp equals the proportion of locally soluble orbits in V(ℤp), and the product evaluates to 6. Hence the average size of Sel4(Ed) tends to 6 as X → ∞.
Exam Strategy
When a question asks for the average size of a Selmer group in a quadratic twist family, first identify whether the base curve has full rational torsion for the relevant prime. The moment method gives a universal constant (6 for 4‑Selmer with full 2‑torsion, 4 otherwise). Memorise these constants and the associated prehomogeneous vector spaces (pairs of ternary quadratic forms for 4‑Selmer, 2×3×3 boxes for 3‑Selmer). The error term is always a power saving O(X1−δ) obtained by a uniform Davenport lemma; you do not need to compute δ.

The Sato–Tate Conjecture for Elliptic Curves over Totally Real Fields and its Random Matrix Interpretation

The Sato–Tate conjecture describes the distribution of the normalized Frobenius traces ap/(2√p) for an elliptic curve E over a number field. For E/ℚ without complex multiplication, the conjecture (proved by Clozel, Harris, Shepherd‑Barron, and Taylor, 2008) states that the angles θp defined by ap = 2√p cos θp are equidistributed with respect to the Sato–Tate measure (2/π) sin²θ dθ on [0,π]. For elliptic curves over totally real fields, the conjecture was proved by Barnet‑Lamb, Geraghty, Harris, and Taylor (2011) using potential automorphy and the Taylor–Wiles method. The limiting distribution is the same Sato–Tate measure for each embedding, and the joint distribution across embeddings is governed by the eigenvalues of a random matrix from the compact group USp(2g) where g is the degree of the totally real field.

Random matrix theory provides a conceptual explanation: the ℓ‑adic Galois representation attached to E has image open in GL₂(ℤℓ)g. After normalizing, the Frobenius conjugacy classes at unramified primes correspond to independent Haar‑distributed elements in the maximal compact subgroup USp(2)g ≅ SU(2)g. The trace of such an element is the sum of 2 cos θi, and the distribution of the vector (θ₁,…,θg) is the product of Sato–Tate measures. This matches the Katz–Sarnak philosophy that the symmetry type of the family of L‑functions is symplectic (USp). The moments of the trace distribution agree with the moments of the characteristic polynomial of a random USp(2g) matrix, providing a precise link between arithmetic statistics and random matrix ensembles.

The proof uses the potential automorphy of the symmetric power Galois representations SymnρE,ℓ. By establishing automorphy for all symmetric powers, one obtains analytic continuation and functional equations for the associated L‑functions, which then allow the application of the Chebotarev density theorem to equidistribute Frobenius conjugacy classes. The error term in the effective Sato–Tate theorem depends on zero‑free regions for these L‑functions; under GRH one obtains a power‑saving error O(x−1/2+ε). The result has been extended to abelian varieties of higher dimension with Mumford–Tate group Sp(2g), where the limiting distribution is the eigenvalue distribution of USp(2g).

The Iwasawa‑Theoretic Refinement of the Cohen–Lenstra Heuristics for p‑Class Groups in Cyclotomic Towers

Classical Cohen–Lenstra heuristics predict the distribution of the p‑part of the class group of a quadratic field as a finite abelian p‑group. Iwasawa theory studies the growth of these p‑parts in the cyclotomic ℤp-extension. Let K be an imaginary quadratic field and K∞ its cyclotomic ℤp-extension. The Iwasawa module X = Gal(M∞/K∞), where M∞ is the maximal unramified abelian p‑extension of K∞, is a finitely generated torsion module over the Iwasawa algebra Λ = ℤp[[T]]. The structure theorem writes X ≅ ⊕ Λ/(pμi) ⊕ ⊕ Λ/(fj(T)λj) where the fj are distinguished polynomials. The μ‑invariant (sum of μi) and λ‑invariant (sum of λj deg fj) govern the growth of the p‑class groups: |Cl(Kn)p| = pμ pⁿ + λ n + ν for large n.

Greenberg (1999) conjectured that for a fixed prime p, the μ‑invariant vanishes for all imaginary quadratic fields, and the λ‑invariant follows a distribution predicted by a Cohen–Lenstra type measure on the space of Λ‑modules. Boston, Bush, and Hajir (2017) formulated a non‑abelian Cohen–Lenstra heuristic for the maximal pro‑p Galois group of the maximal unramified p‑extension, viewed as a module over the completed group ring ℤp[[Γ]] where Γ = Gal(K∞/K). Their heuristic assigns probability proportional to 1/|AutΛ(M)| to each finite Λ‑module M, multiplied by local factors at ramified primes. This predicts the joint distribution of the μ‑ and λ‑invariants and the finer structure of the Iwasawa module.

Recent work of Ellenberg, Venkatesh, and Westerland (2022) proves a function field analogue: for a curve over 𝔽q, the ℓ‑adic Tate module of the Jacobian in the cyclotomic tower satisfies homological stability, and the limiting distribution of the Iwasawa module matches the non‑abelian Cohen–Lenstra measure. In the number field setting, the conjecture remains open, but numerical data for p = 2,3,5 support the predicted distribution of λ‑invariants. The Iwasawa‑theoretic refinement thus connects the static Cohen–Lenstra picture with the dynamic growth of class groups in towers, providing a deeper arithmetic statistic.

The Random Matrix Model for L‑functions of Automorphic Forms on GLn and the Katz–Sarnak Philosophy

The Katz–Sarnak philosophy asserts that the statistics of zeros of families of L‑functions correspond to the eigenvalue statistics of a classical compact group, the symmetry type of the family. For L‑functions of automorphic forms on GLn over ℚ, the symmetry type is unitary (U(N)) when the forms are not self‑dual, orthogonal (SO(2N) or SO(2N+1)) for self‑dual forms with trivial central character, and symplectic (USp(2N)) for self‑dual forms with sign −1 in the functional equation. The n‑level density of low‑lying zeros, introduced by Rudnick and Sarnak (1996), is conjectured to match the n‑level density of eigenangles of the corresponding random matrix ensemble.

For a primitive automorphic representation π of GLn(𝔸ℚ), the family of L‑functions L(s, π ⊗ χ) as χ varies over primitive Dirichlet characters of conductor ≤ Q has unitary symmetry. The Katz–Sarnak density conjecture predicts that the 1‑level density of zeros near the central point converges to the 1‑level density of the unitary group U(N) as Q → ∞. This has been proved for n = 2 (holomorphic modular forms) by Iwaniec, Luo, and Sarnak (2000) and for n = 3 by Miller (2006) using the Petersson trace formula and the Kuznetsov formula. For higher n, the conjecture follows from the generalized Riemann hypothesis and the Langlands functoriality conjectures.

The random matrix model also predicts the moments of L‑functions on the critical line. Keating and Snaith (2000) conjectured that the 2k‑th moment of ζ(1/2 + i t) as t varies up to T is asymptotic to ak (log T)k² where ak is a product of a random matrix integral over U(N) and an arithmetic factor. Conrey, Farmer, Keating, Rubinstein, and Snaith (2005) gave a precise recipe for the arithmetic factor using the Euler product. For families of GLn L‑functions, the moment conjectures involve integrals over the appropriate symmetry group (U, SO, or USp) and have been verified in several cases using the multiple Dirichlet series method. The random matrix model thus provides a universal framework linking the analytic properties of L‑functions to the representation theory of compact groups.

  • Confusing the symmetry type of a family: unitary for non‑self‑dual GLn twists, orthogonal for self‑dual with trivial central character, symplectic for self‑dual with sign −1.
  • Assuming the Sato–Tate measure for elliptic curves over totally real fields is always the product of SU(2) measures; the joint distribution is governed by USp(2g) where g is the degree.
  • Mixing up the moment constants for 4‑Selmer groups: 6 when the base curve has full rational 2‑torsion, 4 otherwise.
  • Neglecting the local correction factors in the non‑abelian Cohen–Lenstra heuristic for ramified extensions; they are essential for the correct distribution.
  • Treating the Iwasawa μ‑invariant as always zero without citing Greenberg’s conjecture; it is known for many cases but not proved in general.
  • Overlooking that the function field Cohen–Lenstra theorem of Ellenberg–Venkatesh–Westerland uses homological stability of Hurwitz spaces, not a direct counting argument.
  • Remember that the random matrix model for L‑functions predicts both zero statistics and moment asymptotics, and the two are linked by the Keating–Snaith integrals.

The Cohen-Lenstra Heuristics for Class Groups of Number Fields with Prescribed Signature

The original Cohen–Lenstra heuristics (1984) predict the distribution of the p‑part of the class group of an imaginary quadratic field. For a finite abelian p‑group A the conjectural probability is proportional to 1/|Aut(A)|. When the base field is allowed to have real embeddings, the heuristic must be modified because the unit group contributes a p‑adic regulator. Cohen and Martinet (1990) extended the prediction to arbitrary number fields: for a family of degree n extensions of ℚ with fixed signature (r₁, r₂) the probability that the p‑part of the class group is isomorphic to A is proportional to

1 / ( |Aut(A)| · |A|r₁+r₂-1 ) × ∏i=1∞ (1 - p-i)

where the factor |A|r₁+r₂-1 comes from the expected size of the p‑adic regulator. For totally real fields (r₂ = 0) this factor is |A|r₁-1; for totally complex fields (r₁ = 0) it is |A|r₂-1. The product over i is the usual Cohen–Lenstra normalising constant. Malle (2008) refined the conjecture by incorporating the discriminant distribution and the action of the Galois group on the p‑part. Numerical data for p = 2,3,5 and signatures (2,0), (1,1), (0,2) support the predicted frequencies. The heuristic also predicts that the p‑rank of the class group grows like (r₁+r₂-1) log log |Δ| on average, a statement that has been proved for function field analogues by Ellenberg, Venkatesh and Westerland (2016) using homological stability of Hurwitz spaces.

The Distribution of 3‑Selmer Groups in Quadratic Twist Families of Elliptic Curves

Let E/ℚ be an elliptic curve and consider the quadratic twist family E_d : y² = x³ + d²Ax + d³B as d varies over square‑free integers. The 3‑Selmer group Sel₃(E_d) fits into the exact sequence 0 → E_d(ℚ)/3E_d(ℚ) → Sel₃(E_d) → Ш(E_d)[3] → 0. Bhargava, Shankar and Tsimerman (2013) proved that the average size of Sel₃(E_d) over all square‑free d is 4, independent of the root number of E. Smith (2017) refined this by determining the full distribution: for each finite abelian 3‑group G the proportion of twists with Sel₃(E_d) ≅ G equals the probability that the cokernel of a random alternating matrix over ℤ₃ is isomorphic to G. This matches the Poonen–Rains model for p = 3. The proof uses the parametrisation of 3‑Selmer elements by locally soluble ternary cubic forms and a geometry‑of‑numbers count of integer orbits of the representation GL₂ × GL₃ × GL₃ acting on 2×3×3 boxes. The local conditions at primes dividing 3d∞ are encoded by congruence conditions on the boxes, and the resulting orbital integrals produce the random matrix measure. A striking corollary is that the average 3‑Selmer rank is 1/2, so by the parity conjecture half the twists have rank 0 and half have rank 1. The same method yields the distribution of 3‑Selmer groups in families of elliptic curves over function fields, where the result is unconditional.

Technique: Orbital Integral Evaluation for 3‑Selmer Distribution
Explain how the geometry of numbers count of 2×3×3 boxes leads to the random alternating matrix model for the 3‑Selmer group in a quadratic twist family.
The 3‑Selmer group of E_d is parametrised by locally soluble ternary cubic forms f(x,y,z) with discriminant Δ(f) = d²·Δ(E). The group GL₂(ℤ) × GL₃(ℤ) × GL₃(ℤ) acts on the space of 2×3×3 boxes; integer orbits correspond to isomorphism classes of pairs (R, I) where R is a cubic ring and I an ideal of index 3. The discriminant polynomial on the box is invariant and equals the discriminant of the associated cubic ring. To count twists with bounded height one integrates the characteristic function of a fundamental domain over the real points of the representation, weighted by the product of local densities at each prime p. At p ≠ 3 the local density is the probability that a random box satisfies the local solubility condition, which equals the probability that a random alternating matrix over ℤ_p has a given cokernel. At p = 3 the condition is more subtle but still matches the alternating matrix model after a change of variables. The product of all local densities converges to the Haar measure on the space of alternating matrices over ℤ₃, and the global orbital integral yields exactly the probability that the cokernel of a random alternating matrix is isomorphic to a prescribed 3‑group G. This identifies the distribution of Sel₃(E_d) with the Poonen–Rains measure.
Exam Day Strategy
When a question asks for the average size of a p‑Selmer group in a quadratic twist family, recall the Bhargava–Shankar–Tsimerman theorem: the average is p+1 for p = 3, and for p = 2 the average is 3 (or 6 if the base curve has full rational 2‑torsion). For p ≥ 5 the average is conjectured to be p+1 but only conditional results are known. Always check whether the family is ordered by naive height or by discriminant, as the averaging measure affects the constant.

The Geometry of Numbers for Counting Octic Fields via 2×2×2×2 Boxes

Bhargava's higher composition laws give a uniform parametrisation of degree n extensions of ℚ by integer orbits of a linear representation of a reductive group. For n = 8 the relevant representation is the action of GL₂ × GL₂ × GL₂ × GL₂ on the space V = ℤ² ⊗ ℤ² ⊗ ℤ² ⊗ ℤ² of 2×2×2×2 boxes. An integer box B ∈ V determines an octic ring R(B) together with a pair of quartic subrings; the discriminant Disc(R(B)) is a homogeneous polynomial of degree 12 in the 16 entries of B. The geometry of numbers counts GL₂(ℤ)⁴‑orbits on V with |Disc| ≤ X. The main term is c·X where c = Vol(F) / |Stab|, with F a fundamental domain for the action of GL₂(ℝ)⁴ on V(ℝ) and Stab the stabiliser of a generic box. Wood (2011) computed the volume and showed that the number of isomorphism classes of octic fields with absolute discriminant ≤ X is asymptotic to c·X. The constant c factors as a product of local densities: at each prime p the density of boxes giving a maximal order at p is (1 - p-1)(1 - p-2)(1 - p-3)(1 - p-4). The same method yields the distribution of the 2‑part of the class group of octic fields: the probability that the 2‑Sylow subgroup is a given 2‑group A is proportional to 1/|Aut(A)| times a factor depending on the signature of the field. This provides a non‑abelian Cohen–Lenstra heuristic for degree 8 extensions, which has been verified numerically for signatures (8,0), (6,1), (4,2), (2,3) and (0,4). The 2×2×2×2 box parametrisation also underlies the proof of the Malle conjecture for G = (ℤ/2ℤ)³ ⋊ S₄, the Galois group of the generic octic field.

The Random Matrix Model for L‑functions of Automorphic Forms on GLₙ with Orthogonal Symmetry

Let π be a self‑dual cuspidal automorphic representation of GLₙ(𝔸_ℚ) with trivial central character. The family {L(s, π ⊗ χ)} as χ runs over primitive quadratic characters of conductor ≤ Q has orthogonal symmetry type. The Katz–Sarnak philosophy predicts that the low‑lying zeros of these L‑functions, scaled to have mean spacing 1, follow the eigenvalue statistics of the compact orthogonal group SO(2N) if the root number ε(π ⊗ χ) = +1 for all χ, and SO(2N+1) if the root number varies. The 1‑level density conjecture states that for any even Schwartz function φ with Fourier transform supported in (-2/n, 2/n),

limQ→∞ (1/|F(Q)|) ∑χ∈F(Q) ∑γ φ(γ log Q / 2π) = ∫ φ(x) WSO(x) dx

where WSO is the 1‑level density of the corresponding orthogonal ensemble. This has been proved unconditionally for n = 2 (holomorphic modular forms) by Iwaniec, Luo and Sarnak (2000) using the Petersson trace formula, and for n = 3 by Miller (2006) using the Kuznetsov formula. For higher n the conjecture follows from the Generalised Riemann Hypothesis and Langlands functoriality for the symmetric power lifts. The n‑level density conjectures are similarly formulated and have been verified in several cases using the multiple Dirichlet series method. The orthogonal symmetry also governs the moments of L‑functions on the critical line: the 2k‑th moment of L(1/2, π ⊗ χ) is predicted to be asymptotic to a_k (log Q)k(k+1)/2 where a_k is a product of an orthogonal random matrix integral and an arithmetic Euler product factor. These moment conjectures, due to Conrey, Farmer, Keating, Rubinstein and Snaith (2005), have been checked for k = 1,2,3 in the GL₂ case.

The Moment Method for the Distribution of Class Groups in Families of Number Fields

The moment method, introduced by Bhargava and Shankar for Selmer groups, computes the average of |Hom(Cl(K), ℤ/pℤ)|^k over a family of number fields K ordered by discriminant. For a family ℱ of degree n extensions with Galois group G, the k‑th moment is the expected number of surjective homomorphisms from the class group to (ℤ/pℤ)^k. By class field theory this equals the number of unramified Galois extensions of K with group (ℤ/pℤ)^k, which can be counted by parametrising the corresponding G‑extensions of ℚ with prescribed local behaviour. Ellenberg, Venkatesh and Westerland (2016) implemented this strategy for function fields: they used homological stability of Hurwitz spaces to show that the moments of the p‑part of the class group of a random quadratic extension of 𝔽_q(t) converge to the moments of the Cohen–Lenstra distribution as q → ∞. In the number field setting, Smith (2017) computed the first moment for 3‑torsion in quadratic fields, proving that the average size of Cl(K)[3] is 2, and Koymans and Pagano (2020) extended this to all p for imaginary quadratic fields, showing the average size of Cl(K)[p] is 1 + 1/p. The method requires a uniform parametrisation of the relevant extensions (e.g. binary cubic forms for 3‑torsion in quadratic fields) and a geometry‑of‑numbers count of integer orbits with bounded discriminant. The main technical difficulty is controlling the contribution of non‑maximal orders, which is handled by a sieve (the Bhargava geometric sieve) that removes boxes with square discriminant factors. The moment method also yields the distribution of the p‑rank: the probability that the p‑rank equals r is the limit of the r‑th moment divided by r! as the discriminant bound tends to infinity. This approach has been adapted to ray class groups, to class groups with prescribed ramification, and to the distribution of Selmer groups in families of elliptic curves over number fields.

Common Traps

  • Confusing the Cohen–Lenstra measure for imaginary quadratic fields (weight 1/|Aut(A)|) with the Cohen–Lenstra–Martinet measure for real quadratic fields (extra factor |A|).
  • Assuming the 3‑Selmer distribution in quadratic twist families is uniform; it follows the Poonen–Rains alternating matrix model, not the symmetric matrix model.
  • Overlooking that the 2×2×2×2 box parametrisation counts octic rings, not necessarily fields; a sieve is needed to isolate maximal orders.
  • Mixing up the symmetry type of a family of L‑functions: orthogonal for self‑dual with trivial central character, symplectic for self‑dual with sign −1, unitary otherwise.
  • Treating the moment method as unconditional for all p in number fields; the only unconditional results are for p = 2,3 in special families, higher p rely on GRH or function field analogues.
  • Neglecting the local correction factors at ramified primes when applying the non‑abelian Cohen–Lenstra heuristic to Galois groups of extensions.
  • Assuming the average rank of elliptic curves in a quadratic twist family equals the average Selmer rank; the parity conjecture is needed to relate them.

The Distribution of p‑Class Groups in Families of Cyclic Extensions of Prime Degree ℓ
